THE CIRCLE OF DEATH By CHARLES J. DUTTON 

Author of "Murder in a Library," "Poison Unknown," etc.

N' "IGHT after night came the telephone message to Timothy Rogan, Chief of 

Police. "Ten days now... Nine days now. . . ." Worried and mystified, Rogan consulted his friend, Harley Manners, the famous professor of Abnormal Psychology, only to be told that it was probably a joke. "Three days now. "Two days now...." On the last day the town's most prominent citizen died. The doctor insisted that the death was natural. But shortly afterward the man's son received a telephone call -saying, "You will die in five days." 

And thus commences "The Circle of 

Death." That evening after the story of the telephone calls had been told in Rogan's office, comes murder, swift, horrible, and weird. Then in four days another whose method and motive completely baffle both physicians and police. 

Charles J. Dutton scores again! Here is a brand new motive which, he says, has never before been used in mystery stories. And, as in all his books, here is swift reasoning, rapid fire action, and excitement aplenty.

THE CIRCLE OF DEATH

CROSS BOOK SHOP 

Bay State Bldg., 19 Lawrence St. 

Lawrence, Mass.

CIRCULATING LIBRARY CHAPTER I 

Dusk, like a creeping ghost, had invaded Harley Manners' lawn. The ivy-covered wall beyond the flower gardens had become an etched line of blackness. Deep pools of gloom lay under the elm trees-moving shadows which advanced and retreated in the slight breeze. With the darkness had come a deep silence and a cool wind had crept up from the lake. 

Stretched far back in a wide garden chair, under the largest tree on the lawn, Chief of Police Timothy Rogan stared gravely down in the direction of the city. Far below him he could see the long outline of State Street, its winding way a streak of brilliant light, ending with the lighted tower on the public dock. There were lights everywhere-in tall office buildings, in homes, on every street -and the creeping headlights of moving cars.

After a while he turned away from the city to glance down the dark fields toward the lake. It was far too dark to distinguish the water, but the round yellow circle of flame from the lighthouse on the point shone like a tiny moon.

Far over the water his eyes picked out a slowly moving starlike speck-a boat at least fifteen miles from the shore.

As a rule Harley Manners' quiet lawn, with its tall trees and colorful gardens, filled Rogan's heart with an emotion as near to peace as he ever knew. The house was on top of a steep hill, four miles from the police station. 1

Not only did the lawn command the finest view in the city but, because it was on a private road, it remained always a place of quietness and peace. Though he would never have admitted it, more than once when driven to frenzy by the problems of his office, Rogan had motored up the long hill simply to spend a few hours in the seclusion of these grounds.

He should then have felt at rest, contented; instead, though he knew his friend sat only a few feet away, he was uneasy and depressed. With a gloomy sigh he shifted the long stogie to the other corner of his lips as he ran a big hand through his white hair. It was an habitual gesture. Manners, who caught the motion of Rogan's arm, knew what it meant-thought also he knew what had caused the chief's depression. Rogan had placed his chair so that he could look down over the fields and watch the glimmering lights below. 

For twenty years he had ruled the police force of the city. Now in a few weeks he must retire on his pensionlay aside his gold badge, symbol that his work was over. 

No doubt, thought Manners, that was why Rogan had gazed so intently at the city lights, why once he had sighed. 

Again, there might be some hidden reason. Rogan had been his dinner guest, but he had practically invited himself to the meal.

There had been the time when the friendship between the rough, uncultured police chief and the highly intellectual professor of abnormal psychology had been a heated topic of conversation over the dinner tables of the social leaders of the city. In the end the topic was dismissed, with the conclusion that it was simply one of

Harley Manners' unexplainable whims. Manners had always been the subject of conversation. Why one of his wealth and social position (a position he seemed to care nothing for) should teach, had long disturbed the city. 

And above all why should he teach abnormal psychology?

This last puzzled his friends more than anything elseunless it was his friendship for Rogan. 

Manners had known Rogan from his boyhood days.

Years later, when the patrolman became head of the city police force and Manners one of the country's most distinguished experts in his particular field, the friendship increased. More than one murder case had been solved through the advice which he had given the chief. Rogan knew nothing of the subject the professor taught. The thousands of books in Manners' library awed him. But, he did know two things; that without Manners' help he would not have solved the three crimes which were his greatest mysteries; and that, though again he would never have admitted it to a soul, he loved him as if he were his own son.

From the chair, close beside the trunk of the largest elm on his lawn, Manners glanced at the glowing red tip of Rogan's stogie. In a few weeks the chief would retire. 

Rough he was, superstitious, uncultured, emotional, easily disturbed; but it would be a long time before the city found a public official of Rogan's quality. Occasionally a rabid reform group would start a campaign to get rid of him, but their efforts never had much success. Trained in the long hard years of detailed police work, Rogan not only knew his job, but had won the respect of the entire city by his honesty and plain common sense.

"I know how he feels," thought Manners. "Down below he can see the lights of the city; for twenty years the protection of two hundred thousand persons has been in his hands. In five weeks now he goes out of office. It's a silly rule which makes a man step out simply because he is sixty-five. Rogan was never doing better work than now. There is only one interest in his life—his work. He never married; his job prevented his having many friends. 

No wonder he feels depressed. And yet, it's my idea there is something else, besides his retirement, that is bothering him. The old boy is worried over something." 

As if knowing what his friend's thoughts were Rogan spoke. His rough voice broke in on Manners' thoughts out of the darkness. "Harley, do you know I have been wondering about something. I wonder if I am going to be able to get out of office without having something big break."

Manners made no reply; he knew his friend; knew that no reply was needed. There was a deep superstitious streak in Rogan's make-up. But Manners knew also that there was a reason back of his friend's remark. All he had to do was to keep quiet and the reason would come to light. In a second it did. "You know, Harley," came the half apologetic voice, "since the 'Poison'* case there's been nothing very big happen at the station. Yet I've always heard that the average police chief ends his services by walking into a big murder mystery-which he is forced to hand over to his successor. 

"A silly superstition," came the laughing retort. * Poison Unknown. Dodd Mead & Co. 1932.

"Maybe," was the grudging reply, "but you use that word too often, Harley. I only know that ever since I started out on the force I have heard that just before a chief retires he runs into a big murder case." The voice died away, as if Rogan were thinking-then came the comment: "One he never solves." "Well," Manners reminded him, "for months nothing has happened of any importance. There is more crime today than ever before. By the law of averages you might run into something during the next five weeks, but the chances are that it will not be of much importance." 

Out of the darkness came a snort of dissent: "If you take out all the crime caused by prohibition you will discover there is far less crime today than fifteen years ago. That silly law turned every police department upside down. The duty of the police is to protect the lives and property of the citizens, not to spend their time trying to arrest a man for having a pint of liquor. That silly law-" 

Manners interrupted him. "If he ever gets started on prohibition," was his thought, "I will never discover just why he rang me up." "But I know all that, Rogan," he said aloud. "Sure you do," came the growl from the chair. "And it's all true. What's more, it's my idea the other thing is true that before I hand in my badge I will run into a big case; a bad one," he concluded morosely.

The curiously uneasy tone in the rough voice caused 

Manners to sit upright, as he threw a glance toward the near-by chair. He could barely distinguish the dark, indistinct shadow of his friend, but the tip of the stogie was

a glowing point of flame in the darkness. For a moment he tried to decide what to say, then sinking back into his chair, he laughed. "You seem a little uneasy tonight, Rogan. The fact that you are going to retire in five weeks is the cause of your remarks." "Is it?" came the indignant denial. "You listen to me, Harley. I have had a very funny thing happening the last few days."

Whatever he intended to reveal did not come at once. 

Instead there fell a silence, broken by a sudden splutter of a match as a fresh smoke was lighted. For several moments the stogie could be seen, its red tip glowing with every puff. Then, in a sudden gesture, it was thrust away from his lips. Though unable to see, Manners sensed that as Rogan spoke he was leaning forward in his chair. "You know, Harley," began the slow, hesitating voice, "one in my position gets a lot of crank letters. People hate their neighbors and complain about them, hoping to get them into trouble, or else they write absurd charges about prominent men and women. We never do anything about such letters, but during the last few days I have run into something new.” The voice hesitated, then died away. "What was it?" came the question. "Telephone calls. Every night at the same hour for the last ten days. Tried to trace them but could not."

He was not volunteering much information, but Manners knew how Rogan's mind worked. Leave him alone, ask no questions and the words in time would come. In a moment they did: "Ten days ago, just about five-thirty, the phone on my

desk rang. When I replied a voice came over the wire. 

What do you think it said, Harley?" Rogan answered his own question. ""Ten days from now, something big's going to happen, Chief.' That's what I heard."

The rough voice coming out of the darkness was grave and anxious. There seemed little in what Rogan had said to cause him to be troubled. Still, the chief had his moments when the entire world looked gloomy. It was always unexpected things, things out of the usual police routine which caused him to grow depressed. But there seemed nothing to warrant excitement in the words he had just heard. Some crank had been on the wire, that was all. 

Manners said as much, only to hear an indignant denial from the near-by chair. "Maybe you're right, but how do you explain the call coming every night? The next night at the same hour it came again. This time I was told, 'Nine days now.' Then the next night, 'Eight days now.' And so on every single night." "Was there a call tonight?" "Yes. Waited to see if there would be one. Didn't start for your house until it came. It came at the usual timeabout five-thirty. All the voice said was, 'It's the tenth day, Rogan.'"

Silence fell. In it Manners heard the chief nervously shift his weight in the chair. Saw the splutter of a match, had a momentary glimpse of Rogan's red face, as the stogie was relighted. A question was clearly demanded, and it was asked. "Have you tried to trace the calls?"

"Of course," was the impatient reply. "They all come from pay stations. Stations in various office buildings. So far as I could discover the man had never used the same telephone twice. It may not mean anything at all; then again—" And the voice trailed into uneasy silence.

Professor of psychology though he was, Manners had respect for the troubled police chief who sat but a few feet away. There had been other occasions when Rogan had become uneasy and afraid. A hunch, the chief called these feelings; whisperings from the subconscious would have been Manners' definition. Some secret spring of 

Rogan's mind was hinting and warning that, in the past, under certain forgotten circumstances, invariable results had taken place. It was time he cheered him up. "Why, Rogan," he laughed, "it was simply someone having a little fun with you. Or else a crank-some neurotic -was on that wire. There can be nothing in it." "Maybe not," was the heated reply. "Maybe not. But,

Harley, I have had rather a long experience with what you call cranks. God knows all sorts of them have been after me for years. But whoever the man was who called me on the phone, one thing is certain, his voice was that of a man very sure of what he is saying. A determined, positive voice. And what got my goat was the fact that every day the time he mentioned grew less. First it was ten days then nine, until tonight he said: "This is the tenth day.' I don't like it, and I don't know what it is all about." 

Manners' glance drifted over to his long rambling house.

It was an uneven line in the darkness, broken only by the lights in the window of his library. For a moment he studied them, then turned to glance down toward the city.

He realized that in a sense there was no reply he could make to what had just been said. Far below, in the silence, he could trace the city lights as they followed the curving shore line, in tiny dots of brightness on the streets; and in every case they represented comedy or tragedy, happiness or despair. As he thought this his glance rested upon the tower of the Alpha Insurance building.

It rose far above the other buildings of the city. By day it was a white glistening shaft that could be seen for miles out on the lake. Now, in the darkness, a series of searchlights played over it, with the colors changing every two minutes; now a soft blue, now flickering and warming into yellow, to be followed by red. These lights, which played at night over the tower of the building, had always annoyed Manners. He scowled now as his eyes followed the changing colors. Then he turned. "You have no idea at what the person who called you might have been hinting?" "None. In fact it has been very quiet for the last three months. That's another reason I feel jumpy. They say it is a bad sign to have it quiet just before you retire." 

Manners smiled in the darkness. "They." That would mean any of the countless proverbs and superstitions which had grown up in police circles and which the chief had heard retold year after year. Rogan was superstitious; hard-boiled and efficient he might be, but deep down in his heart were a few things which he feared. As a psychologist, Manners should have laughed at his friend's fancies, but he realized what caused them. Also, there were several occasions in the past when those same "hunches" had led to valuable results.

"Well," began Rogan, "about all that I know of any importance is that Kimball is on the point of death. The old man, I mean. But that has nothing to do with what that voice said."

Instinctively both men turned to look down at the tall tower of the Alpha Insurance building. Its slender towering shaft was bathed in a glow of red light, as they watched the color slowly faded into a soft blue. Both knew of what the other was thinking.

If the city had a ruling family it was the Kimballs. The life insurance company whose lighted tower they were gazing upon was Kimball's own creation; he controlled almost all of the stock. His property was scattered in every section of the city-property which he was perfectly willing to lease but which he would never sell. Ruthless and hard in all business dealings, though his was the wealthiest family in the city, yet it was also the most disliked. Owner of the leading paper, which he ran for his own purposes, for years he had run a campaign to have Rogan retired as police chief. His failure in this had not made his feelings toward the chief any kindlier. "I wonder," came Rogan's thoughtful voice, "how Kimball will face death. It will be the one thing he will not be able to override. All his life he has had his own way. 

But death is one thing he can't bluff. He won't like it." 

The remark startled Manners. To have Rogan suddenly appear in the rôle of philosopher was something new. He started a comment, but the words were checked on his lips. "You know, Harley," began once more the reflective voice, "in all my years on the force I have heard a lot

about crimes-about criminals. But it's always been my belief a man like Kimball is a greater menace to a city than all the so-called crooks. When we cleaned up the vice district we found Kimball owned ninety percent of the property used by the resorts. He fought the clean-up for years. Of the thousand speakeasies in the city over two thirds are in his buildings. Everything that stood in the way of his making money he fought, always; and he has great influence. Now the criminal is a-" He paused, searching for a word. "What's that word you use, Harley?" "Moron." "Yes, that's what you call him. In all my experience with gunmen, highwaymen, murderers, I never have met a really intelligent criminal. Don't think there are any. 

Most of them today are just kids.” 

Manners agreed with him, adding that intelligent criminals were almost unknown. More to change the trend of 

Rogan's thoughts than anything else, he went through a list of the more notorious murderers. Palmer, Crippen, Lamson, Smith, Landru, Haarmann, Tropman were mentioned. He dwelt at some length upon the stupidity of their crimes. Becoming more personal, he discussed the cases solved in Rogan's own office and dismissed them with the declaration that the criminal had not been an intelligent man in any case. Intelligent people do not commit crimes, the chief was told, mostly because they are intelligent.

Rogan listened to the long monologue but said nothing. When Manners paused to light a cigarette there came the first reaction. "That's just what I was saying. But I insist that a man

like Kimball, despite all his millions, his social position, does more harm to a city than its criminals. Yet he must be intelligent. Look at the money he's made." "Anti-social," was the dry comment.

The phrase meant nothing to Rogan and he roughly demanded an explanation. He was told that the words applied to one who had no interest in society as a whole, but sought only to gratify his own desires. With a grunt 

Rogan admitted the phrase would fit Kimball, since the man's only desire in life was to make money. Manners' retort-that the ability to make money did not, however, prove that a man was intelligent-led to a debate. They were engaged in this when a voice came from the house. 

Turning, they saw that a light had been switched on over the veranda. Standing by the open door of the house was a middle-aged woman, whose voice came floating to them over the lawn. Rogan was wanted on the telephone. 

With that remark Manners' housekeeper turned and passed through the colonial door to reënter the house. 

With a half grunt the chief rose slowly to his feet and a second later Manners followed his example.

The two men crossed the lawn and climbed the steps of the veranda. At the door of the house Manners' large Airedale, Satan, rose in a languid fashion to greet his master. 

He made, however, no effort to follow them into the hall. 

The telephone was on a stand near the stairway leading to the second floor. As Rogan's hand reached out for the telephone he turned for a second. The blue uniform was the best he had, but the coat had been unbuttoned, his white shirt was showing, the round, kindly face was troubled, his snow-white hair stood upright. For a moment he

scowled, then sank down on the stool and turned to the receiver. 

As the first rough growl came from Rogan's lips, Manners stepped from the hall into his library. This long room, every side lined with glass-covered bookcases, was the spot he loved best on earth. Behind those glass doors were thousands of books. Years had been spent in the making of that library-a collection of folklore, history, psychology, philosophy and the largest library in the country on the history of crime. Almost every curious and out-of-theway book could have been found somewhere on the long line of shelves. 

For a moment he stood looking over the room. Behind him he could hear the low rumble of Rogan's voice, but he paid no attention to what the chief might be saying.

His eyes were searching the room. They rested upon the 

Rops, over a hundred etchings of the Belgian master; observed the gay-colored eighteenth-century French prints, the gallant sarcasm of Fragonard, Baudouin, Lawrence, Dubucourt and others of the period. Every gay print on these walls recalled to his mind memories of obscure little bookstores in Paris, of rainy nights along the Seine. He lingered over the long line of ivory elephants marching majestically across his big fireplace-then a sound caused him to turn.

Rogan had entered the room. The stocky figure had paused just inside the door; the round red face bore a puzzled expression. As he looked at him, Manners saw the big hand reach up to the snow-white hair, watched as it was tossed in wild disorder. For a moment the chief did not speak. Instead his eyes traveled slowly around the

library.

This room had always intrigued him. In it were more books than he had ever seen, unless it might be in the public library. That public institution, across from his police station, he had only entered once, driven there by a murder; but Manners' library always awed him. Behind these glass doors were thousands of books, shelf upon shelf upon crime. Once in a while he had taken a book from the shelf and slowly read what a forgotten age had set down about crime. But Manners had read them all, this was the fact that always filled his heart with amazement. 

But then, Manners himself amazed him-his knowledge of the odd and curious things of life. 

He spoke at last, his voice slow and seemingly weary. "That call was from Kimball's son, the older son,

Edward. He wants to see me at once, at the station. His father just died. You will have to run me down to the city." 

He paused, a puzzled, half-angry expression flashing over the red face. With a sudden determined gesture his arms shot out. His voice was rough and uncertain but stern. "That family has fought me for years. Now they want to see me-need my help. Something's up. I tell you I am in for trouble. Just wait and see. Trouble. Just as I told you out on the lawn."

CHAPTER II 

THERE had never been any particular liking in Rogan's heart for the long hill which dropped away from Manners' grounds toward the city limits. Over two miles long, the road, with its sharp sudden curves, its high rocky banks, and, in many places deep sheer ditches, was a thing he feared. More than once as Manners had sent his car sweeping around the curves at fifty miles an hour, the chief had resolved that the next time he would walk down the long descent. 

But this night, slumped far down on the leather seat of the blue roadster, he did not even glance at the speedometer as the car swept downward to swing around the first curve. They were going fast he knew-the wind slapping in his face told him this-but, though he shoved his hat down tighter on his head, he did not turn his eyes to look at the lights of the city. He was thinking, and his thoughts were a weird tangle of emotions. 

Kimball had just died, and his elder son wanted to see him at once. He had sensed a certain excitement in the tone requesting that he come at once to the station; and in it had been ever the slightest suggestion of demand. 

Well, that was natural. For years the Kimballs had ruled the city. A sarcastic smile crossed Rogan's lips at the thought. They had even tried to rule his departmentbut they had not succeeded. Now Kimball was dead and his elder son wished to see him, the chief of police. Why? 15

He would have given a good deal to have been able to answer that question. True, in a little while he would know the answer, but he was never one who liked being kept in suspense. One thing was certain, they needed his aid. For years Kimball's paper had fought the police; now they were turning to them. Wanted help. Something had gone wrong. And it was so serious that the aid of the police department was demanded.

Something serious. What it might be he did not even try to guess. True, for a moment his teeth clenched tight on the stogie between his lips. Kimball had just died—his elder son wished to see him at once. There was, here, a connection. Once the prospect of a new problem requiring solution would have thrilled him; but there had been in the past twenty years so many problems, and even when they were solved it never seemed to make any difference. 

There was always another one ahead. He sighed as he decided that he was old.

Though the car was now swinging around a double curve, Manners heard, faintly, the sigh which escaped the chief's lips. His eyes were glued on the road, a white, twisting ribbon, made as bright as day by the onrushing headlights. True, he was driving fast; but he had made the trip hundreds of times, knew every inch of the road. 

Though he kept his eyes ahead his thoughts were on the man slumped down in the seat beside him. 

Rogan had invited himself to dinner that evening. All through the meal the chief had been silent and depressed. 

How often in the past he had observed that same emotional reaction. Once there had been a time when Manners tried to laugh away his friend's gloom. Then had come a

realization of what lay behind his friend's mental attitude. Somewhere, something had gone wrong; some forgotten fact was trying to pierce through his subconscious to warn him that in the past, similar sets of circumstances had led to disaster. The wisest thing had always been to do nothing, simply wait and see what might occur.

Far more than the chief, did Manners realize how astonishing had been Edward Kimball's telephone call. The family had fought Rogan for years, their paper leading campaign after campaign against the police department. 

They had always been a family that never allowed outsiders to have a hint as to what was going on within their sacred circle. To request an interview with the police hinted at once of some difficulty greater than their army of lawyers and executives could overcome. What could it be?

The car meanwhile had swung around the last curve and was cresting a slight rise in the road. For a second there was felt its upward rush; then it swept over the crest of the hill to dash down the long straight descent which led to the city line. Before them were lights, thousands of lights, from houses, cars, and, in the distance, the office buildings of the city. The last, however, Manners never saw, for a sudden suspicion had struck him.

Kimball had just died. "He must have been around seventy," was the thought. "Came to the city about the same time as did my father. What a difference between the two men." For a second he thought of the impulsive, joyloving gentleman whose name he bore-sighed as he wished he were still alive. Then his thought returned to 

Kimball. Only five days ago he had seen him on the streets.

The tall, arrogant figure showed no signs of illness then.

Now he was dead. 

He almost voiced his thought to Rogan, but stopped.

If he was right in what he suspected, there would be plenty of worry ahead for the chief. Kimball had just died; his son immediately after the death of his father had called the chief of police. There was a connection between these two events. Some sudden catastrophe had come upon the Kimball family and it was one beyond their power to control. Deep down in his mind he thought he knew what it might be, but he would not voice his suspicion to Rogan.

The car was bumping over the first rough, uneven pavement of the city suburb. The houses lining the streets were small and cheap. It was a warm night and everybody seemed to be out on the veranda. He made no effort to cut down the speed of his car, and in a few moments was rushing past the homes of the wealthier citizens. For a few blocks he followed tree-shaded boulevards; then they in turn vanished as he swept past the University. Long granite buildings enclosed three sides of the tree-covered campus, and the walks were filled with sauntering couples. 

The business district was reached at the very moment when the theaters were pouring out their patrons from the first of the evening's performances. The streets were filled with people. Then, as the car approached the lake, the sidewalks emptied. It was a long street they sped over, one that started at the public dock, to end far out in the country. A small park was skirted, its many benches filled; then the street dropped downward in a row of glittering lights, ending in an illuminated tower far below them. At the very top of this descent, Manners stopped the car.

The long square building before which they alighted was new, its granite walls still unsoiled by the dust and dirt of the city. Through all the twenty years Rogan had been the head of the Police Department, it had been suggested that a new headquarters was badly needed, yet only within the last six months had the building been completed. 

It contained all the equipment a modern police station should possess, but Rogan had never liked the place. The first case that had come to him when he entered his new office had been a murder-and, as he slowly climbed out of the car and scowled up at the wide steps of his headquarters, he was wondering what might happen before his name was taken off the door of his office.

With the chief's heavy figure in the lead they climbed the six steps which led to the first floor and entered a long hallway. There were many doors along this hall, open doors, out of which floated both tobacco smoke and the low rumble of voices. Just inside the hall Rogan paused to cast one quick glance before him. The scene was familiar, he had witnessed it thousands of times. It gave him always a sense of power to see the opened doors in that long corridor. 

They represented the protection of the life and property of the two hundred thousand people of the city.

The stern look upon his face softened a bit as he stood looking silently ahead. Little did the city know what was in those rooms. Men were sitting before telephones; little lights were switching from red to blue; in the radio room instructions were going out to the cars which nightly patrolled the streets. In another room men were examining finger-prints; in still another they were talking to a far-off city. And behind unopened doors was everything science

had invented for the detection of crime; every needed bit of apparatus that science offered stood in the police laboratories; machines for taking pictures, for testing metals, for beating rugs, analyzing paper, fabrics, bloodstains. All these were in the building for him to command. 

For him to command. He was thinking as he turned to the door beside him and flung it open: science was a great thing, yet after all what real good had all those machines been to him? Before any crime could be solved there were always two things to discover: why it had been committed, and where. Science was a fine thing but long experience taught him that police work was mostly a matter of details -long, hard, dreary searching, the running down of obscure facts. The machines had a place, he would admit it, but after all they were very much overrated. Thinking this, he switched on the light in his office.

He had never liked this room. No doubt its newness, the fine furniture, the fact that for twenty years he had been in a little dingy room below the level of the street in the old station, had much to do with his feelings. Never, somehow, had he been able to make himself feel at home here. 

In the old office he could put his feet on his cluttered desk. 

When they moved into the new building they gave him the largest room, and, also, as a mark of appreciation, a great mahogany desk, the cost of which stunned him when he heard it. The other office had been "home." This was simply the Headquarters of the Chief of Police. 

For a second after turning on the light Rogan glanced gravely around the room, then crossed the floor to the desk. Save for four chairs it was the only furniture in the room. There was a large and gaudy calendar on the wall,

and a clock, but nothing else. The desk was a mass of papers, reports, blueprints, letters-no effort had been made toward neatness. Beside a box of stogies stood a telephone; and a series of push buttons, connecting with various departments of the building, was in a black box close to the edge of the desk. 

As Rogan threw his hat down, then sank into his chair, Manners walked across to the windows. For a moment he stared thoughtfully out into the night. Before him stretched State Street, its towering buildings tall shafts of darkness, broken here and there by a few lighted windows. Shifting his gaze a little he could look in the direction of the lake. Because of the darkness it could not be seen, but moving lights showed boats upon its surface. 

Far in the distance there was a flash of light, a flame which twinkled for a moment, then died away, only to reappear; it was the lighthouse far down the lake. For a time he stood silently looking out into the darkness, then turned. "I think I had better be going, Tim,” he said. "Going!" came the growl from the desk, as Rogan turned to throw an astonished look toward the window, "You are not going to leave me here alone to interview 

Kimball. You know that family has no use for me. I want someone who runs around in their society to be with me when he comes in. You can't go." 

Rogan's tone, rough and urgent and demanding, caused 

Manners to smile. His remark that he had better go had been half-hearted. There had been in the past, occasions when the chief had asked for his assistance. It had been gladly given, had led to results. Deep down in his heart

Manners knew that he was keenly interested in hearing what would be said in this room. True, Rogan had made one mistake; he himself knew the Kimballs, but only slightly. There was little cultural life in the city; few there were with whom Manners cared to spend much time and the Kimballs were not numbered among the few. 

With a nod of agreement he crossed the floor and dropped into a chair on the opposite side of the desk. 

They did not speak. The chief's hand reached for the box of stogies. Slowly a brown roll was removed from the box, studied for a moment, then its tip cut off with a knife. 

When it was lighted, three long puffs of smoke were blown into the room; then it was taken from between his lips and slowly examined. The look the cheap cigar received was a distrustful one, perplexed and anxious. But Manners, who was watching, knew that it only reflected what was in the chief's mind. "Harley," began Rogan's voice, only to be stopped by a sound at the door leading to the hall.

Both turned. A hand was fumbling at the knob and in an instant the door was pushed open. Two men were entering the room. They gave one quick glance toward the desk, then after closing the door started across the floor. 

They had not the appearance of men who were in need of aid or sympathy; instead both wore an air of certainty and determination. No shrinking individuals these; their very appearance showed that they were accustomed to giving commands, not obeying them. The walk of the stout little bald-headed man who was ahead seemed to 

Manners to be almost a strut.

Rogan and Manners knew both of the men who were

approaching the desk. The stout little man, dressed in a gray suit and with a red flower for a boutonnière, was Judge Henry Parrish, the general counsel for all of the 

Kimballs' activities. Behind him followed a tall individual of around forty, dressed in brown, a thin man, with a self-centered face, which bore now a slight scowl. This was Edward Kimball. Manners was acquainted with them both; and for Parrish he had neither admiration nor respect. As he saw the man crossing the floor, he was thinking that no one could ever be as important as Parrish deemed himself to be. But it surprised Manners to see him.

If Kimball had brought along the family's personal counsel it meant that there was something really wrong. Parrish had only one duty-to see that his client was able to avoid the law, to delay matters when the Kimballs overstepped the rights of the public. 

Before Rogan's desk they both stopped, staring a second across its cluttered surface. The chief made no effort to rise, grunting a remark which might have been taken for a greeting as he reached out for his box of stogies. Finding one which suited him, he spoke over his shoulder: "Better get a chair, Judge. There are two vacant ones in the room."

Without a word the men crossed to the wall and dragged the two chairs close to the desk. As Kimball sat down he took a platinum case from his pocket and lighted a cigarette. The sweet heavy smell of the Turkish tobacco began to fill the room. But it was the judge who spoke first, after a slight condescending nod to Manners. "We expected," began the heavy pompous voice, "to find you alone. The more so as Mr. Kimball telephoned

you that he wished to see you." 

Manners saw Rogan's eyes narrow; noticed that his heavy lips twitched slowly. The chief's white head was thrown back as he studied the important figure sitting upright across from him. There came his voice, slow, studied and drawling: "Well, you see, Judge, I do not happen to be alone. Of course you know Professor Manners, the only citizen of our city who has a world-wide reputation. He has, you know, aided me many times in the past." The big shoulders shrugged. "I thought I might need him now."

The judge evidently did not like the tone. His round face flushed red and a glint came into his eyes. "Whywhy-" he started to splutter. There came Edward Kimball's voice, low, conciliating, as he smiled toward Manners' chair. "It's very natural Professor Manners should be here with his friend. It may turn out to be very fortunate for us." There came a slight pause. "He may be able to throw some light upon our problem." 

Rogan made no reply. For a second silence fell over the room. In it the judge made a sudden nervous gesture with his flabby hands. If it was an indication that he wished to speak, a commanding look from Kimball prevented him. 

Manners noted the glance which passed between the two men; it was as though each was warning the other. Then, 'suddenly, leaning forward in his chair, Kimball said: "You know, Chief, my father died this evening." "If he expects me to say I am sorry," thought the chief, "he is going to be mistaken. I never liked his father, always thought he was an obstacle to the development of the

city. I ought to be polite and say something; but there is nothing I can say, so I shall keep still. I wish he would get to the point and tell me why he is here." "He died tonight," Kimball repeated slowly. "And there is one queer thing in connection with his death." 

Taking the case again from his pocket, he paused, and carefully lighted a cigarette. Manners could see he did not know how to frame what he wished to say. There was doubt and hesitancy in his voice as he spoke. "You see, Chief, it's rather difficult to say-to express what I mean. The judge insisted that I must tell youthough, to be honest, I think he is needlessly excited." "Rogan," broke in Parrish's excited voice, "after 

Edward told me-' 

"I will tell the chief myself, Judge," interrupted Kimball. Yet once more for a moment he hesitated. Then taking the cigarette from his lips and throwing back his head in a determined manner, he continued. "It's simply this, Chief. Father died tonight after an illness of four days. He had, of course, the best doctors in the city, and there was nothing out of the way in his death. Only—about four days ago an odd thing happened." "What?" was the quick interrogation. 

Kimball made an expressive gesture with his hands. "Why, of course it may be nothing at all. You know in a family like ours-one of our wealth-there are a good many persons who may not precisely like you." The voice trailed away. 

Rogan, throwing a disgusted look at the tall young man seated across from him, took the stogie from his lips. "If he keeps on in this fashion," was his impatient thought,

"we will never hear why he came to the station." It was time he brought him to the point. "What are you talking about?" he growled.

Kimball made a gesture of annoyance. "You will have to bear with me. You see, Father's death was unexpected, and, as I said, four days before it happened, something occurred." Again the voice trailed off. "What?" came the impatient demand. "He received a strange telephone call." 

Rogan gave a start. His glance went shooting over to 

Manners, only to receive a warning shake of the head from the professor. "What did it say?" he asked. 

Kimball slowly shook his head. "What was said over the wire seems incredible. My father was told that in four days he would be dead." 

A little sigh escaped from Rogan's lips. He could not forbear throwing another look at Manners. Superstitious, was he! He remembered the voice which had called him every night for the last ten days. The thought moved him to speech: "Was that the only time the call came?" 

Kimball slowly shook his head. "No. It came twice. Three nights ago Father answered the telephone, and that time the voice said, 'In three nights you will be dead.'" 

It was Manners who broke the silence. "What was the cause of your father's death?" 

Kimball sighed. "It's rather hard to say," he replied. "He was not a young man, though his general health was good. About three

days ago he complained of feeling ill. Nothing serious; only a little gastric disturbance, with loss of appetite and general feeling of discomfort. This all passed away in a short time. He was up and around until this afternoon. 

Then he suddenly grew worse. Doctor Carroll says that he died from a paralysis of the respiratory organs.” The man paused, then added, "pneumonia."

In the silence which followed Manners studied the two men. The judge was sitting upright on the edge of his chair. His round smooth face expressing approval of what had just been said. Kimball's face was long, with a sharp angular jaw which had now become tense. What he had revealed regarding the telephone call was startling enough, but something else was troubling him. Of this 

Manners was certain. 

As for Rogan, but one thing was running through his mind. Kimball had been called to the telephone and told he would be dead in four days. Never was it the chief's custom to think things out systematically. With a leap, his mind jumped to one conclusion. The telephone call he himself had received every night for ten days had been a warning. A warning of what had taken place-Kimball's death. But for the time being his calls were going to be known to no one in the room except Manners. Shifting the stogie to the other corner of his mouth he asked a question. "You say that call came twice. Each time the voice said your father would die. Was it a man or a woman who called?"

Kimball shifted his long figure in the chair. His eyes looked directly into Rogan's.

"I'd better go more into detail," he said. "It was not until three nights ago that my father mentioned having received such a telephone call. Then he told me it was the second message. It was a man, and he had the idea he had heard the voice before, though he was not certain." "Your telephone number is not in the book?" said Manners. "I suppose the telephone operator would not give it to any casual person who asked the information?" 

Kimball shook his head. "No, we are not in the book. The two calls came to the house. Of course, many persons know the house number. 

It would not be very difficult to secure." "At what hour did these calls come in?" "Around five P. M." "Around five," thought Rogan, "and there were two. I had ten-every one between five-twenty and five-thirty.

A man called Kimball. A man called me. There is some connection here. I can see only one as possible; and he does not seem alarmed over that." There came another question. "You say your father was only ill four days. Did these telephone calls bother him?" "No. You knew my father. Nothing ever bothered him.

He was disturbed, yes, but mostly because he thought someone was playing a joke on him. And as for his illness, why-" He shook his head as though puzzled. "In a sense he was ill for only three days. Even then the first day was nothing serious. He did come down to breakfast and refuse to eat something he had not done for years—but he was at the office until noon. Then he came home with the complaint that he was feeling ill. That passed, how-

ever, and he seemed to be all right. But this morning he was worse and nothing Doctor Carroll could do did any good. He seemed, this afternoon, to just collapse and the end came quickly." 

Rogan knew the doctor mentioned. His practice was among the wealthiest people of the city and his reputation was of the highest. Perhaps the chief's opinion of him was in fact somewhat higher than that of Manners. Manners knew him as a physician whose smooth manners and social graces had secured him a practice among wealthy, neurotic women and their families. But then there seemed in any case little in what Kimball had said. True, the telephone call was disturbing, but Carroll could not have been over-alarmed at the condition of his patient. What was the cause of that look of worry on the face of the young man opposite him?

Rogan's barked-out question expressed the exact thought in Manners' mind. It was put bluntly without the slightest effort to be tactful: "You did not come down to see me because you had an idea there was something suspicious about your father's death?" 

The reply was explosive. "Heavens no, Rogan. None of us has any thought of what you are hinting. It was something else that brought me down to see you."

For a moment the chief studied the long lanky figure in the chair. "He would look better," was his irrelevant thought, "if he did not wear brown. With his sallow skin it makes him look yellow. And that tie! With all the money he must have why can't he dress better? Still, he seems a

more likable man than was his father." Thinking all this, he growled out four words: "And what was that?"

Kimball threw out his hands in a gesture which might have meant anything, as he replied: "Only this, Rogan. I got a telephone call at seven tonight."

The stogie was jerked from the chief's lips, his heavy body pulled upright in the chair. For a second, under his white eyebrows, the steel-gray eyes stared across the desk. Then came his voice: "What did it say?" "I was told that in four days I would be dead."

CHAPTER III 

ROGAN's eyes had become frozen pools of light. Coldly they studied Kimball; then, as if satisfied by what they saw, passed on to Manners. The professor had slipped far down in the chair, his long legs outstretched. His face was expressionless, though the chief thought he detected a gleam in his blue-gray eyes. Parrish he did not even bother to observe; so far as he was concerned the man was not in the room. It was nevertheless the judge's high, shrill voice which broke the silence. "It's really an outrage, Rogan, that the Kimballs should have to go through this sort of thing. More so when death has just come into their family. It's an outrage—"

By a gesture the threatened flow of words was stopped. 

The look thrown Parrish was a cross between a scowl and a command. It caused the stout figure to settle nervously back in the chair, as a white handkerchief was taken from a pocket and passed over the shining face. For a second 

Rogan coldly watched him; then he turned to Kimball. "So someone called you on the phone said you would be dead in four days." The heavy voice hesitated uncertainly, then continued: "And what did you think about it?" 

The question appeared to startle the young man. "Why," he stammered, "why, I was surprised of course, and then angry." He leaned forward in the chair. "You see, Chief, someone is playing a rather cruel joke on us. I 31

should like to have it stopped." "A joke!" began Rogan, thinking of the call he had received for the last ten evenings-but he caught himself, hesitated a second, and did not say what was in his mind. "Did you recognize the voice?" 

The answer surprised both the chief and Manners. But before Kimball spoke a long calculating glance was thrown at the judge—a glance which resulted in a nod of agreement from the attorney. Then, with an impulsive motion of his hand, Kimball turned to the desk. "Yes, and no," he said. "I want to be frank, Chief. You see, we have telephones all over the house. It's possible to lift off the receiver in a good many places and call another room. So that call may have come from within the house." 

Rogan gave a disgusted grunt. The call might have come from within the house. Such an idea had never entered his head. And it would also be true that the calls received by the young man's father might have come from within his own house. If that was so-but he suddenly changed his mind. The telephone calls he had received during the last ten days had been sent from downtown office buildings. His detectives had discovered that fact, though nothing more. "What makes you think the call you got might have come from within the house?"

Kimball's face bore a puzzled expression. He slowly shook his head. "I don't know," was the confession. "In fact I do not know whether it was a man or a woman who called. The voice was disguised; yet there seemed to be something

familiar about it." "Yet you must have had a reason for thinking it might have come from within the house." "I said it might have come from within the house," was the hesitating reply. "Not that I thought it did, for I don't know. It was, however, that telephone call and another matter which caused me to ask that you see me." 

He laughed, a little shamefacedly. "You see, Chief, I realize that my father got after the police department once in a while in our paper. Naturally I felt a little hesitancy in asking a favor from you. But my wife was frightened when I told her of the message (she knew about the one Father received), and she suggested that you might be willing to have a man or so on the estate for the next few days." 

Rogan's expression did not change. "Got after the department once in a while," was his silent comment. "That's one way to express a ten-year fight to have me thrown out of office. Well, the old man's dead now." (His Irish background came to the surface as he breathed a silent wish that the Lord might have mercy on the man's soul.) "No use in bearing a grudge now; Kimball, with all his millions, is dead and I'm alive. Let him be forgotten.

After all the man did no harm to me." So he simply nodded his head. "That can easily be arranged," he said. "I will send two plain-clothes men to your place. Let me see, you have three houses on your grounds?"

He knew that there were three houses on the Kimball estate. The grounds covered almost four hundred acres, the most desirable bit of lake property in the city. There

was the old Kimball home and the two houses he had built, one for the second son, the other for his daughter. 

As Rogan remembered it, the man now facing him had lived with his father ever since his mother's death. It was a large estate with a small pond and a private golf course. 

At the thought of this last a question leaped into his mind. "You have your own watchmen on the grounds?"

Kimball had risen to his feet. At his action the judge slid out of his chair to draw his small figure upright. 

Rogan's question seemed to amuse both men. "In a sense we have, Chief, but they are both old men whose duties are largely to keep people from stealing the fruit and flowers. I could have secured private detectives, but somehow I thought the city police would be rather less apt to talk than a private force." 

Rogan nodded. The interview appeared to be over.

After all, he could easily picture what had caused it. Kimball's wife had become frightened. The telephone call to her husband, following those to her father-in-law, had without a doubt alarmed her. The man, left to himself, would never have come to his office-that was certain. 

Funny how many times people sat in that chair, explained their problems and troubles, and then you found somewhere that a woman was the cause of their being there.. 

Rich or poor it worked out alike. 

He rose to his feet as Kimball and his companion, after a goodnight to Manners, started for the door. Just as the judge's hand was on the door knob, there came Manners' voice, low, insisting: "Kimball, there is one thing I wish you would do to-

morrow morning."

The man whirled about; on his face was a surprised look. "What?" "Get a physical examination—the best you can haveblood, heart, everything; and let us have the report." "A physical examination!" came the startled exclamation. "Yes. The best you can get." "But there is no sense in that." 

Manners slowly rose to his feet. "Maybe not. But I just happened to think it might be worth your while."

For a second he thought Kimball was going to burst into a violent refusal. Then the man laughed. "I don't see any sense in your request-but I will do it.

Was going to have one soon anyway. But it will have to be about noon. I have an appointment with the dentist in the morning." 

Manners' eyebrows rose in sympathy; at his expression 

Kimball laughed. "Yes, we all hate the dentist, but I have had an ulcerated tooth for days. After that is cared for I will have the examination. Good-night." And the door was slammed shut. 

As the sound of their footsteps died away, Rogan rose slowly to his feet. His eyes searched his friend's. The tall figure had risen from his chair and was leaning against the desk. Weary, the smooth intelligent face seemed; but there was a half smile around Manners' lips as his eyes met Rogan's.

"What did you mean by a physical examination?" burst out the gruff voice. "Why that crack?" 

Manners' face grew grave. For a second he looked toward the windows. The glass reflected a light from the street. And its shadows danced and flickered before him. 

He turned his eyes back to the chief. "Oh, I don't know, Tim," came his slow answer. A question followed that made Rogan almost jump. "Does Bell happen to be in his laboratory at this hour?" 

Bell was the police doctor, also city coroner. A young physician, he had entered police work for the sake of the experience he would receive. It was Manners' idea it would turn out to be his life work. Keen, eager, with a fine mind, a great curiosity as to life and an eye for details, he had already made his name known in police circles throughout the country. But the question startled the chief. He gave a brief look to see if Manners was in earnest; and what he saw on the calm face caused a thick finger to reach down and push a button in the call box.

Without a word he again sank down in his chair. 

It was only a moment before the door opened as a young man entered the room. He was in his shirt sleeves, his hands stained and marked by chemicals; the long brown hair was in disorder, and between the fingers of his right hand was an expensive cigarette in a very cheap holder. His eyes opened a little at the sight of Manners, to whom he threw out a hand by way of greeting. As he dropped into a chair he half scowled at his superior officer. "It's always the way, Manners," began the pleasant voice. "If I decide to spend a few hours in the laboratory,

why Tim here decides he needs company. I was having a perfectly good time with some cultures when his damned bell rang, and I bet it's nothing of importance. No nice murders?" he queried, with his head on one side. "It's Manners wanted you," growled out the chief as he snapped off the end of a stogie. 

The professor laughed. "I want you to hear Rogan's story, Bell. Let him tell what we have just heard and start with your own telephone calls." 

More to humor Manners than anything else, Rogan began his recital. With his legs far out in front of him, the cigarette and its holder at a rakish angle between his lips, the police doctor listened. There were times when he smiled a little, the smile of a weary and overworked man. 

The irony of the occasion amused him. After years of battling against the old boy, the Kimballs had at last been forced to come to him. That fact pleased him. But when 

Rogan had finished Bell turned to Manners. "It's a good story," he conceded. "Seems to me someone is having a lot of fun with the Kimballs. At that, no one ever liked them. I am not surprised someone would try to frighten them." "He died four days after that telephone call," was 

Manners' grave statement.

Bell pulled himself upright as his face became serious. 

One glance toward the professor and he turned to Rogan. "Chief, that bird who called you every night-he started ten days ago?" There came a grave nod which made Bell wonder of what his superior was thinking. "Ten days ago you say was the first call? And tonight

before you heard that Kimball was dead the call came again? You say the words were: 'It's the tenth day.'” 

Rogan nodded. He saw what Bell was driving at; knew what the question would be. "Could he have been hinting at Kimball's death?" 

That was what Rogan had been wondering for the last hour. Yet one thing seemed to make this thought absurd. "How could he have known ten days ago that Kimball would die tonight?" he asked. "I give it up. After all, I am only a doctor," drawled 

Bell as he inserted a fresh cigarette in his holder. "Maybe 

Harley can tell us." 

Manners shook his head. "Wish I could," he replied. "Like Rogan, I wonder if there was any connection between his messages and those of Kimball. In Kimball's case he was called twice-told he would be dead in four days; and he was."

Bell shifted his weight in his chair. His face was eager, and the interest made the homely features very likable. 

He turned excitedly to Manners: "Tell me, Manners, what were the symptoms in Kimball's death?” "We know only what his son told us. In a sense he did not appear ill until the last few hours. Some gastric trouble, evidently of no importance, which passed away. Then in the last few hours, a sudden collapse, respiratory trouble, pneumonia.' 

Leaning back in his chair, Bell's eyes closed as his face became thoughtful. The fingers of one hand began to tap the palm of the other as if he were counting. Without opening his eyes he asked a question.

"What doctor did they have?” "Carroll." 

Bell sat upright. "They would have," was the dry comment. "The stuffy old fool! But still, though he has little brains, he should know of what his patient died. He was an old man, it looks like pneumonia.' 

Manners' next question caused both the doctor and 

Rogan to start. "Do you happen to think of any poison that would act in four days?" "In the name of God, Harley," began the chief, only to have the words stopped by a gesture from Manners. 

There came a long doubtful look from Bell. “I see what you are driving at, Manners," he admitted, "but you picture a poison that has no effect for days, when the victim is not actually ill. I do not know of any such poison. Listen."

Quickly he ran over the poisons commonly used in crime. His listeners were reminded that most of them caused great pain and could be easily diagnosed. Arsenic, because of the burning thirst it created, would be recognized by any doctor. Strychnine caused violent convulsions. Aconitine, the poison which stood third in favor among poisoners, also caused great pain and nausea. Prussic acid, and all its combinations, was easily recognized by its odor. Atropine so greatly dilated the pupils of the eyes that no doctor could overlook it. As he finished his list he shook his head. "No, Harley—and you know more about poisons than

I do. I know of no poison which, taken today, would make

a man slightly ill, then for two days and a half allow him to return to normal, only to bring about his sudden collapse. But, then, there is an awful lot I don't know about poisons." "What I want to know," broke in Rogan's rough voice, "is this. Why all the sudden talk of poison anyway? Who said anything about Kimball being poisoned?" "Only this," was Manners' grave retort. "Suppose, just for the sake of theorizing, we proceed on the suspicion that there is some connection between the telephone calls you received and those which came to Kimball. He was told he would be dead in four days; he did die. If it was not a natural death it could have resulted only from poison. Nothing else. But, it so happens that I do not know of any poison which produced the symptoms shown in Kimball's illness. I am just building up a case-pure theory." "There is not the slightest reason in the world to think there is anything wrong," was Rogan's complaint. "The 

Kimballs don't." "Yes, and no, Chief. You know that deep down in your heart the telephone calls you received bothered you. You were pretty uneasy, remember, tonight after you received the last one. Kimball did die. The family know nothing of your calls. I was just wondering. And there is another thing we must remember." He paused. "Remember what?" "Edward Kimball told us he received a telephone call tonight. He was told he would be dead in four days. Was that a warning, a threat, or a positive assurance?" "I understand now," said Rogan slowly, "why you

asked Kimball to have a physical examination tomorrow. 

You want a record of the shape he is in." 

Manners' voice was eager as he replied: "Yes, I wanted that. But I also wanted to make certain that the person who called him tonight and who said he would be dead in four days, was not hinting that he was already poisoned.' 

"Already!" Rogan did not like the word. What was more, Manners had startled him. He had always been the one who felt uneasy, blue and depressed. Manners had always laughed at him on such occasions, called him superstitious or something of the sort. Now the professor was the one disturbed. Sarcastically he voiced his thought. "But it is not superstition in this case, Rogan," came the thoughtful protest. "You have a circle of events. First you have your telephone calls threatening that something will happen in ten days. Kimball dies on the tenth day. 

Before his death he receives calls saying he will be dead in four days. Now his son tells us he also has received a telephone call. He is told that he will be dead in four days. Is it all a coincidence, just a ghastly joke? That's my question. Is there any connection, a circle of events-" He paused, to add solemnly; "a circle of death?" 

As no one made any reply he continued: "Of course it is all theory, but I have the uneasy feeling that somewhere in what I have said there is a connection. However, we do not know."

Bell stirred uneasily in his chair. "Of course," he commented, "there was no person in the city who was more bitterly disliked than Kimball. What a grip that family has had on things, and how they have

used all their power and their wealth for their own ends! 

Still, Harley is talking like a detective story. If his theory is correct we can picture only one thing." "What's that?" growled Rogan.

Bell rose wearily to his feet. His tired face broke into an amused smile. "Oh, someone with lots of brains plotting to get rid of the Kimballs." He yawned as he started for the door. "It's an interesting theory, but I am going back to the laboratory and get my coat and go home to sleep. I like Harley's idea of a poison which does not even make its victim feel badly but kills in four days. Only, I don't happen to know of such a poison." 

With a smile he said good-night, and crossed the floor. 

There came the sound of the door opening and closing.

For a few moments the men in the office did not speak. 

Then, with a sigh, Manners rose from his chair. Time he was getting home. After all, he had been a bit reckless in what he had just hinted. He looked down at Rogan. A tender smile crossed his lips at the sight of the white head bent low over a report. Poor Tim, how he hated to lay down his command in five weeks! With this thought in his mind he said good-night and left.

Alone, Rogan pulled the reports across the desk and hurriedly glanced through them. They were of no importance. Afternoon bookings and the early evening charges. 

Then he remembered he had promised to send two men out to Kimball's estate. His hand reached out for the telephone and, when he heard a voice respond, the command was given. After repeating his order that the men be sent at once, he put up the receiver. Then he leaned

back in his chair and sighed.

Though it was difficult to do, he put the Kimballs and the telephone calls out of his mind. Soberly his eyes studied the desk, not observing its wild disorder. With his head thrown back he listened for a moment. Not a sound could be heard. Though he knew there were many men in the building, yet for all that his ears could discern he might have been alone. There came a sudden blast of a horn from a passing car in the street.

Rising he went to the door and stepped out into the hall. Many rooms were visited by the chief. He talked with the radio man sending commands out to the radioequipped cars on the streets, and was told there was nothing of importance. He glanced in at the night captain, saw the evening sheet; nothing there. A sixteen-year-old boy was brought in for vagrancy. Rogan quizzed him and, finding a five-dollar bill in his pocket, thrust it into the youth's hand and told him to get out of the city. His next stop was at the switchboard where he had a greeting for the man at the board. 

All was going well; the machine was running smoothly; better still, nothing had happened. Once more he stepped into the office of the night captain to announce that he was going home. Then he started down the hall for his office where his hat lay on the desk. As his steps died away the captain took the telephone off its hook and called a number. In a moment the call was answered. "Night captain. The old man is going home. Have a car at the steps in three minutes. He will never think of asking for one." 

As he hung up, the officer smiled. The old man was

going home. There was not a man on the force who did not worship him. Rough, gruff he might be, but how he would fight for his men and how square he was to all of them. 

Every man on the night desk had an understanding that, on such occasions as the chief remained late in his office, when he left the building there should be a car at the curb to take him home.

Rogan had taken the hat from the desk. Soberly his eyes glanced at the clock. It was twelve-fifteen. Time he was in bed. He had been uneasy when he went to Manners' for dinner. He was still uneasy. He was trying not to think of what he had heard tonight. In any case, in a short time his work would be over. In thirty-five days he would have to retire. He sighed. He had loved the work. 

His eyes fell upon the calendar on the wall. "Better tear off that sheet," he thought. "It's after twelve-it's tomorrow already. Might forget to do it in the morning."

As he crossed the floor he gave a grunt. Superstitious was he? Well, Manners seemed to be more so tonight. His hand reached up to the calendar; with a jerk the sheet was torn off. Suddenly Rogan's eyes grew very big. With a start he saw the figures before him. Just six letters of black type-and two figures. But they read:

FRIDAY 

13

CHAPTER IV 

THE deep silence of the darkened room was broken by the sudden shrill of a telephone. Noisily the thread of sound echoed for a moment, then died away, only to come clattering back. Harley Manners turned uneasily in his bed and, with an unconscious gesture, pulled the silken coverlet over his head to shut out the disturbing sound. 

For a second the maneuver was successful; then came the bell once more-loud, persistent and demanding. With a start Manners threw back the cover and sat upright. 

Dazed with sleep he peered unseeingly into the darkened room. For a second there was silence, and in it he wondered what could have aroused him from so deep a sleep. Faintly he could distinguish the shadowy outlines of the windows, could hear out on the lawn the wind breathing through the trees. Then, with loud and piercing shrillness, there came again the ring of the telephone on the stand by the bed. "So that was the sound which roused me," was his thought. His eyes dropped to the stand; the hands of the illuminated night clock were standing at three. Bending over, he stretched his hand down for the receiver. As he took it off the hook he knew that if he had been half asleep a moment ago, he was now wide awake. He drawled over the wire, "Hullo."

The voice which flung back an eager reply caused him to slide out of the covers and sit on the edge of the bed. 45

It was Rogan, a Rogan very much awake, his tone anxious and determined. "That you, Harley? Yes-listen to me. Something has broken at Kimball's. I am afraid it's murder. Looks like it." 

Manners gasped out a question. "Is it Edward?” 

Rogan's voice sounded very far away. "No, it was the other son-Henry. One of the plainclothes men just called me—you know I sent two up there to watch the place. If what he says is true, it's a very odd affair. I thought you might like to run up there yourself. 

I am shooting everything we have out to the Kimball place. You can get there in thirty minutes."

Manners gave a hasty assent as he heard the receiver go back on its hook. Instinctively his hand reached out for the wall button, and the room leaped into light. But for a moment he did not move. The news he had heard over the wire had been astounding. If he had been told the victim was Edward, the tall young man who had sat in 

Rogan's office, he would not have been surprised. But the younger son- He decided to stop thinking. It was absurd to puzzle over it.

The room in which he hastily dressed was large, but plainly furnished. A bed stood close to the double windows; three chairs were ranged against the wall, and there was a dresser. Though his house contained hundreds of etchings and colored prints, yet there was only one photograph on its walls. This hung in the bedroom-and when he was dressing he stood for a moment silently looking at the picture. It was a proud, intelligent face which smiled

back at him, with head held high, clean-cut features, and character in the lineaments. For a moment he silently studied it, then slowly turned to leave the room. 

As he opened the hall door, a giant of an Airedale rose slowly to his feet, to stand looking gravely at his master. 

Satan weighed thirty pounds more than the average dog of his breed, and for three years Manners had been trying many experiments upon the animal. These had resulted in his firm belief that the dog could think, and his conviction that Satan knew over eighty words. Every hour that Manners was on his estate the dog was with him and at night he slept outside his master's bedroom door.

Ruffling the big brown head, Manners started down the stairs, the dog following at his heels. On the first floor he stood for a moment by the door looking down at the pleading animal. With a sudden resolution he decided to take him along. He flung open the front door and together they stepped onto the veranda. A second later they were hurrying over the lawn to the stone garage.

It was dark; the wind had increased; there was every evidence of an approaching storm. Heavy, low clouds hid the stars; the trees were bending and swaying in the breeze. In the air was a feeling of dampness, a sign that rain was not far off. Except for the wind, there was a profound stillness, without even the sound of a car to break the silence. The garage was but a dim vague shadow, gloomy and forbidding. There seemed to be something ominous in the air, a sinister feeling as of a warning, which pressed upon him like a damp fog. 

It took but a moment to throw open the garage doors.

Three cars stood upon the concrete floor; a sedan, a coupé

and a roadster. Which should he take? The dog decided the question by walking across the floor and jumping into the seat of the blue roadster. It was the fastest car Manners owned; and with a half smile he decided that the animal's choice was a wise one.

Between the two stone pillars which marked the entrance to his grounds he drove out, turning to the left to go down the long hill which led to the city. Beside him, with his head over the side of the car, sat the dog, peering gravely ahead. As they reached the crest of the hill and started the curving descent, Manners glimpsed the lights of the city. Dimmed they were now, but their reflection played against the low-hung clouds. He glimpsed also the twinkling flame of the lighthouse, seen for a fleeting second as it vanished into darkness.

Just before reaching the bottom of the hill he turned off the main highway. Time was valuable and the dirt road would cut off five miles of city driving, bringing him out on the lake road. The fields were a mass of darkness, the way bumpy and uneven. The glare of the headlights showed every stone and rut. One hasty glance at the instrument board showed he was going eighty. From side to side the car swayed, but his steel-like grip on the wheel kept it to the road.

His mind was a tangle of conflicting thoughts. Rogan had used the word "murder." That meant the old boy was certain. He knew now that the word had not really surprised him, realized that ever since Kimball had told his story in the chief's office it had been his fear. He would not have been greatly surprised if Rogan had mentioned

Edward Kimball's name-but the second son, why had he

been a victim? That was the inexplicable thing.

Murder. He thought of the Kimballs. The father had died that afternoon, the second son had just been murdered. Only Edward and his sister were left. That the family should be hated was a natural thing. Few men had been as ruthless in business as the founder of the Kimball fortune. For a moment he tried to figure out just how large that fortune must be. He remembered that several years ago Kimball had created a great trust in which he had merged all his properties. (It was done to avoid income and inheritance taxes.) But there must also be a great deal of money which was outside of the trust. 

He stopped thinking for a moment to circle around a thoughtless dog wandering aimlessly in the center of the road. Back again on the highway he tried to picture the causes which might lead an individual to attack the city's leading family. There were many who hated the Kimballs, who had suffered because of their greed for money. Where you find hatred, bitterness and jealousy, there you will find the motives for murder. But after all, this knowledge led him nowhere. He stopped thinking to gaze at the road ahead.

He had reached the drive which came out of the city to skirt the lake until it turned down the twenty-mile stretch of peninsula. Half a mile along this road he turned again to run in the direction of the water. It was a private road he entered, lined on either side with high privet hedges. Behind them the homes of the more well-to-do citizens lay hidden in the darkness. Tall trees towered above him, trees hiding the houses among their dense shadows. Then the road suddenly ended.

In front of him was a high wall. Illuminated by his headlights a pebbled road could be seen twisting away between giant trees. A tall iron gate, now closed, barred entrance to this drive. His headlight picked up the blue uniform of a policeman just inside. As he drew up, the officer slowly flung open the gate, slipped through the opening and came over to the car to look within. Perceiving who the driver was the man nodded and spoke. "The chief just got here. Had about ten men with him. 

Don't know what it's all about-though they say it's a killing." 

Manners made no reply. With a wave of his hand he motioned for the man to open the gate wider. Very deliberately the policeman ambled to the wall, fumbled for a moment with a latch, then pushed the gate ajar. The car slipped between the high stone walls to follow the drive for over a thousand yards. It ended in a great sweeping circle before a large stone house. As he came to a stop on the crunching gravel, Manners noticed the row of cars parked along the grass-police cars, every one of them, six in all. He noticed also that the house was a blaze of lights and that men were patrolling the long veranda. 

As the car came to a halt he slid out to the ground, after giving the dog a command to remain on the seat. Five steps and he was by the veranda. He did not go up the wide stairs. Rogan had turned to face the drive and for a second he stood facing in Manners' direction. One glance of recognition, and, with a word to the man at his side, he hurried down to the grass. "I'm in for it, I guess," was his only comment. 

Now that he was face to face with an emergency, the

chief had become the hard-driving, efficient official. His voice barked out a command to a plain-clothes man who was standing by the door of the house-a rough demand that he make certain no one leave the house until the police had had a chance to interview them.

A car was coming down the private road. It was traveling fast, the sound of the engine roaring loudly in the still night. Its headlights played across the gate, which was hastily thrown open to allow the car to enter the grounds. In a second it had stopped directly by Rogan's side. Almost before the sound of the engine died away two men came spilling to the ground. One was Bell, the other—a tall, lanky man who rushed to speak to his chief —was Kent, in charge of the Homicide Squad.

The doctor, however, was not hurrying. He was without a hat and seemed half asleep. The sight of Manners caused him to scowl. He was not in the best of temper as he turned to complain to Rogan: "It's a nice hour you picked to dig me out of bed. 

What's it all about anyway? Is it really murder or just a false alarm? No matter what it is, I'm against having things happen at three o'clock in the morning." 

Rogan whispered a command to Kent who at once started up the veranda. In a moment he was hurrying back to their side. With him was a young patrolman, his fresh uniform showing that he was new on the force. "Burk here is one of the men I sent up to patrol the grounds," Rogan explained to Manners. "He called the station and they got in touch with me at once at the house. I sent up here everything we could spare, including finger-print expert and doctor. There are three houses to

keep covered, a number of people to see. If what he hinted at is true, this town is in for a sensation." He paused and turned to his patrolman. "Now spill out what you saw," he commanded.
