The officer was eager to talk, though he seemed a little embarrassed. An imperative motion of Rogan's hands caused him to find his voice. "It was like this, sir. Mike and I were sent up here, told to keep our eyes on things and nab anyone we found hanging around. It was after twelve when we got here, so we reported at the house." He waved his hand at the lighted veranda. "This one, old man Kimball's." "Well," demanded the impatient voice of Rogan, "get on with your yarn." "It's a pretty big place, sir," was the confession. “We fixed things so that we could circle the grounds and meet every thirty minutes. Place is fenced in, and where there is no fence there's a hedge—a high, thick one. About one o'clock I happened to be close to the other house." "There are three houses on the grounds-which one do you mean?”

There came a vague motion of the officer's arm. "The one Kimball's younger son lived in. I spotted him coming out of the side door, saw him go over the grass toward the lake. First I thought I would follow and ask him what he was doing out at that hour. But-" the voice hesitated-"after all, I thought whatever he was doing was his own business. He lived here. Besides—well you see ' 

Rogan's body seemed to stiffen, as he gazed with a stony expression at the embarrassed young patrolman be-

fore him. "If he had only gone up to that young man," was the chief's thought, "without a doubt there would have been no crime." Life and death depended on little things like that. If he had only followed him. No doubt, like most people, he was awed by the Kimball name. 

Then Rogan's good nature came rushing back. After all, the officer was young. We all make mistakes. "Did you follow him?" "No. I saw he was going toward the lake. I told Mike about it; but he thought, like me, that it was none of our business what he was doing. But a little around three I got a bit curious." 

The remark interested Manners. "What caused you to become uneasy?" he asked. 

The officer shook his head. "Just one little thing: I noticed when he went out of the house that there was a light shining in a second-story window. It was the only light in the house. A bit before three it dawned upon me that the light was still there, and 

I began to wonder. If he had come back, the light would no doubt have been out. But it was still to be seen. I decided that he hadn't come back." "That showed good reasoning," stated his superior. "Then what did you do?" "Well, I walked over the grass till I reached the high bank by the lake. I figured that, as he went over that way, he must have been going to the lake. No car had left the grounds, so when I reached the bank I decided that he would have gone down the stairway, for I knew he never would have fought his way through the thick bushes. I had my flashlight and started down the stairs. And

then-" He hesitated as if dreading what he must say. The scowl which he saw on Rogan's face, however, prompted him to speech. "I found him at the foot of the steps." He paused to slowly shake his head. "Dead-shot."

He was ready to talk now, eager to give his story, but for the time being Rogan had heard all he wished. Further words would be useless. There was work ahead, a lot to be done. For a second he stole a look at the house. 

Every light must be on in that house, he thought; all the windows were ablaze. Later he would have to see the families—but not now. He turned to the patrolman, telling him to guide them down to the shore. 

The five men struck off over a closely cut lawn. Save for the long, thin ray of light from the officer's flashlight, it was still dark. Manners knew they were very close to the lake, could feel its cool dampness in the breeze which swept his face; sensed by the deep blackness that pressed upon them that it would soon be dawn. For a second the old proverb, "It's always darkest before dawn," flashed into his mind.

No one spoke, nor were they hurrying. Bell was walking by the side of the officer holding the flashlight, and his arm was wrapped around a bulky bag. Kent and 

Rogan were with Manners, a little in the rear. No one spoke or felt like speaking. The darkness, broken only by a narrow shaft of light, the utter stillness, did not make for speech. All were dreading what they were about to see. 

Rogan was thinking hard. No longer was there a feeling

of depression in his mind, but he was worried, anxious as to what lay ahead. It might be a very simple crime, but somehow he felt certain that ahead there would be many long perplexed hours. Behind him walked Manners. He was glad he was there. But he had known when he reached him on the phone that he would be there. For a second there returned the troubling thought of the interview which had taken place in his office early in the evening. 

He dismissed that; it was no time to think of two problems.

By this time they had reached the end of the lawn. The ground dropped sharply downward to the shore. They could hear the faint sound of water lapping against the beach. Small trees, bushes and tangled shrubbery plunged downward to the invisible water. The shore was a dark void of space, a pit of gloom, without form or substance. 

Then they saw the steps. 

The flashlight played over the first step, and they crowded toward it; then it started slowly to waver downward; step by step the flickering flame descended. Ten steps in all there were, leading from the grounds to the shore, steps poured from concrete, which shone white as the light swept over them. At last the flame passed the last step, to flicker upon the shady beach. Here it suddenly came to rest.

Below them, with the narrow ray of light playing across it, lay a dark motionless object. It seemed for a brief instant to be but a shadow thrown in high relief by the light. But there was something about this shadow which made them pause as they gazed soberly downward. They were gazing at the motionless body of a man lying in a

sinister attitude at the foot of the steps. 

It was Rogan who broke the silence, but it was Bell who snatched the light from the officer's hand and started down the steps. No one paid any attention to what the chief had said; all followed the doctor. At the bottom of the steps they watched Bell fall on his knees and carefully lift the dark head whose face was buried in the sand. 

Inch by inch the light traveled across the motionless figure. "There never seems to be anything very dignified in death," thought Manners. "This man at my feet is deadyet he looks like an obscene scarecrow. Only a little while ago he was alive, a thing of power and of pride. Now—"

He shook his head. There was nothing dignified in death -only a sort of pathetic helplessness. He turned to look up the steps, they were only faintly visible. Poor beggar! 

Perhaps he had slipped and fallen. Then a startled exclamation from Bell caused Manners to whirl about.

The body had been lying partly on its side, the arms thrown out in a half circle, the legs twisted toward the bottom step. Bell had slowly started to turn the man on his back. As the exclamation escaped the doctor's lips, they saw him pause, then complete his motion so that the man was lying with his face downward. Very slowly the physician rose to his feet and pointed. 

Their eyes followed the accusing finger. Here at their feet lay a motionless figure for whom the darkness did not matter-nothing could ever matter. But this they had known for some moments. It was something else which caused a half-oath to escape from Rogan's lips and which made Manners' lips tighten into a straight line.

Projecting from the back of the grotesque figure at their feet was a thin steel shaft whose polished surface glistened under the beam from the flashlight. For a moment Manners could not believe his eyes, but he saw clearly outlined the row of bright feathers on the end of the shaft. He caught his breath as he realized the incredible thing they implied. His own amazed exclamation was broken into by Rogan's shocked question: "What does it mean?"

Bell's voice came in answer. The tone was cold, though the intense excitement could be perceived: "It's murder, Rogan. He was shot by an arrow."

CHAPTER V 

ROGAN had suddenly felt his years. He must believe his eyes-yet for an uneasy second he wondered if he could.

Murder was not unknown to him; there had been many murders in the twenty years he had headed the police. 

But they had been mostly ordinary crimes, so ordinary that after a time they fell into well-defined patterns. One or two had presented bizarre features in the beginning, but in the end the usual formulas had been satisfied. But this one- His eyes stole down to the silent figure on the sand, glimpsed the shining shaft of the arrow. Then suddenly he swore.

An arrow was protruding from a man's back; he knew little about arrows, had thought they were things children played with. Never, in all his police experience, had he heard of anyone being killed with an arrow. He gave a grunt. It was absurd after all to be startled by anything. 

At his feet lay a man-dead-murdered without a doubt. 

No one could shoot an arrow into his own back, nor, for that matter, into any part of his body. It was murder, a case for the police. Unthinking his hand went down to touch the steel shaft. Manners' cry caused him to desist. "Don't touch it, Rogan," came the eager warning. "Whoever shot that arrow had his fingers on the shaft. 

There may be finger-prints."

No one spoke as Manners bent down to examine the arrow. It was a long thin shaft of steel and this fact was 58

of importance. He happened to know that it was only within the last few years that steel arrows had come into use. He knew also that it was America which had introduced the hollow, thin tube. In 1927 one of his friends had won the Double American Archery Round, using for the first time steel arrows. This knowledge brought a second conclusion to his mind. 

Steel arrows were rather expensive things. From the portion which protruded from the man's back he judged that this one was at least thirty inches long. It told another story also. Few persons were interested in archery; bows and arrows cost money; the sport was expensive and it would take an expert to know just where to drive that steel shaft in order to kill. Also, it would take strength. As the last thought came to him he rose and turned to Rogan. "I presume this is Henry Kimball?”

Bell replied, and there was an uneasy shrug of his shoulders as he spoke: "Yes, it's Henry Kimball without a doubt." "Well, Rogan, at least you can start with one fact.” "What?" "The old saying, 'look for a woman,' won't apply here. 

You can be certain no woman killed Henry Kimball." "I don't see how you can say that. You have nothing to go on." "Oh, yes, I have," was the retort. "It so happens that 

I know a little bit about archery. The arrow that killed

Kimball is made of steel and about thirty inches long. It was driven through his body, and of course would require a heavy bow to shoot it. Women's bows take a pull of

from eighteen to thirty pounds, though I would say that twenty-five pounds is an average. Their arrows are shorter and lighter than a man's. This steel arrow was shot from a bow requiring a pull of from forty to eighty pounds. I think sixty would be about correct. It would be a powerful weapon, having a terrific driving force. The length of the arrow will show the weight of the bow and will prove that no woman shot it." 

Rogan's eyes examined the steel shaft. They lingered over the feathers at the end, narrowed when he thought of their significance. Then, all at once, his thoughts clicked. It was absurd to be dallying here on the sand. 

It was murder; there was work ahead. No doubt Manners knew what he was talking about-deep down in his heart he acknowledged that. But time was passing and there was much to be done. He turned to Burk; barked out a command that he find the finger-print officer and have him come down to the shore. As the officer started up the steps there followed a second command. The photographer must be found and instructed to take pictures of the body. Burk vanished in the darkness and 

Rogan turned to Bell. "How long do you think he has been dead?" he asked.

The doctor cast a thoughtful look at the dim shadow lying at his feet. He was thankful Rogan was holding the flashlight and that it was playing aimlessly over the sandy shore. Before speaking, he found a loose cigarette in his pocket and slowly lighted it. "Not more than three hours. Perhaps less." Rogan started to reply, but the doctor added: "There are bruises on his face, as if he might have rolled down the steps.

It's my idea that he was shot just as he was about to descend to the shore." 

Rogan grunted, turning the flashlight upward. The white concrete steps rose before them-ten in all, wide steps, with an iron hand rail on either side. The light swept upward until, for a second, it rested on the first step; then it traveled to the trees, high trees which were very close to the stairway. Only for a moment did the men pause to look, for Rogan had started to climb. Manners and Bell followed him.

Reaching the bank above the shore they saw that a line of trees ran almost to the edge of the first step. They were giant trees, and below them, running down the bank to the shore, was a tangled mass of shrubbery; shrubbery comprised of small bushes and tangling vines. Far across the dark lawn was the house, every window ablaze with light. On the lake, almost invisible to them now, was a light, a boat moving slowly down the coast. 

Manners looked down the length of steps. If Bell was correct, and there seemed no doubt he was, Kimball must have been shot just as he was about to descend to the shore. The position of the body showed that he had plunged downward from some height. The flashlight had revealed the bushes; they were not broken, as they would have been if anyone had struggled through them. Kimball could not have come to the shore by way of the bushes; their very thickness would have prevented his doing so. He had obviously been shot at the top of the steps and had fallen down their white length; and that presented a new problem. 

Where had the murderer stood? Burk had not men-

tioned that Kimball carried a flashlight. The grounds were so familiar that he needed none. Yet it had been dark, very dark. Manners pictured the man coming over the lawn. Just as he reached the steps, as his back was turned, the murderer must have pulled the bow which shot the arrow. The bow would be long and heavy. Where had the man stood? (He knew it had been a man.) He glanced around.

Close to the edge of the bank was the shadowy outline of the trees. Though the darkness made it impossible to distinguish them clearly, it was his impression that they ran the entire length of the estate. High trees they were, planted no doubt to hide the grounds from anyone who might be on the lake. Now they were an unbroken line of gloom, a line of darkness in which could be seen not even a shadow. On each side of the steps there was a slight clearing, but not of any great width. It would have been very easy for an individual to hide in the shadow of the trees.

He pictured what might have taken place. Behind one of the trees-without a doubt the one closest to the steps -the murderer had been waiting. Knowing his victim was approaching, he had stood ready in the dark shadow.

Then, as Kimball paused, outlined at the top of the steps, he had come from the shelter of his hiding place. His victim was a fine target silhouetted against the deeper darkness. The murderer had shot-and Kimball had plunged to his death.

There was a corollary which followed: The murderer had known that his victim would come to the steps. There was, therefore, no doubt in Manners' mind that Kimball

had been keeping an appointment at the moment he had been killed. It did not stand to reason that he would have left his house at midnight to cross the dark lawn simply on a whim. It had been an appointment-with the murderer. Unless he had known the very moment Kimball would be at the steps leading to the beach, the murderer would have been unable to commit his crime. 

Manners voiced his thought to Rogan. The chief listened-without comment. For the time being he did not care to speak. In a few moments he would have to interview the Kimballs-and he did not relish the task. And what did he know? Nothing, only the fact that the younger son had been killed-and with an arrow. What 

Manners was saying without a doubt was correct, but the theory must be discussed later. He sighed, thinking of the long weary hours ahead-hours spent in interviewing people, listening to their stories, trying to discover some item of value amid a mass of irrelevant words. That, however, was police work, a long hard grind of careful detail. It was the only way in which crimes were ever solved. 

There was one more thing to do before his experts came. Giving a command, he walked to the nearest tree, sweeping the ground with his light. Leaves hid the grass, though there were small patches of dirt. Though the light crept over every inch of the bare ground, its flame revealed nothing. With a shrug of his shoulders, Rogan walked over to the clearing to look under the trees at the left of the steps. Here he found something.

Under the first tree, a massive elm, was a patch of green mold. A few leaves half covered it, leaves which

had been pressed down into the soft dirt. As a footprint it was not an entire success-little more, in fact, than a suggestion that someone had stood there. Rogan bent down as he allowed the light to rest upon the mold. Someone, he felt convinced, had been standing under the tree; faint though it was, an impression of a heel remained. There was something else: clinging to the matted leaves were a few grains of sand. As he rose the chief pointed a finger downward. "There is little doubt someone has been standing under this tree." He scowled down at the faint impression. "Not that it's of much good as a footprint, for it's of no value at all. But see those bits of sand where a heel crushed down the leaves? Whoever stood here came up from the shore. The Kimball shore is all sand. Manners was right. 

Whoever shot that arrow was hiding in the shadow of this tree, waiting for Kimball to reach the head of the steps. It won't help us very much to know this-but it's something."

A car was coming across the lawn. The uneven going caused the machine to lurch from side to side. A powerful searchlight was playing over them, its ray wide and bright.

It was the new police car, radio equipped, and with a high-powered light fastened to the running board. In silence they watched it approach, saw the driver bring it to a stop at the edge of the bank. Then the light swung downward, rendering the beach as light as day. 

Rogan made a hasty decision. He would leave Kent in charge at the shore. Bell would have work to do; the finger-print man would be needed; and the photographer would have to take some pictures. He walked to the car

to speak to the middle-aged man. "Hart, you have Kent tell you of the heel impression under the tree. It's pretty faint. Do your best to get a picture. And photograph the steps and the body." He turned to the finger-print official. “Look at the end of that arrow for finger-prints. One should be there.” He paused to ask: "Where's Burk?"

Someone told him that the patrolman was trying to find the other policeman-the one sent with him to guard the house. With the remark that he wished to see Burk again before entering the house, Rogan started across the lawn. 

There was no need for him to stay behind. The men descending the steps were experts in their line. They knew what to do and would do it. But he was surprised to hear

Manners remark that he wanted to go again down to the shore.

It was a sudden impulse which caused the professor to linger behind. He succeeded in obtaining an extra flashlight from the police car, and with this in his hand walked back to the tree where they had seen the impression. 

Bending down he carefully swept the light over the moldy leaves and soil. There was no doubt about the faint print of a man's heel. It was behind the tree; obviously the individual who had stood there was hidden from anyone approaching the steps. But Manners was looking for something else, another impression which he hoped he might find. In a moment he found it.

It had been his theory that there should be another impression in the soil. Kimball had been killed with a steel arrow, shot from a bow at least five and one half feet tall, perhaps six. He knew that such a bow would

require a pull of from forty-five to sixty pounds-perhaps even eighty. Whoever had been standing behind the tree would have found the bow an awkward thing—over five feet in height. He would not have held it in the air; the natural thing would have been to rest it on the ground. 

The soil was soft, the mold damp, it would have made an impression. And he found one.

Two feet away from the mark of the heel he discovered a slight hole in the ground. As he bent over his discovery he saw that the hole had been made by a pointed object -an object that had sunk almost two inches below the leaf mold. For a moment he stood motionless staring down at the circle of light at his feet. A small fact, a hole, yet it pictured what had taken place. As the murderer waited for his victim to come over the grass, he had rested his weight upon his bow. Then something else occurred to Manners. 

Kimball had been killed with a steel arrow. Only within the last few years had steel arrows been introduced into archery contests; they were used mostly in America. 

They were costly things, something the average man could not afford. Furthermore, the average man knew nothing about archery. It was the sport of the few, and the cost of the equipment was exorbitant. If the murderer had used a steel arrow, the chances were he had used one of the new steel bows. That would explain the sharp hole in the ground where the bow had rested-and might give some hint of the social class to which the murderer belonged. 

The light traveled upward in Manners' hand, sweeping aimlessly in a circle. Trees, bushes, shrubbery sprang

into relief as the flame swept round. The professor was considering a problem. There were a few grains of sand in that heel impression. How had the murderer entered the Kimball grounds? The estate was almost enclosed by a high stone wall, and in the few places where there was none, there was a high, thick hedge. True, it might be possible for an intruder to climb over the wall-and he might even struggle through the hedge. But there had been men on guard. (However, it would have been rather easy to elude them.) The sand in the heel impression suggested that the man had walked on the shore. Anyone could land a boat on that beach. The deep darkness would have effectually screened both boat and passenger. 

Turning, he walked to the first step and looked downward. The powerful searchlight was playing over the sinister figure on the sand. He saw a camera near the body, noticed opened bags and apparatus. Rogan's men were at work going through the first preliminaries, the ordinary precautions which are taken in every murder case. But as

Manners looked he had the feeling that, although all these things were needed, they would be of no avail. 

With a start he realized that it was no time for theories -Rogan was waiting. But before he went to the house there was one thing he wished to do. Hastily he descended the ten steps. The police, working over the body, did not even raise their heads to glance at him. Nor did he stop to speak. Walking across the sand he came to the water's edge. Because of the breeze there was a slight surf. It was getting lighter; dawn would soon creep across the sky.

Dimly the water could be seen, stretching away into the darkness.

Keeping close to the water's edge, the flashlight casting a narrow ray before him, he went up the beach. It was a sandy shore, crunching under his feet with every step. 

There was no tide on the lake. True, once in a while the wind caused the water to rise a little higher than usual on the shore, but there was scarcely a breeze stirring now. Up the beach he went, his eyes always searching the sands. He was looking for a mark which, if he was right, he knew he would find at the water's edge. And just as he decided he had best turn back, he found it. Running up from the wet sand was the clearly marked outline of the prow of a boat. 

For a second he scrutinized it, then turned to glance down the beach. A thousand yards away he saw the policemen's figures outlined in the light pouring down from the bank. They seemed oddly grotesque, bending over some object which he could no longer distinguish-though well he knew what it was. He turned again to the marks in the sand. They began at the water's edge, running perhaps five feet into the dry sand-a well-defined triangle-made by the prow of the boat that had been dragged out of the water. 

One of Manners' hobbies was boats. His own speed boat, the Flash, was the fastest in the city. The marks before him had been made by a large boat, and he knew by the narrow triangle that it was an expensive one. 

Gravely he followed with his light the deep indenture in the sand. In a sense it proved nothing, though they did tell him that a boat had landed upon that shore only a short time before. His eyes narrowed as there came the sudden conviction that his theory was corroborated.

Close to the water's edge, pressed deep in the moist sand, he saw a small hole. Three feet inshore was another. 

As he studied the marks, he knew with certainty what had made them. Someone had stepped from a boat, supporting himself for a second by an object held in his hand. 

The marks in the sand could bear but one interpretation. 

A short time ago someone had landed a boat on the shore. 

It was evident that he had dragged it up on the shore and in stepping out of it had used something for a support. 

Manners knew that the two round holes in the sand marked the spots where the end of a bow had rested. 

There was a half-burned log a few feet away. He dragged it close to the triangular lines. It would serve as a marker. Then turning, he hurried down the shore to the side of the busy men. They had finished. The body was covered with a blanket (which made the unseen form more sinister than before). Bell had gone, but the photographer was still fussing with his camera. Telling of his discovery, Manners advised him to take a photograph of the marks in the sand. With a grunt the man agreed to do so. Then Manners began his ascent to the lawn. 

He paused, for a moment, upon reaching the top of the bank. Dawn was drifting across the sky. Far over the lake the sky was becoming brighter. He could hear the uneasy stirring of birds above his head, chattering. The lawn could now be seen, the tall trees dimly defined, and, beyond them, a gray shadowy outline of a large stone house. Though dawn was approaching, from every window of the house a light shone. For a moment he studied the rambling outline; then, dreading what was ahead, he started toward it.

He reached the veranda just as a long, rakish roadster came sweeping around the graveled drive to stop before the steps. The tall, white-haired man who hurried out of the car was Henry Stone, the Police Commissioner. An aristocrat, the finest lawyer the city had ever had, tolerant and cultured, Stone had surprised his friends five years before by accepting the office of Police Commissioner. In a sense he was Rogan's superior, but in reality he allowed the chief to run the department without political interference. He gave one quick look at Manners and a smile broke over his fine face at the sight of his friend. 

They started up the steps together. Stone's arm had entwined itself with Manners'. His voice came low and vibrant: "Looks like a nasty mess. What is it all about?"

Hastily, as they stood before the door for a moment, Manners told of what they had discovered. From the hall a light shone upon Stone's clear-cut face. His lips twisted ironically at the story and as his hand pushed open the door he shrugged his shoulders. But he said nothing as they passed into the house. 

Every light was on in the long passage. The house was old-fashioned, with a central hall, from which curving stairs ran to the second floor. On each side were rooms with open doors. Lights were everywhere, on the stairway, shining from the second floor, and from every room. 

It seemed as though by turning them on someone had sought to drive away the shadow which had swept over the family. From a room far down the hallway came a voice; it was loud and shrill and demanding. Both men recognized it. Judge Parrish was airing his opinions to

listening ears. And the judge was angry.

With the insolent voice ringing in their ears, they walked down the long hallway. They had a glimpse of lighted rooms, rooms well furnished but empty. Two thirds of the way down the passage they came to a pause before an open door. From within it a voice rumbled and roared like an angry mastiff. As they paused on the threshold Stone's hand fell upon Manners' arm and he said dryly: "His master's voice-doing his stuff to earn his salary."

The room into which they were looking was an oldfashioned library. On three sides were built-in bookcases, their glass-covered shelves rising to the ceiling. Behind the doors could be seen the somber bindings of ancient volumes. Directly opposite them was a large fireplacethough now there were no flickering flames in its yawning cavity. A table stood in the center of the room, surrounded by four chairs: a table of antique and massive design. Around it were clustered four persons, one man and three women. 

For a moment no one observed Stone and Manners.

The professor's eyes swept the room, pausing at the group around the table. His glance rested for a second upon Edward Kimball; saw there was a worried expression upon his angular face; passed to the woman standing beside him not a beautiful woman, one with a stern, severe countenance whose jaw was set in fixed determination-swept on to the second woman who was leaning on the table, her face stained with tears, her mouth trembling. The first woman he knew to be Edward Kimball's wife; this second one, then, must be the wife of the man

whose grotesque, silent figure he had seen on the beach. 

But it was the third woman who held his gaze. 

Like everyone else in the city, he had heard much of Kimball's daughter, had even met her several times. 

She was standing some distance from the others, an ironical smile playing over her lips. She would be a beautiful woman was his thought—with her shapely body, her crown of black hair, were it not for the selfish face, the sensual lips. With her head thrown arrogantly back, one hand resting upon the edge of the table, she was casting a contemptuous look at her sister-in-law. As he noted the expression upon the curling lips, Manners remembered all the gossip he had heard of Marjorie Kimball. Stories of wild escapades, of battles with her father, flashed into his mind. Then Parrish's voice again came ringing over the room.

The stocky little figure was standing before the fireplace. One hand was in a pocket, the other waved wildly in the air. The round face was red; the voice which shrilled through the room, was insolent and angry. "I tell you, Rogan," came the piercing tones, “It's an outrage. A family like the Kimballs will not submit to being herded together like animals while you ask foolish and absurd questions. I can have you thrown out of the police department for this insult."

Rogan was standing close to the wall. His eyes never left Parrish's face. If he had given vent to his feelings, said what he thought of the stout little figure before the mantel it would have taken him some minutes to finish. 

Instead he scowled, then half smiled as he spoke. His voice was low and cold and the tone caused Stone to

chuckle. "Judge, neither you nor anybody else can put me off the police force. I retire in thirty-five days." His voice suddenly changed, became hard and commanding. "And

I don't care what you say about the Kimballs' position in the city. There has been a murder done here tonight."

The word seemed to shock the room. Manners was certain Edward Kimball knew that his brother had been killed, but the long narrow face was expressionless. Only one person in the room showed any emotion. It was the sister of the dead man. For a second she stared at Rogan.

The dark head was thrown back, the blue eyes studied the determined face of the chief. Her look was grave. Manners could see that she was both troubled and anxious. 

It was her voice which broke the silence of the room. "We were told that my brother was-" there came a pause; then throwing back her head she continued- "dead-murdered," she said bravely. "But-but no one has told us how he died."

For a second, Rogan studied the woman standing beside the desk. His glance took in the beautiful figure, the proud dark head, noticed also the quivering lips. "This woman is afraid," he thought. "Of all the Kimballs, there have been more stories about her than about all the others. Proud, arrogant, self-willed, she has invariably gotten herself into trouble. Yet she is but a child, not over twenty-three." He answered her question. "He was murdered-there is no doubt of that," he declared. 

Judge Parrish threw out a flabby hand as if he did not like the word. Edward Kimball's face grew white, though

he made no effort to speak. It was his sister who again broke the silence. She was leaning against the desk as if she needed support. Her voice whispered through the room: "Yes-but how?" 

Blunt and direct was the answer. "With an arrow," said Rogan.

There came a curious stillness. It was broken by a sound, half a moan, half a spoken denial. Then suddenly there followed another sound. With a gasp the woman had slumped down to the floor. Edward Kimball's sister had fainted.

CHAPTER VI 

It was Rogan, strange to say, who rushed across the room and lifted the woman in his strong arms. It was not that he was the nearest-her brother and Judge Parrish were much closer. It might have been the suddenness with which she crumpled to the floor which prevented the others in the room from moving. Rogan was accustomed to unexpected situations. In thirty-five years he had learned never to be surprised at what might happen. As the woman's body began to sway, he was on his way across the floor.

Lifting her in his arms, he placed her in a chair beside the desk. He was thinking, and his thoughts were very irrelevant. "She looks slight," flashed through his mind, "but she is much heavier than I would have expected." 

A second thought, far more important, followed. "Why in the devil should the remark that her brother has been killed with an arrow cause her to faint?" As he barked out a command for someone to get a glass of water, he was thinking that, though like the rest of her family she had known that her brother had been killed, she had been ignorant-as had they-that he had been killed by an arrow. It was that last fact which had caused her to collapse.

Her eyes were slowly opening now. Standing a few feet away from the chair, Rogan looked gravely down at the young woman. “She has a beautiful figure," he thought, 75

"yet that face is self-willed, sensual and ill-tempered. But then, I do not need to look at her to know that. Of all the Kimballs, she has been most in the public eye. How many times has she been arrested for speeding or driving while under the influence of liquor?" He shrugged his shoulders as he thought of other stories. 

Manners, standing by the door, might have disagreed with his friend. Marjorie Kimball was not over twentythree. The professor gave the white, relaxed face a swift glance. It was a beautiful face, and he liked her mouth; it showed character. But there were tense lines about her lips as if she were unhappy. He thought of the stories told about her father: how he had ruled his family with an iron hand-insisted that their every action be according to his desires. This young woman interested Manners. 

Of all the Kimballs she was the only one who had shown any psychological signs of rebellion. 

Her brother was now hurrying to her side. The glass held in his hand contained a pale yellow liquid. "Scotch," thought Rogan-and he hoped it was. The woman had opened her eyes. She threw a frightened, bewildered look around the room, evidently not knowing where she was for a moment. Memory came sweeping back to her; the white face flushed a deep red. Her hand swept out to snatch the glass from her brother's hand. With a gulp the yellow liquid was drained; then she sat upright. 

The look she cast around the room was a mixture of fear and defiance. Manners noted one thing. There were two other women in the library-her sisters-in-law-and neither of the two women hastened to her side; neither one had met the glance the younger woman threw at them.

On the stern, angular face of Edward's wife was a contemptuous expression. Never, thought Manners, had he seen self-righteousness more clearly expressed. As he was wondering what it might all mean, there came the spluttering, protesting voice of the judge. "This is an outrage, Rogan," shrilled out the stout figure. The little man was almost dancing up and down in his anger. "A damnable outrage. To subject a family like the Kimballs to your crude police methods." 

With a start Rogan whirled around. His growled command partly expressed his own confusion: "Shut that mouth of yours and keep it shut. One more word out of you and I will have you thrown out of the room."

Though Stone's expression did not change, yet inwardly he was smiling. Parrish was always a pest, and it was time he was put in his place. With a nod to Kimball, the police commissioner started across the floor. "I think you will have to admit that it's an affair for the police, Judge," was all he said. He looked at Rogan, raising one eyebrow as he saw the chief captured his glance. 

Rogan's face was calm, but inwardly he was uneasy.

Though it would have been impossible to explain why he felt so, he was positive that not one of the individuals in the room would be able to throw any light upon the problem to be solved. There were doubt and horror upon the faces of the women, anxiety upon Kimball's. But there were no signs of guilt, nor the slightest suggestion that anyone was afraid. Then as his eyes passed over to Marjorie Kimball, he suddenly made one reservation in his

mind.

She was sitting on the edge of the chair, her head thrown back, her eyes fixed upon the police official. Apparently she was calm; but he noticed that one handthe right one, clutching her blue dress-kept uneasily opening and closing. The fine lips were shut, and it seemed to him that it cost her an effort to hold them in a tight line. There was plenty of rouge on her cheeks, yet in the places where the color had not been applied the skin was a deathly white. For a moment he gravely studied her, realizing that she was frightened. Yet the knowledge did not reveal itself in his statement. "I am going to be as brief as possible," he said, speaking directly to Edward Kimball. (Stone half chuckled at the smooth courteous tone.) "If you are all frank, this disagreeable matter can soon be over."

Edward Kimball's voice, troubled as it was, held an eager note. "We will do all that we can to aid you," he promised. 

Rogan nodded. There should be little trouble with this family, but he wished he knew what he ought to hunt for in his questions. Deep down in his mind he had the feeling, whatever might be the motive for killing Henry Kimball, it would never be found in this room. Still, the necessary questions must always be asked. He ventured the first: "I presume none of you knew that Henry was dead until my men notified you?"

Henry Kimball's wife suddenly sank into a chair. As she bent down over the desk, her head in her arms, Rogan knew that she was weeping. It was Edward who spoke.

"No," he said slowly shaking his head. "One of your men notified us that my brother was dead. I got up, dressed, and went over to his house and told Mary." "There are three houses on the grounds?" asked Rogan more to gain time than for any other reason. "Yes. I lived here with my father. When my brother married, a house was built for him. It's about a thousand yards from this one. And," he hesitated for a second, "my sister has a studio cottage on the bank overlooking the lake. Three houses is correct." "Would you know of your brother's movements after he came home from the city?"

Kimball shook his head. His face was grave, with tired, weary lines around the lips. "We are not what you might call a friendly family," he said with a faint sigh, "though there has never been any continued bad feeling among us." His hands went out in an inexplicable gesture. "Perhaps we were too selfcentered. My brother and I were not like some brothers, always close; though we never had a true quarrel. He would come home from the insurance company and I would never see him as a rule until the next morning at the office." He added: "I did not see him last night." 

Rogan started to speak but, seeing that Kimball had not finished, allowed his question to die upon his lips. "You see, Father never gave up the control of his properties. He established a trust, the Kimball trust, but that was for legal reasons-to escape taxes and so forth. 

He was president of the insurance company. True, I was carried on the books as treasurer and Henry was secretary; but the titles meant very little. Father gave us each

a house and a good allowance, but he ran his business affairs to suit himself. I saw very little of Henry except in the office.” "Do you think your brother had any enemies?" 

A startled expression swept over Kimball's face. "I know just what he is thinking," was Rogan's thought, "those damned telephone calls. And what's more, he knows that his family was not liked." But he waited for Kimball's answer; and when it came it was only what he expected to hear: "None that I know of. Of course a family of our wealth is bound to be disliked by some people." "The mob always hates the successful individual," started Judge Parrish's shrill voice, but Rogan's scowling glance caused the voice to desist. There came an uneasy silence. "Getting nowhere, learning nothing at all," was the thought which trickled through Rogan's mind. "It's a queer family. Have an idea that girl showing too much of her leg knows something; but I will leave her to the last. And there's the dead man's wife to be questioned. 

She looks tired, weary, a faded-out blonde. Wonder why it is men with money marry such absurd women. Well, it's none of my business. And I know darned well she won't be able to tell me anything. Still, I must ask her." 

His voice broke the silence. As he spoke, Henry Kimball's widow threw a pathetic, wondering glance around the room. "Do you know anything about your husband's leaving the house last night?"

She was far from a beautiful woman, nor was she well

dressed. The eyes which struggled bravely to meet the chief's gaze were outlined by dark circles. It was a plump face, to which make-up had been applied-make-up through which tears had streaked deep lines. Not an intelligent face, though honest in its frightened homeliness. 

Her lips, thick and unlovely, trembled as she spoke. "Yes," was the low answer. 

It was not what Rogan had expected. For a moment he stared at her. The woman was crushed and broken, a homely, pathetic figure. Her dress did not fit her well; the brown hair was disheveled; but the eyes which met 

Rogan's were clear and honest. His voice was courteous as he spoke: "You knew your husband was going out?" "Yes," was the faint reply. "He had a telephone call about eleven. And-" 

Rogan shot into attention. "A telephone call?" 

The untidy head nodded. "Yes; he told me he was going to see someone." 

Rogan's keen glance shot across the room. "Did he tell you why he was going, at midnight, to see this individual?” 

The round face trembled as her eyes filled with tears. "Yes," she faltered. "He told me someone wished to explain to him what the telephone calls to his father had meant." 

It was an unexpected answer. Rogan had been trying to forget the telephone calls-his own and those which had come to the Kimballs. Here they were again, though to save his life he could see no connection. He saw a

sudden gleam pass over Manners' face but disregarded it as he asked: "You knew about the telephone calls?" 

Her head went back in surprise. "We all knew; knew that someone was trying to frighten Father." 

Rogan stifled back a grunt. Not much in her answer. "Did you sit up for him?" he ventured. "No. We have separate rooms. I know he went out about twelve. But he often took a walk over the grounds at that hour. I went to bed. Not—” the round face whitened at the recollection-"not until Edward told me what had happened did I know there was anything wrong." 

There fell a silence. In it was heard the call of a robin on the lawn. Day had come; the darkness had vanished.

Out on the grass the birds were welcoming the returned day. In the library was silence, a silence which Rogan wished he did not have to break. Edward Kimball was standing beside the old-fashioned desk, his long face troubled and anxious. But it was Henry Kimball's widow at whom Rogan was looking. Slumped down in her chair, she was applying a handkerchief to her tearful eyes. Her world had vanished-that she knew. It was useless to ask her any more questions. As the chief came to this decision, the silence was suddenly broken.

Marjorie Kimball had risen from her chair, her tall shapely figure held erect, the dark head high. There was both contempt and impatience in her voice. "Why don't you question me?" she asked. 

Rogan's glance was sharp. "Have you anything to tell?" he countered.

The dark head was held high. For a second her eyes traveled over the room. Then she spoke. "Yes. I saw something.' 

"What?" came Rogan's voice.

She did not speak for a moment. In the glance with which she swept the room, Manners saw both fear and determination. Then came her voice, low, compelling: "I was unable to sleep, so a little after twelve went out to walk over the grounds. I left my cottage and—” "Where is your house?" broke in Rogan. 

She half smiled. "Almost at the end of our grounds. A little cottage on the edge of the bank overlooking the lake. I have a housekeeper," she volunteered.

The chief passed up her information. "What did you see?" he half growled. 

There came a second's hesitation. "I left the house and started to walk along the edge of the bank above the shore. I went about five hundred yards when I saw-" she paused. "Saw what?" the chief barked out. "A man. He was hiding behind a tree. A tall man with a beard. I saw the gleam of his eyes." 

It was Manners who broke the silence. "You saw the gleam of his eyes?" 

The dark head nodded. "Yes. I heard a sound, looked over toward the dark line of the trees and saw a man's eyes gleaming at me in the darkness. I turned to run back to the house." "Did you have a flashlight?" Manners questioned. 

A startled look swept over her face. For a second she

hesitated, as though trying to recall. Then, looking the professor in the eyes, she slowly shook her head. "Of course not," was the answer. "This woman," thought Manners, "is very much afraid. 

I wish I knew why. But there is one thing I do know."

Walking past the table he crossed to the fireplace. 

Here he whispered something into Rogan's ear. What he said caused the round face to grow even redder, as there came a quick nod of the white head. Then a look of consternation grew upon the chief's face. In a second he spoke to the group at large. "For the time being," came his uneven tones, "I will not require your presence." The gray eyes traveled over the room, noting the weary, anxious expression upon all the faces. His voice became softer. "I would advise you all to try and get some sleep."

The room stirred into sound. Chairs were thrust back; a woman's pleading voice began to speak. The chief walked slowly to the desk to bend down and speak to 

Marjorie Kimball. What he whispered could not be heard, but she rose quickly to her feet. With a hand clutching the desk for support, her dark eyes stole over to the wide window. For a second her face was grave, then with a shrug of her shoulders she started toward the hall. Behind her trailed Rogan, the police commissioner and Manners.

The room Marjorie Kimball entered was at the end of the wide hallway. It was close to the entrance door, a small room, much cluttered with dilapidated furniture. 

An antique desk stood against the wall, its surface covered with old magazines and papers. It was furnished

with a worn sofa, six chairs of various designs, and beside a small table stood a much-used leather armchair. 

A room for a man, thought Manners, and for one who had not cared very much for style. The lights were on, though they were no longer needed. Daylight was filtering through the windows.

Walking over to the desk, the woman swept the men with her glance. She stood erect, her figure clearly outlined by the clinging dress. Long and lovely was the curved line of her thigh, a curve accentuated by the attitude in which she stood. The dark head was thrown proudly back as her grave glance swept over the three men. Only a slight trembling of her lips betrayed her nervousness. Slowly the look swept over Manners, lingered for a moment upon Stone, then rested at last upon the chief. Her voice came slowly: "You wanted to see me alone?"

He had indeed; but the remark caused him to cast a troubled look at Manners. Rogan had but vaguely understood what had been whispered in his ear a few moments before. It was a matter for the professor to probe. After all, this interview was his idea. Besides, the calm young woman standing close beside the desk baffled the chief. 

Manners' suggestion had been startling to say the leasthe would allow him to ask the questions. His look toward his friend was a command. 

But Manners did not, for a moment, speak. Gravely he considered Marjorie Kimball. "She is a beautiful girl," was his thought, "and despite the unrest and tension in her face, character shows there. She is unhappy." (The irrelevant thought came that everyone was unhappy.)

"She is afraid, also; no woman who is not afraid would have that look deep within her eyes. But she is trying to be brave-her glance has not turned away from mine." "Miss Kimball," he began carefully, "you said you went out from your cottage. You told us that you saw a man standing behind a tree; said you saw the gleam of his eyes in the darkness."

A doubtful look swept over her face as she slowly nodded. "You carried no flashlight?" was the reiterated query. "No," came the low response. 

Manners' hand fumbled in his pocket. Finding the cigarette case he brought it forth, extended it to the woman. Very slowly a cigarette was taken, more slowly lighted. Not until his own was also alight did he speak again. His tone was a curious mixture of irony and humor. "Miss Kimball, why did you tell us such a preposterous story? You never saw any man. I happen to know it." He smiled. "So do you." 

Her dark head was thrown back as a warning flash of anger leaped into her eyes. But the violent protest he had expected did not come. She took two short steps and her hand fell upon the back of a chair. Slowly she seated herself, sinking down as though she were very weary. 

She swayed a little as she spoke; her voice was cold and toneless. "How do you know I did not see any man?" "You said you saw the gleam of his eyes in the dark.

You could not have done so. The eyes of a human being never gleam. Even an animal's eyes must have a light shining upon them before the reflection can be seen. But

you had no light. In the darkness you would not have been able to see a man's eyes. In any case they could not have gleamed at you, and without a light you would have been quite unable to distinguish them. You saw no man." The voice grew softer. "Why did you tell us that story?" 

Her body was rocking to and fro. As she raised her eyes from the floor, Manners saw she was very near the breaking point. Her face was deathly white, with only a tiny spot of color burning in each cheek. She looked like a child, a frightened, unhappy child. The arrogant woman had vanished. As his eyes met hers, pleading and helpless, he knew that whatever she was about to say his sympathy was already hers. 

It seemed for an instant that she was not going to say anything. So long did the wait appear to Rogan that he half started to growl out a command, but a motion of 

Manners' hand caused the words to falter. Marjorie Kimball slowly shook her head. "You are right," came her low confession. "I saw no man-for that matter I never left my cottage.'

Again the chief started to break forth, only to be interrupted by Manners' low voice. "Why did you tell us that you left your house?"

Words suddenly began to flow uninterruptedly from between the woman's rouged lips. Words uttered in a cold, even tone; a voice containing not the slightest suggestion of passion. So icy was the monotone that Manners recognized it as an expression of utter hopelessness; the voice of one who felt that it was of no longer the slightest use to dissemble. Though he could not see her eyes-for

she was looking at the floor-he knew that she was speaking directly to him. "What's the use," said the unemotional voice. "You know my family, knew Father-everybody knew him." 

She hesitated, then the hopeless voice continued: "You know how he ruled his family-like a czar. Everything we did, every action of our lives, he insisted upon ordering. 

My brothers, well, they did not seem to mind. Henry was a good deal like Father; he loved power, enjoyed feeling that people were dependent upon him; loved to drive down town in the Rolls and know that people envied him. 

All my life I have been forced to do what the family wanted-what my brothers and father commanded. Never have I had a chance for happiness-never able to do what I wished." The voice trailed off into silence. 

Rogan's eyes narrowed as he watched the figure sunk down in the chair. "It's all true, what she says," was his unspoken comment. "Kimball did rule his family with a hand of iron. I have even heard that he picked out the clothes the women wore. And the sons took after the old man. But still, I don't see what all this has got to do with the investigation. Yet, this woman knows something." 

He broke into speech, only to receive a warning glance from Manners. Marjorie Kimball was again speaking. "Tonight, or rather last night, after Father's death, I told my brothers I was going to be married, that I would be able to get away from all this. And-" her face lifted and they saw a pathetic smile tremble for a moment upon her lips. "And" suggested Manners. "Henry, of course, made a scene, ordered my fiancé

from the grounds, told him that if he ever set foot on them again he would have him arrested. And-" "Your fiancé?" questioned Manners. 

A softer expression swept quickly over the white, tired face, and a tiny smile trembled for a second upon her lips -only the faintest trace of a smile, vanishing almost as soon as it came. Something came into the dry voice, a hint of deep feeling which she was trying to hide. "You know him, Professor. Walter Oxford."

The name was familiar to Manners, but that the quiet, assistant professor of English, whom every day he saw walking over the campus, should be engaged to the woman before him, seemed hard to realize. She gave him, however, no opportunity to puzzle over it. "After Father died, Walter came up from the University and for an hour we talked things over. Together, then, we went to Henry's house and I told him that as soon as possible we should be married. Then I could get away from all this." Her arm swept out again in a wide circle. "All the narrowness and bickering in the family." "Your brother, I judge, was not pleased." 

There came a dry laugh from her lips. "No," she agreed. "But then he was never pleased with anything which he did not himself suggest. He called 

Walter a good many names—a pauper, a fortune hunter and God alone knows what. Then he told him to get out of the house and off the grounds, at once. He even tried to threaten me. But-" the proud dark head was held high-"I am not a person easy to frighten." 

Rogan turned to throw a significant look at Stone.

There must always be a motive in a murder; one had

now been found for the Kimball crime. But to his surprise the face of the police commissioner remained grave, and, as he looked back at the woman, Rogan noticed that his eyes now bore a friendly look. She had risen from the chair. Walking to the desk, she stood confronting them all. There came a nervous gesture of her head, her lips trembled. "What you don't understand is this," she said. “I did make up that story about seeing a man behind a tree; 

I was afraid-" "Afraid?" came back the repeated word. "Yes, afraid. There are bows and arrows all over this place. I won the state archery contest last summer—it was the only sport my brothers ever engaged in. And—” 

Her voice trailed away only to come swelling back. Its timbre now was a curious mixture of fear and defiance. 

Her dark eyes were full of fright-and pleading. "You see," she went on, "I wanted you to think there was someone behind that tree; a man with a beard, for yesterday afternoon I let Walter take one of my bows and a few arrows. That was why I told you an untruth."

She paused, turning impulsively to Rogan. Then she spoke again, her voice now as pleading as a child's: "Now that I have told you the truth, won't you please let me go to my cottage? I am so tired."

CHAPTER VII 

THE lilting whistle of a quail floated into the little room, and the sound caused Rogan to cast a moody look toward the window; the sun was climbing into the sky, giving promise that the day would be warm. His tired eyes glanced down the tree-lined drive, observing the cars parked near the house. He shrugged his shoulders at the sight of the automobiles, most of which belonged to the police department. Night had ended, day returned, and his men would be weary. The thought caused him to turn slowly and study Marjorie Kimball.

She was standing beside the desk, a hand clutching its disordered surface for support; and in her weary eyes, which looked at him, was an appeal for mercy. The slim shoulders drooped, and she swayed a little. The eyes beseeching him were those of a bewildered, frightened child, not those of the proud, arrogant woman who had entered the room. Somehow, was Rogan's thought, she seemed very much alone. A scowl crossed his face. She had a brother and two sisters-in-law in the house. They knew why she had been brought into the room. Yet not one of them had even come to the door, had even asked if they might be of assistance.

Hard-boiled, rough, without a spark of human emotion: these were some of the phrases that had been flung at Rogan by Kimball's paper. Little they had known the man. Much of his roughness had been assumed, a gruff 91

mask to hide a sentimental heart. And now Marjorie

Kimball's white, appealing face was too much for the chief. He tried to reassure her by what he intended should be a smile; it more nearly resembled a scowl. "Miss Kimball, you may as well go to your cottage and rest," he said. "Better sleep all day. I may have to see you again—but I do not need you now." He paused, then added roughly: "Don't worry too much." 

The dark head bowed a weary thanks. Slowly she started for the door. Just before she passed into the hallway, Manners' voice caused her to turn. "Miss Kimball, you say you let Walter Oxford have a bow and some arrows. I presume they were the usual arrows you used yourself in archery contests?" 

The question seemed to startle her. But Manners' smile brought a return of confidence. "Oh, yes," came the reply. "I do not remember which bow he took, but the arrows were the usual ones, wooden." 

She lingered as though expecting another question. 

None came; the dark head slowly turned, and she vanished into the hall. As she disappeared, Rogan heaved a weary sigh, fumbled in his vest for a moment, found a stogie and then eagerly lighted it. "Why that question about arrows?" he demanded. "Just this, Rogan. The arrow which killed Kimball was a steel one. I have little doubt you will find it was shot from a man's bow. It's rather doubtful that a woman would have steel arrows. You can ask Miss Kimball later. But women shoot a much lighter arrow than do men and use a much smaller bow." 

The chief gave a grunt. He was tired and in bad humor.

"What difference does it make what she gave him?" he protested. "He might have had a steel arrow. If he did, no doubt it would be capable of killing a man, even if shot from a woman's bow." 

Manners nodded. Rogan might be right. Though the more he thought of the assistant professor of English, the more positive he became that the man knew nothing about the murder. The borrowing of the bow was an unfortunate circumstance, mostly because it had occurred only a few hours before the crime. But Walter Oxford was a shy, diffident individual whose only passion in life was English literature. That he should be concerned in a murder seemed absurd. Manners voiced this thoughtonly to receive a sharp, sarcastic retort from the tired police chief. "It may be very absurd, as you say," came the ironic voice. "No doubt college professors never are mixed up in such sordid things as murder. But at the present moment that bird is very much mixed up in this one. Quiet or not, he has some explaining to do. I will have a man get hold of him at once."

He sighed again. It would be a good many hours yet before he would get any sleep. There were hundreds of questions to be asked; the reports from the photographer and finger-print expert must be examined; Bell would have lots to say-and there was this young college professor. Rogan half swore. Why had the darned fool borrowed a bow and arrows a few hours before Henry Kimball was killed? Why under heaven should a grown man want to shoot arrows anyway? That was something for children to do.

Manners watched the mingled emotions play over his friend's face. The chief was tired; yet, unlike his own case, it would be hours before Rogan could sleep. The police detail work was ahead. Examination, investigation, the painstaking search for every fact. As for himself, he was going home and to bed. He half chuckled at the thought of the outraged expression which would come over his housekeeper's face when she discovered that he had been out most of the night. But he was going home —and that at once.

He conveyed his intentions to Rogan, only to hear it bluntly granted that it was a good idea. Saying good-by to the two men, he went out into the hall. It was now a silent hall, without the slightest sound seeping through the open doors on either side of its long length. Someone had at last put out the lights. He saw, as he walked toward the front door, that the rooms were empty. Not until he pulled the door open and stepped out on the veranda did he see anyone. Then it was only the policeman on guard.

With a nod to the officer, he proceeded to the front steps, lingering there for a moment to drink in the freshness of the early morning air. Though a slight breeze was coming in from the lake, the sun was already warm. Below him was the graveled drive, running down through rows of elms to a high privet hedge. To his left he glimpsed the lake, its surface a bright blue with only here and there a tiny whitecap.

The roadster stood where he had left it, and he could see on the seat the brown, curled-up ball which was the dog. Not until Manners came down the steps and reached

the side of the car did the animal stir into attention. 

Disturbed, one eye opened, he slowly pulled himself upright on the leather seat. Giving his master a disapproving look, he yawned. In a second Manners did the same. 

As he climbed into his car to sink down on the seat, he realized of a sudden that he was very tired. The police cars were still lined up under the trees, though it was his impression that several had departed. One thing surprised him. There were no reporters. This seemed incredible. The city's richest man had died; a few hours later his younger son had been murdered. Furthermore the crime was odd, fantastic-just the sort of crime the newspapers enjoy. Then he remembered. The morning paper was owned by the Kimballs, and the evening one would have had no night man at the police station. 

He drove between the long rows of trees, glad for the song of the birds, the warmth of the sunshine. A policeman opened the iron gate which led through the stone wall, making some sort of half salute as he did so. The private highway ran up to the lake road. As he drove past the closely clipped hedges, he glimpsed, behind them, large houses, half hidden by the trees. In a few moments he had reached the main highway.

Though there was little traffic, he drove slowly. The air was fresh and clean, the slight breeze warm against his face. Beside him, sitting very erect with his head stretched over the side of the car, was the dog; his eyes always on the road ahead. Tired though he was, Manners enjoyed the drive.

He was not theorizing over the crime. The temptation to do so was strong, but Manners was a psychologist.

He realized that in his weary condition his thoughts were very apt to go plunging off on a wrong tangent. And if there ever was a crime in which it was much too early to indulge in theories, it was the present case. One thing did trouble him. There was something he should have said to Rogan, a warning which should have been given.

To save his life he could not think what it was. He did not try to think. He knew that, when he was less weary, the missing idea would come leaping out of his unconscious.

It was just eight when he drove into his garage. With an eager bark the dog leaped to the ground. More slowly did his master step out of the car and walk over to the white house. The grass was a mass of sunshine, and his tired glance rested for a moment upon the many flower beds which dotted the lawn-colorful spots against the green of the grass. Wearily he walked up the steps of his veranda and, inserting a key, opened the white door. 

Going to the kitchen he had a word with his elderly housekeeper. The tall, white-haired woman gazed suspiciously at him as he said that he had no interest in breakfast, that he was going to bed and wished to be disturbed by no one. She half sniffed as he went over to the large icebox and took from it a dark, squatty bottle. He must be tired, she thought-"and I just know he has been out all night with that Timothy Rogan." Manners' Scotch housekeeper had never approved of his association with the police. He was a professor-far above the ordinary run of mortals. He had no business running around with policemen. 

With a glass in one hand, the dark bottle in the other,

Manners stepped into his library. For a moment he gazed soberly over the room. It was the spot he loved best on earth. Behind the low rows of bookcases was his wellknown library. Upon the walls were the gayly colored prints of the French eighteenth century, and his wonderful collection of Rops. Peace, rest, intelligence whispered through the room-a room where a man could sit down and be alone with his thoughts. 

He felt the presence of the bottle under his arm. If he had ever needed a drink he required it now. Gravely he watched the yellow liquor fall into the glass, then, after measuring it with his glance, poured out a little more.

Slowly re-capping the bottle, he placed it on the desk. His hand reached down for the drink but, before lifting it to his lips, he looked over to the sweeping bay windows. The breeze was causing the dark draperies to sweep into the room. He watched the trembling fabric as he slowly drained the Scotch.

In the hall, on his way to his bedroom, he halted before the telephone. It stood upon a mahogany table with a chair close by. The instrument caused him to remember something. Sitting down he dialed a number. In a moment a voice replied, and of it he requested a favor. Then, replacing the receiver, he sat for a moment scowling at the instrument. Perhaps he had best not call up the other number- With a sudden resolution he reversed his decision; the receiver was again removed and his long fingers dialed a number. No reply followed. The receiver was once more replaced and he reached for a small pad of paper. Upon it he wrote a brief note to his housekeeper, asking her to call the number he wrote down.

Undressed, his blue pajamas on, he walked across the floor to throw high all the windows. For a moment he stood looking down at his lawn. The morning sun played over every corner. The wind had died away and the trees were still. Beyond the ivy-covered stone wall the ground sloped sharply downward, and in the distance could be glimpsed the lake: a lake blue as a maiden's eyes. Motionless, he stood taking in the beauty of the scene. Then he turned toward the bed.

His waking, when it came, was with a sudden start. For a moment the brightness of the room bewildered him; then came the memories of the night. All his thoughts for a while were disconnected; tangled threads of memory. 

At last, with a start, he became suddenly wide awake.

The room was silent, and, as he turned his eyes toward the windows, he saw the curtains sway toward him. From the lawn came the cry of a bluejay, followed by a quail's note rising from the meadow below. His eyes went to the clock on a table beside the bed. The hands stood at three. 

It was indeed time he was getting up!

His bath taken, dressed in a light gray suit, he descended to the main floor to find that a cold lunch had been arranged on his dining table. Beside the plate was a brief note, containing his housekeeper's apology for having gone to the city, and mentioning that she had called the telephone number he had jotted down on the pad. 

Soberly he ate his lunch, spending a good deal of time over the newspaper which he found beside the plate. 

It was the Star, the Kimball paper. Not a word could he find in its columns mentioning Henry Kimball's death. 

There was plenty, to be sure, about his father. Big head-

lines on the first page announced the death of the city's wealthiest man; the story spilled over to the second and third pages. Lighting a cigarette, Manners leaned back in his chair as he glanced over the eleven columns of print. If all he read was true, there had never been in all history such a paragon of virtue and ability as George Kimball. But as he allowed the paper to fall to the floor, he grinned. "It's a fine thing to own a newspaper at the moment you die," was his thought. How different had been the man's life from the picture his paper drew of it. 

But after all it was far too fine an afternoon to remain in the library. There were the first sheets of an essay to be corrected. Taking them from the long desk, he went out to the veranda. Under the tallest elm was a small table; because of the fine weather it had stood there for days. Taking a wicker chair under his arm, he crossed the grass, to come to a halt in the shade of a tree. Sinking down in the chair, he took his pipe from his pocket, slowly packing the bowl with the dark, stringy English mixture he smoked. When it was going to his satisfaction, he turned his attention to his essay. 

It should have interested him, for he had enjoyed the writing of it. The subject was one dear to his heart—a discussion of the gallante literature of France in the years preceding the Revolution. Few would read the magazine in which it would be published, but the few were a critical audience. He enjoyed, as a rule, the unseen battle with his enemy, the proof reader; but, after perusing a few lines of the printed proof, he threw it upon the surface of the table. The glitter of the ironic passages did not interest him.

Last night a man had been murdered, and it was an unaccountable, bizarre murder. The motives for such a deed were few; in the end they always proved to be simple. Murder was committed for revenge, for money or for one of the many obscure sexual reasons. From long experience he realized that few normal individuals ever committed a premeditated murder. Such an individual might commit a crime on the spur of the moment, as startling to the criminal as to the victim. But as he thought of Henry Kimball's death one thing seemed apparent; if there had ever been a deliberate, premeditated murder, this was one.

A sound came from his driveway. A car had come through his gates, and drawn to a roaring stop. It was a long machine, painted a vivid red. As soon as he saw it he knew to whom it belonged. He did not need to see the tall, lanky individual who half fell out of the seat, to know that he was receiving a visit from Frank Gale, editor of the Kimball paper, the Star.

The man who waved his hand in his direction and started in a leisurely fashion over the grass might have been around forty, though he looked fifty. The brown suit was expensive enough, but it had not been pressed for weeks and the tie was outrageous in its eccentric appeal. 

A half smoked cigarette was stuck at a rakish angle between the thin lips, and the man's face wore a cynical smile. Manners, at his approach, rose to his feet and started for the house.

It was into the library that he led Frank Gale. Excusing himself, he vanished for a moment, returning with an unopened bottle of Scotch, two glasses and a bottle of

English soda. Gale's eyes brightened as he watched the bottle placed on the table and observed the cork being drawn. When the Scotch was pushed over to him, he filled the tall glass halfway, then thoughtfully added a little soda. Manners' drink was far more modest. Not until his visitor had drained his drink and at his suggestion had refilled the glass, did the professor speak.

He knew the man sitting before him (there were few in the city who did not know him). Editor of the Kimball paper, he was without doubt the most brilliant journalist in the state. Deep cynical lines crossed the nervous face; a rather hopeless look peered out of his dark eyes. Many stories were told of Frank Gale, stories which reflected little to his credit. Manners had heard them all, but, as he looked at the tired, strained face, he thought of the nights this man had sat in his library, discussing, with keen interest and insight, rare and obscure pamphlets dealing with the French Revolution.

Gale's long fingers picked up the tall glass. A sip and then another. Then came his drawling voice: "It's the best Scotch in town, Harley. I always say so." 

Manners laughed. "If so," he replied, "the credit goes to my father. Before prohibition came he laid away hundreds of cases." 

Gale sighed. "I would have enjoyed knowing your father," was his comment.

In the silence which ensued, the editor's eyes studied the long room. They lingered longest upon the rows of bookcases. Manners' library, the best chosen he had ever seen, always intrigued him. He noticed particularly the

rows of glass-covered shelves at the end of the room. 

Thousands of books were there-Manners' crime collection-legal reports, odd and obscure comments upon the curious bypaths of humanity. The sight caused Gale to speak. Carefully pouring two fingers of Scotch into his glass—and refusing the soda—he began: "You know, Manners, you are really the only person 

I ever envied. You have money enough for all your wants; you can do as you wish, read all the books you are interested in. You are content. As for me" He swore a violent, obscene oath. "Think of my job. Wet nurse for the Kimballs! Running their daily paper-shading every article writtena woman who keeps a house of ill fame has the same sort of job."

Manners' grave eyes studied the worn, nervous face. 

He had the opening he wished. "Two of the Kimballs are dead," he said. 

Gale drained his glass; empty, he thrust it back upon the desk. "Yes, one was murdered. I have often wondered why someone did not bump them all off." He paused, his voice became half apologetic. "The girl would have been all right if she had been given a chance, but the family never gave her one." 

Manners bent forward. "Gale," he said earnestly, "I asked you to come up here for a reason. I want to get your slant upon the Kimballs." He hesitated, then smiled. "Rogan is up against a murder which he may find almost impossible to solve. 

No one in the city knows the Kimballs as you do." He

paused, realizing that he must make an exception. “Unless it is Judge Parrish," he added. 

Gale mentioned the judge in five short words. Then he suddenly laughed. It was not a pleasant laugh. "Parrish! That white-haired, smiling hypocrite! God knows, Manners, Kimball was no saint, but at least he never covered his crookedness by preaching sermons about morality. Parrish-bah! "You want my low-down on old man Kimball? I've known him only during the last few years. You know his early history. His fortune began with a lucky land speculation; then he started to pick up lake front property. 

Later, wherever there was a foreclosure sale on land, there you would find Kimball. By the time he was fifty he owned almost every valuable bit of real estate in the city. After that followed the founding of the insurance company." Gale lighted a cigarette, laughed. "The old boy had only one object in life-money. He never cared where it came from so long as he got it. You know he owned most of the property in the vice district, and I would say two thirds of the speakeasies we have in the city are in his buildings. He loved to make money, but how he hated to spend it! His sons were kept on a small allowance for years. I happen to know it's only lately they've been receiving much in the way of salaries. And -get this, Manners, for it's true-Kimball's eyes searched into every penny they spent, even their cigarettes, the clothes they bought. Only within the last few years did the old man begin to change." 

Eying the bottle, Gale paused to throw another spot of liquor into his glass. With a sneering smile he drained

his drink. "In the last few years the old boy did spend some money. Bought the Rolls, even decorated his grounds. But he did even those things only to impress the city. He began to enjoy hearing people say, 'That's Kimball, the city's richest man.' You ask my opinion of him. God knows I have run his dirty advertising sheet enough years. 

Well, here is what I think of him—a narrow, ignorant, cruel, stupid—” He paused, laughed, and shrugged his shoulders. "Oh, well, fill in the adjectives yourself. They will all be true. To be honest, I always thought he was our most undesirable citizen."

Manners made no reply. Instead he studied the lanky figure slumped far down in the chair opposite him. Gale's hair needed combing and a shave would not have been out of the way. What he had just said was only what Manners already knew. He would agree beforehand with the conclusion of the outburst. But he had not brought Gale into his library merely to be told that.
