Somewhere in the background of the Kimball family, in their manner of living, their reactions to life, might possibly be found the motive for the murder of last evening. What it was he was seeking he could not have told; he only knew it was his opinion that in this case the ordinary physical clues would be of no avail. Something else was needed. "How about the two boys?" he questioned. "Well, of the two I think Edward might have turned into a fairly good sort of chap if he had even been given a chance. But of course his father crushed every bit of spirit out of him. He is just a slow, ordinary individual, who might have held down a bookkeeper's job if his

family had been without money. But, Henry? Well,

Henry was a good deal like his father. He had the same arrogant conceit, the same disregard for the rights of others. You understand me-neither boy was what you might call educated or cultured. In no sense. Edward was far the better of the two; he has more human qualities. 

Still, I think the old man was a bit disappointed in them both." "What do you mean by that?” "Well, remember his trust? The Kimball trust? The primary reason for its existence was to avoid the national inheritance and income taxes. But there was also a secondary reason. The old man wished his estate to increase after his death. He was afraid that neither Edward nor 

Henry had the business ability to bring that about, though in the last few years it is true he began to look with more favor upon the younger son." "Did the boys have any hobbies, or-any private vices?" 

Gale laughed. "Vice? My God, to have vices costs money! No, they were good boys. In fact, could have been that model 

Sunday-school pupil I used to hear about when I was a kid." He laughed, "Oh, they smoked, took a drink once in a while, but nothing else. You see, they all lived together right under the old man's nose, and they never had a chance to bust loose." He paused to add thoughtfully: "I don't suppose they ever thought of doing it." "Gale," came Manners' low voice, "if you were expecting someone to murder two of the Kimballs, which two would you pick out as the likely victims?"

The editor threw back his head and grinned. "It sounds like one of those problems we run daily in the papers. Three persons are lost in the desert. You can save only two. Which two would you save?" He pulled himself suddenly upright to cast a glance of suspicion at Manners. "Murder—and which two of the Kimballs? It's my idea it would be the old man and Henry. They were the most disliked. But there was only one murder, I thought." "True," was the admission. But the question had in truth caused a sudden doubt to come into Manners' mind. 

The forgotten question of the early morning had leaped out of his subconscious. As the thought came over him he controlled his expression. It would never do to allow Gale to realize that he was startled, or why. He quickly put a question. "I presume once in a while Kimball would receive a threatening letter?" 

Gale threw back his head and laughed. "Not a letter, but letters, Manners, lots of them. You see, the paper never took any stand on any question, municipal or state, unless it was to protect some Kimball interest or further some of their ends. The old boy liked to drop into the office mornings-why, I don't know-and he would show me the anonymous letters he received. Letters by the score. You know that, in his grasping for money, he must have injured hundreds of people. You bet your life he received letters. Letters of all kinds. But they never bothered him. One thing I will say for him, he had courage aplenty. Fear was unknown to him." "Letters written upon the cheapest sort of paper, con-

taining many misspelled words and the most atrocious grammar?" Manners queried. 

Gale nodded. "Always. All the letters were from ignorant, stupid people, individuals without the slightest spark of intelligence." "Intelligence." The word was the cue for Manners' thoughts. Stupid and ignorant people committed murder -in fact, the bulk of all murders might be laid to them. 

But their crimes were the ordinary killings-sudden outbursts caused by jealousy and unstable emotions. The premeditated crime, shrewdly thought out in its every detail-ah, that was a very rare thing in criminal history. 

Once in a while, however, there was such a crime, committed by some individual who had intelligence, though, as a rule, one emotionally unbalanced. The suspicion forming in Manners' mind was not a pleasing theory. Henry

Kimball's death had been murder; further, Manners had decided that it had been a carefully planned and wellthought-out crime. If this was so, trouble lay ahead. 

If Gale would only go. There were other questions he had thought to ask him, but they could wait. Until this tall gentleman, who was slumped down in his easiest chair, left, he would be unable to call the police station. And he needed to speak at once to Rogan. Slowly and deliberately he rose to his feet, hoping Gale would take the hint. The man did. Languidly, he in turn pulled himself upright, as he half yawned. "I suppose you will tip me off on anything that breaks.

I know you had a reason for insisting that I come up here, and for all those questions you asked about the Kimballs.

But it's all right with me. If I were the editor of another paper I would be very keen to discover what you are after; but," he grinned, "being the editor of their own sheetwhy we will try to give as little space as is possible to what has happened to the Kimballs."

Manners walked with Gale out to the veranda, watched him go down the drive and approach his machine. But he did not wait to see the car leave his grounds. Instead, turning, he hastened into the hall. Sitting down by the phone he swept the receiver off its hook and hastily dialed a number. In a moment came Rogan's voice slow, gruff, impatient and tired. The tone changed a little when Manners spoke. "Rogan," he shot into the phone, "one thing we both forgot this morning. It's the most important thing you have to do." 

No reply came for a second. Then the chief's voice came over the wire; it was a cautious, doubting voice, now, the tone of a man wondering what he was to hear. "Do what, Harley?" he asked.

Manners' lips bent closer to the receiver. His tone was serious, insistent as he said: “We forgot one thing, Rogan.

There must be an autopsy upon George Kimball. Have it at once, before it is too late."

There came a shocked silence, broken into the next instant by the amazed words of the chief: "Hell! Harley, what under heaven do you want me to get into? There was no suspicion of foul play there. His doctor said his death was a natural one. Why an inquest?" "Because," came the quick retort, "the real mystery is not the death of Henry Kimball, but the death of his father.

We know how Henry died. But, remember the telephone calls, we do not know how his father died. I am afraid you need that inquest, Rogan, if only to avoid something worse." "Avoid what?" came the shocked question. "Another murder, Rogan."

CHAPTER VIII 

WITH a snort of disgust, Timothy Rogan threw a photograph upon his cluttered desk. For over an hour he had been examining photographs. Not that the task had brought any results, but he wished to satisfy his own curiosity. A dozen prints lay before him. As pictures they were good enough-clear-cut, finely printed, the sort of thing he expected from his finger-print expert, aided by the official photographer. But they were simply picturesnothing more. One was really a work of art. It was the photograph of an arrow, the glistening steel shaft thrown into high relief by the dark background. 

He had spent some time over this picture and the others which accompanied it. His eyes had narrowed as he first glimpsed the print, and he had gazed at it with an expression of wonder. The long steel shaft, with its gayly colored feathers at one end, the sharp pointed tip at the other, had been an instrument of death. But it was the two attached prints which really interested him. The report pinned to them said: "Cannot find finger-printsthink smirch shows where they were wiped off.” The two prints were enlarged photographs of a wide, smooth stain that had been found upon the shaft of the arrow.

The chief studied these for some time, with, in reality, unnecessary concentration. The report might be rightthen again it could be wrong. Only one fact remained of importance; no finger-prints had been found on the arrow. 110

It brought but one conclusion to his mind. Whoever had used the arrow realized that its steel shaft was an excellent surface on which to leave finger-prints and had taken good care that none should be left. Without a doubt the enlarged smirch revealed that the arrow had been cleaned. 

The other photographs were simply detailed thingspictures of the steps, of the body lying on the sands, of the triangular lines Manners had found in the sand. There was one, however, over which he spent a considerably longer time. A clear print it was, showing the heel impression he had discovered under the tree. Yet when he threw this last on his desk he grunted. As a photograph it was a great success; as a bit of evidence it was of no value at all. 

There were also reports, a number of typewritten sheets on the desk to be read through. Bell's short, concise statement was the first. The autopsy showed that Henry Kimball's death had been almost instantaneous. The arrow had pierced the heart. At the bottom of the sheet the police doctor had written one line in his short, jerky handwriting: "All I know is that an arrow killed him." Rogan grunted as he flung this report on the desk. "Not much information there," was his thought. Nor was there much in the others he read through.

They were statements of the detectives. Though the homicide squad had been busy, their results were pitifully meager. When the typewritten sheets were thrown aside, Rogan realized that he knew little more about the crime than he had in that first hour after arriving at the Kimball home. As he reached for a stogie, he decided in disgust that no one knew anything about it. 

He glanced at the clock as he lighted his smoke. It was

four-thirty-and he was very weary. At ten that morning he had climbed into bed; at one-thirty he was back at his desk. Once he would not have minded the lost sleep, and he did not like to admit that he minded it now-that it was his sixty-five years that made him unable to stand the strenuous hours of the work in which he had once delighted. And he realized something else.

Crime, as a rule, was a simple thing. Nine out of every ten murders fell into well-defined patterns. If one theory did not solve them, another, of the eight remaining, did. 

True, once in a while there would come a crime outside all previous experience. But even those exceptions were usually solved in the end. Irrelevantly he wondered, for a second, how many murders had really ever come to the attention of the police; pondered over a remark Manners had once made, that only one out of every ten crimes ever came to the light. It might be so; he did not know. But of those the police did have brought to their attention, they certainly managed to solve the bulk. 

But this crime- He shook his white head. There was not the slightest hint of a motive in the reports he had examined—no reason to suspect anyone. His thoughts lingered over Marjorie Kimball's remark about having lent a bow and some arrows to her fiancé. But he put that aside. Until he could talk with the young man himself, he did not wish to think about him. Somehow Oxford did not fit into the picture in his mind, provided what he had in his mind could be called a picture-which he doubted. 

There were those who worked from conclusions to facts; he had always preferred to do the opposite-assemble his facts, then reach his conclusions. And the facts he now had

were of little avail.

It had been a warm afternoon, and for comfort his coat was off. The white hair needed combing and the blue tie was absurdly crooked. A stogie was stuck at a sharp angle between his clenched teeth, and the eyes which surveyed the desk were cold. For a moment they rested on the mass of papers, scowled at the typewritten reports, lingered, in passing, upon the photographs. Then, as if suddenly making up his mind, his finger shot down to push a button, one of the many which stood in a little black box at the end of the desk. Leaning back, he waited. 

It was a short wait. The door directly opposite him opened almost immediately, as a tall awkward-appearing man entered. His face was long, not good-looking, though pleasant. He wore a blue uniform. George Kent, head of the Homicide Squad, was answering the chief's bell. 

Taking the indicated chair, Kent promptly refused the stogie which was pushed in his direction. For a moment there fell a silence. Leaning back in his chair, Rogan studied, as he had so often done before, the man he hoped would be his successor. If he had a favorite on the force, it was the tall, lanky head of his murder squad. There was determination in the rough-hewn face, a hint of driving power. "I am afraid," was Rogan's thought, “George knows little more about this puzzle than I do." With a heave of his heavy body he twisted around in his chair as he spoke. "Have you discovered anything new, George?" He gave the man no time to reply, switching instantly to another question. "You must have checked up on the family by now?"

Kent nodded. The family had certainly been checked up, was his gloomy thought, and the police knew little more than when they had started. "Yes," he confessed, "and we are still working on them." "Found anything?"

Kent's hand searched in the inside pocket of the blue coat to come forth with a black notebook. Slowly he thumbed its pages, then raised his head: "Well, Edward Kimball got home at twelve and went to bed. His wife swears to that."

Rogan suddenly sat upright as an idea come over him. "Twelve? Why he left here about ten. I thought he was going directly home." "He made his dentist come down to the office. Had a tooth which had been bothering him and concluded he wouldn't wait till morning to have it treated." 

Rogan grinned. Kimball had mentioned that he had an aching tooth. So he had made the dentist come down to the office and treat it! He did not blame him; he had done the same thing himself once. He winced as the recollection of that aching tooth came over him, and nodded in sympathy. 

The look caused Kent to put his book back into his pocket and to become confidential. "Chief, I do not think the end of this murder trail will be found in any of the three houses on the estate. The two daughters-in-law-" He shrugged his shoulders. "Well, they are much too pleased to have married into the Kimball family to have any thoughts of committing crime. 

Besides they wouldn't have the nerve. And they were in their houses all the evening. That can be proven, though—”

A startled expression shot over his face. "Come to think of it we have only the word of Kimball's sister that she did not leave her house. That woman has passion, spirit. She did not seem to care very much for her brother 

Henry, either." 

Rogan reflected for a moment. He was willing to agree that the murderer was not one of the family. Not that he had any evidence to that effect; but from long experience he knew it to be very unlikely. Edward Kimball and his wife could be counted out, so could the wife of the murdered man. That left the sister. For a moment he thought of her, high-spirited, self-willed, quick-tempered. 

How she had raved one day when they brought her in the station for speeding! Still, it was one thing to have a temper and another to commit murder. "How about the bows and arrows?" he grunted. "There were four bows and about fifty arrows in the homes of the two brothers-big, long, wooden arrows. No steel ones in either house. Their wives, servants, all said they had never seen a steel arrow. The girl had seven bows, and oh," he waved his hand, "lots of arrows. But no steel ones. Her housekeeper says she never saw a steel one in the house." He paused, to add thoughtfully: "But at the time of the murder there were two persons on the grounds who were capable of shooting a bow." 

Rogan made a gesture of disgust. Bows and arrows. 

Kid things. "Hell!" he growled. "Kent, though Henry Kimball was killed by an arrow as you said, do you think it takes any skill to stand directly back of a man and plug him in the back with an arrow? I could do it myself. Anybody can

pull a bow." 

As he lapsed into silence, Kent grinned. The old man was getting worked up. But he was right. That all the 

Kimballs were used to bows and arrows was of little importance. What was worth considering was the fact that no steel arrows had been found in the houses, that no one, servants or others, had even seen one there. He volunteered some information. "The people at the sporting goods store say they have never carried steel arrows or ever had requests for them. 

They must be something new-not used very much."

Rogan nodded and produced the information that it was only what he had expected. But his thoughts had passed on to something else. "How about that college professor-what was his name -Oxford?" he asked. "Did you get hold of him?" 

Kent nodded. The assistant professor of English had taken the midnight train to New York. The words caused 

Rogan to start. "He did! If that's true, why it lets him out. For if there is one thing certain, it's that Henry Kimball never left his house until after twelve. And Kimball's grounds are at least seven miles from the station." He paused, as a suspicious thought swept over his face. "But if he left for 

New York at midnight why in the devil did that woman tell us that yarn she made up? Why was she afraid we might suspect him?" "I don't think she knew just what time he was going to 

New York," was Kent's reply. "Oh, she knew he was going, but she had an idea it was on the morning train. 

She admitted to me that she knew he was going to the

city for a day or two. I made her wire him to come back at once. There is no doubt he took the midnight train." 

Rogan shrugged his shoulders. "Well, if that's true, our only real suspect is out." "Our only real suspect!" Kent retorted. "In the name of heaven, Rogan, who are the others?" 

The chief threw up his hands. "None, George, none," he admitted. "I was just talking. I never really thought that the professor would turn into a real suspect. But God knows, George, we have a lot of things to discover."

He began to check off on his fingers. "First, the motive. Lots of persons are glad today that

Henry Kimball is dead. But that hardly means they would kill him. Still, among all the people who hated that family, there must be a lurking motive for murder. And," he paused to pull down the second finger, "why use an arrow, when a gun would have been much simpler?" The third finger went down. "And how can we discover who it was that called Kimball and brought him out to meet his death?" "Chief," came Kent's interruption, "have you thought that Kimball must have known the person pretty well who called him? To be called out of your house at midnight and go over that dark lawn because of a phone call means but one thing. He knew who called him."

The look he received was a mixture of disgust and reproach. "George," came the sarcastic voice, "I am not a fool. 

Of course I thought of it. And I thought of something else. Something about that phone message bothered me.

The person called and promised to explain what the calls to his father had meant. Now that means that somebody outside of the family knew about the calls. And I would like "

The sentence was never completed. Breaking in on his words came the shrill ringing of the telephone on the desk. 

Slowly, almost reluctantly, Rogan's hand reached out, and, picking up the receiver, he barked out, "Hullo." Then he suddenly sat upright, and Kent saw the red face grow tense. The conversation was difficult for the head of the homicide squad to follow. There came disconnected words, phrases which were shot violently from the chief's mouth, followed by long doubtful pauses. But it was a disturbing conversation; for, when the receiver had been replaced, Rogan slowly turned round in his chair to throw a perturbed, calculating look at his officer. "That was Manners," came the admission. The tone was perplexed, that of a man who had committed himself to a course of action which he felt to be doubtful. "What do you think he wants me to do?" He did not wait for the reply. "Wants an autopsy on old man Kimball.” 

There was only one thing to say and Kent said it: "Why?" 

Rogan threw out his hands. "God knows just what is in his mind. He's hinting we may be up against another murder," he said. "George Kimball's?"

The gray eyes met Kent's. They had become cold, remote. "He suggested Edward Kimball."

In the silence which followed Rogan's hand began to

creep toward the buttons in his black box. Manners' suggestion had shocked him. Yet as his hand stole over the desk he knew that in his heart was a feeling of relief. He had been uneasy all afternoon. The recollection of the warning voice which for ten days had called him to the telephone had never left him. He had tried to put the thought aside, but had found it impossible. He had nothing to base his hunch upon, yet he felt that there was some connection between the telephone calls that had come to him and the deaths in the Kimball family. With this sinister feeling growing upon him, his finger plunged down upon the button.

Kent wished to speak; a hundred questions were in his mind; but he decided it was no time for words. Rogan's tense, set face, with the lips snapped into a tight line, was ample warning that he must not disturb him. The implication of the chief's dry voice was that he was in no humor to discuss theories. After all, Kent decided, it was his paramount duty to take orders-and in a moment one came. Rogan had twisted around in his chair. "Get hold of Stone at once," came the sharp command. "Tell him to get to my office as quick as he can." He turned to snap off the receiver. Manners also must get down to the office at once.

It was almost six when the five men met in the chief's room. Manners was the last to enter, and he received a reproachful look from Rogan as he slowly walked across the floor. "He might have hurried," was the chief's thought, "though I know from long experience that nothing can hurry him. He looks as if he had just come out of a bandbox. That gray suit set him back a good penny-and that

tie. I wish I knew how much he pays for his ties." But as 

Manners sank down in the chair nearest the desk, Rogan did not hint his thoughts. Instead he said: "Suppose you tell us now what you said to me over the telephone." 

Manners nodded. Before he spoke he opened a gold case and lighted a Turkish cigarette. His grave glance swept the room, noting each of its occupants in turn. Bell was slumped down far into his chair, a weary smile playing upon his thin lips. Close beside him sat the police commissioner, gravely studying the floor. Kent seemed a little nervous, while Rogan was fidgeting about in his chair. It was evident the chief had not told them why he had brought them together. "It's simply this," Manners said. "I told Tim he'd better have an autopsy at once-on George Kimball." 

Bell straightened up to a startled attention. There came a swift glance from his dark eyes, as if he wished to make sure that Manners was serious. Stone's glance swept up from the floor, to rest for a second upon the professor's calm face. It was he who spoke. "That's a rather startling suggestion, Harley," he said. 

The professor nodded. "In a sense," he conceded. "But remember; for the last ten days Rogan has been warned that something was going to happen. Then Kimball died. Four days before his death, he also was warned over the telephone of his own death. And he died. Next, his son Edward received a telephone call and was told that death was waiting for him. 

Under the circumstances I think there should be an autopsy. In a sense it may get us nowhere; then again it may enable us to see what we are up against.”

"Up against?" asked Stone, as his eyebrows rose. "Yes. I have been doing some thinking. One thing we must face. Someone is trying to exterminate the entire 

Kimball family. I may be wrong-I may be terribly wrong; but I have been wondering." He paused to light a cigarette. 

It was too much for Rogan. "Been wondering what?" he growled. "Wondering if we are not up against a well-planned circle of murders. First Kimball, then-" He hesitated, slowly shaking his head. "If my theory is correct, Edward should have been the second one to die. But instead it was his brother." "Harley," came Bell's drawling voice, "you know of course that if someone very neatly put old George Kimball out of the way, they had to use poison. And we discussed that fact-you and I. We know of no poison which would produce the symptoms George Kimball's illness produced. You know that."

Manners agreed, but he added that, under the circumstances, it seemed very necessary to have an autopsy. For their own peace of mind they should make very certain of the cause of George Kimball's death. It might have been a natural one; then again-he shrugged his shoulders-it might have been something quite the opposite.

The police commissioner produced from his pocket a well-worn pipe, taking his time in lighting it. When the smoke was curling into the air, he spoke, his voice slow and deliberate: "We'd best be very certain what we are seeking before we order that autopsy. Under the law, Rogan has a right

to have one if he wishes. I think the law mentions 'suspicious circumstances,' and so forth. At any rate Rogan can order an autopsy and no one can prevent his having one. True, he may run into trouble. Parrish will rave round. But it's my idea the family is stunned by the two deaths. Only, I warn you, we want to be very certain about our grounds for issuing the order." 

It was Manners who replied. The police commissioner was reminded of the telephone calls, of Henry Kimball's bizarre death, following a telephone call. It was admitted that no very definite facts could be brought forth. His friend's grave, earnest tone caused Rogan to look at him in amazement. As a rule he was the one who worked on "hunches"; now his learned friend was acting on his emotions. The sight startled the chief. It also filled him with foreboding. If Manners felt like that, there must be some reason for his fears. For that matter he was uneasy himself.

The debate which followed was a heated one. Bell took little part, though once in a while he contributed an ironic remark. He declared himself perfectly willing to hold an autopsy, though he told them he would like to have some idea for what he was to look. Kent said nothing. Stone and

Manners always kept him silent, for he stood a little in awe of them both. In the end, Stone threw out his hands in a defeated gesture. "All right," he said. "Have an autopsy. I will back up the chief."

At the words, Bell rose languidly to his feet. Leaning against the desk, he slowly shook his head. "It's all right with me," he said. "There is only one

thing I want. Let Manners get Hoff down here at nine.

He knows a thousand more facts about poisons than I ever heard of. And you can just bet if anything's wrong, it's poison."

Manners left the police station after making the promise that he would see Hoff, the famous toxicologist of the University and his own warm friend, and persuade him to be at the station by nine. As he stepped into his car and started up the street, he knew that the famous scientist would need little persuasion. The problem would be one to intrigue him. The quest of what might lie behind this crime would fill his heart with delight.

Reaching the University campus he drove down a gravel walk to come to a halt before a two-story brick building. It was the oldest building on the campus. The bricks were stained by the ravages of years; ivy struggled to gain a foothold on the walls. The University was closed, but he knew Hoff had not left the city. On the second floor he would find him; no doubt hard at work upon some obscure problem. Climbing out of the car, he mounted the three worn granite steps, then ascended the stairway to the second floor.

He was in a long hallway. To his right it ran down to the end of the building; but at the left, only a few feet away, was an open door. Passing through this, he came into a deserted chemical laboratory, whose long tables and racks were filled with apparatus. At the end of this room was a glass door, partly open, through which filtered a faint haze of tobacco. It was Hoff's own office, and the smoke told Manners that the toxicologist was in. Hurrying past the empty tables, he threw the glass door ajar.

The room he was looking into was small, but every inch of its floor and wall space was occupied. Magazines and books overflowed from the bookcases, spilling over to the floor where they were pilled upon one another in a disordered mass. Between two windows was a cluttered desk; and, behind the desk, his feet almost at the level of his head, smoking a corncob pipe, sat a slight little figure. As 

Manners entered the room the man turned to throw an inquiring glance toward the door.

Hoff was without doubt the most famous member of the faculty. As a toxicologist his name was known throughout the world. An odd figure in appearance, with his short, unkempt hair, his thick, heavy glasses; yet the large rounded skull hinted at the man's intellect. A nervous individual-gruff, hasty in speech and action-he cared nothing for the social life of the college and little for his associates, though for some reason he had an intense liking for Manners. At the sight of his visitor he dropped his feet to the floor and rose from the chair. "Well, well, Harley," came the high voice. "It's a long time since I saw you. Find a chair and sit down. Somewhere in this place is some liquor worth drinking. We have a drink; then we talk. Yes?" 

It never paid to disagree with Hoff. Manners brushed a few magazines off a chair and brought it close to the windows. He watched the little figure peer into corners, throwing books aside, and finally saw him discover a bottle under an old coat. With a cry of triumph Hoff rushed into the laboratory. In a second there came the sound of running water. When he returned he carried two glassesglasses to which tiny drops of water still clung.

As a drink, the rye which Hoff poured into the glasses left a good deal to be desired, but Manners downed the fiery liquid. Then, placing his glass upon the table, he turned to meet the sharp gaze of his friend. For a moment the eyes of the two men met and held. Hoff's high voice came first: "Something troubling you, Harley. This is no social call. Tell me what it is."

As Manners began to speak, the toxicologist pushed aside the magazines from the corner of his desk and seated himself there. The bright eyes sparkled behind the thick glasses; the large head was held quizzically on one side. But he did not speak again, while carefully and thoughtfully Manners went through his story, beginning with the telephone calls, ending with his idea that an autopsy was needed. When he had finished, Hoff's comment was brief. "You're right, Harley," he agreed. "You should have an autopsy." 

Manners gravely nodded. "The trouble is, Hoff," he explained, "I know of no poison which will produce the symptoms attendant upon George Kimball's death. He was slightly ill for a few hours, then recovered until a brief time before death.” 

Hoff's eyes gleamed behind his glasses. His voice was dry. "You are a very bright and distinguished man, Harley.

You know a great deal outside your own field, which is why people act as they do, why crime takes place. I know a lot about poisons; so do you; but you don't know all.

There are poisons which may act as you describe."

Manners turned and looked at his friend. The little figure was seated on the end of the cluttered desk. The corncob pipe was being relighted and the operation was serious and deliberate. When the pipe was going, Hoff slid off the desk and walked over to the window. For a moment he stood silent, looking across the campus. Then he turned. "You would know the older poisons, the ones found in

Webster's and other books. But you know little of the newer ones, the terrible death-dealing agents we are discovering every day. You know nothing of the agents of destruction which we can produce from the decomposition of foods and animal matter, know little of certain rare vegetable poisons.' 

"Hoff," broke in Manners, "tell me. Do you know any poison which would produce the symptoms present in 

George Kimball's death?"

For a second the toxicologist gravely studied his friend. 

His voice was cautious as he replied. "Harley, I will not be certain," he said shrugging his shoulders. "No man of science should jump at conclusions.

I know of several poisons which might have been used, but they are very difficult to recognize." Again he paused; then suddenly he started for his desk. "You run along, Harley," he commanded. "I will be very glad to aid Bell tonight at nine. Tell him we try to find something. Run along."

It was dismissal, Manners knew that. With a good-by, he left the office, walked through the silent laboratory and then downstairs. As he climbed into his car he was thoughtful. There was no better toxicologist in the

country than Hoff. He had been much impressed by the manner of Kimball's death, and he knew poisons. 

Manners drove home, for his housekeeper had asked if he would return for dinner and he had assented. It was not ready when he entered the house, however, though the clock stood almost at seven. Picking up a book he sat down by his desk and attempted to read, but the book failed to hold him. He placed it resignedly upon the desk and allowed his eyes to travel over his library. Upon the row of books which composed his crime collection his glance came to a halt. He had read them all—many of them again and again. Yet in none of these thousands of volumes was there any hint of what he now had in mind. 

That crime repeated itself was a maxim, but as he went into the dining room he wondered if it was unfailingly true.

The dinner he sat down to was a simple one, cooked with understanding and delicacy. The wine was from his own cellar, one of hundreds of bottles chosen by his father.

It was a silent meal, with nothing to disturb him until the coffee was reached; then the telephone out in the hall rang. The housekeeper was out in the kitchen. Manners rose to answer it. He glanced at his watch as he picked up the receiver, it was just eight-thirty.

The voice which came floating over the wire was low; the words were spoken in a cold, even tone. A disguised voice, was Manners' immediate thought. But the message caused him to grip the receiver until his hand was white. "Professor Manners," came the cold, even voice, “if I were you I would not bother about the Kimballs. If they all die it is of no importance. It is far better that you show

no interest in them."

The voice ceased. As it did so, Manners flung a word into the receiver. It was useless. There came no reply. The voice was gone.

CHAPTER IX 

A SINGLE high-powered light bulb glowed in Rogan's office. It hung directly above his desk, making the room as light as day. Clearly illuminated was the face of the clock upon the wall-a clock whose hands pointed directly to midnight. The light fell upon three men seated around the chief's desk, tired men, whose glances had again and again turned to the dragging hands of the clock. 

Since ten, Stone and Manners had been waiting with the chief, waiting anxiously for some word from Bell.

They had talked during the first hour, a desultory, wandering conversation which had been little more than a smoke screen to hide their thoughts. All were nervous; and, because they were tired, their dispositions were not of the best. Manners' story of his telephone call, of the low, cold voice warning him to have nothing to do with the Kimball affair, had been discussed from every angle. His remark that he had made no effort to trace the call brought a nod of approval from the chief. It would have been useless, as it was agreed the call had without a doubt come from a public pay station. But the relating of the incident had brought a nervous tension into the room. 

Because it was a warm evening Rogan had removed his coat. He was slumped far down in his chair, the usual stogie clenched between his lips. The red face showed signs of needing a shave; his hair looked as if it had not been combed for days. Across from him, a well-worn pipe 129

between his lips, sat the police commissioner, his white linen suit as well creased as if it had just been donned. 

Manners, stretched out in his chair, had not taken off his coat, in spite of the stuffiness of the tobacco-filled room. 

No one had spoke for many moments, mostly because every subject of conversation had been exhausted. 

The chief was nervous. He had expected the Kimballs to object when he ordered an autopsy. To his surprise there had been but one short protest. He could not account for this on the grounds that they were still overcome by the tragedy which had visited them. Edward 

Kimball might very well have objected—but he had been feeling a little indisposed; no doubt the reaction from the death of his father and that of his brother. Rogan recalled with pride that he had gained the family's consent without revealing the real purpose of the autopsy. He had made them think it was the regular procedure after a tragic and violent death. Now, as he glanced over the smokefilled room, he was wondering if he had been wise in his recent action.

A sound in the hall caused the three men to stiffen as they turned and looked toward the door. The knob slowly turned; the door was pushed open. Two men entered the room, weary men, walking slowly and with very grave faces. Bell was in the lead, his thin face drawn and serious, his hair unkempt. Behind him came the slight little figure of the toxicologist. Through his heavy glasses they could see the glitter of his sharp eyes. They were very tired men, their weariness showing in every gesture as they walked slowly over to the desk. 

Pushing aside a mass of papers to seat himself on a

corner of the desk, Bell threw a long, cold, calculating look at the three occupants of the room. The gesture with which he drew forth a battered package of cigarettes was that of a man who had ceased to care what happened. Very deliberately the cigarette was lighted; then, with a shrug of his shoulders, he turned to the chief. "It's poison, Rogan," was the short statement.

The chief's expression did not change. Now that the sinister words had been uttered, he felt calmer. No more wondering, no more doubts lay ahead. Poison. As he paused over the thought, his lips grew stern. Instead of one murder to solve, he had two. "What kind of poison?" he half whispered. "Ricin," was the drawled reply. 

Rogan threw his police physician a disgusted look.

Snatching his stogie from between his lips he barked out an anxious question: "And what the devil might that be?” 

Bell waved a hand toward the toxicologist. "Better let Hoff give you the low-down." "Ricin," began the latter's slow voice, "is an intensely poisonous protein or albumin found in the seeds of the 

Ricinus Communis, along with castor oil, which last, however, does not in itself contain the principle."

The look with which Hoff was favored was not one of intense admiration. Rogan's eyes had opened wide as the high voice snapped out the technical terms; then in desperation he slammed his hand down upon the table. "For God's sake, Hoff, tell me what you are talking about and do it in words I can understand. What's all this stuff about castor oil and-what in hell did you call it?"

"Ricin. You find the protein-the poison-in the seeds from which the castor oil is made. It is a very powerful poison. Persons have been killed from eating castor-oil seeds. Further, though the seeds are rather common in some countries, the poison we are speaking about-ricinis rather difficult to secure and little is known about it." 

So someone had poisoned Kimball. Manners had not been startled when the sinister words fell from Bell's lips.

The toxic agent had been a new one to him. He had the dim idea that somewhere he had heard of it; yet he realized it was no common poison. A vegetable poison, one which without doubt was a very powerful one. He was about to ask how it had been administered when Rogan voiced the identical question. "When was it given to him and how?"

Bell shrugged his shoulders and shot an amused glance at Hoff. The toxicologist did not reply for a moment. 

Standing across the room was an empty chair. He walked over and brought it close to the desk. As he did so, he in turn shrugged his shoulders and answered: "That's the trouble, Rogan; we don't know." "You don't know? You must know how it was given. 

How much did he have to drink, or is it eat?” "But he drank, or ate, as you put it, nothing. You see, ricin is contained in the castor seed, and though, if one ate enough of them it would produce death, few cases have been known of poisoning from eating the seeds. 

They are so obnoxious to the taste that only a very young child would ever eat them. But if the protein be extracted from the seed, and, say, placed directly in the blood stream or tissue fluids of the body, it is one of the

most powerful poisons known. I refer, of course, to injections of a hypodermic, subcutaneous or intravenous nature. And there is another remarkable thing about ricin. If it is introduced into the blood stream or tissue fluids, death results from it only after a lapse of from two to four days, and there are barely any symptoms until the end. At first a slight loss of appetite would be evidenced, but little else—nothing in fact, until there comes the sudden collapse just before death. The action is partly local-gastro-enteritis; partly central-respiratory and vasomotor centers."

There were portions of what Hoff said that the chief did not comprehend at all. Only one thing was clear in his mind. The crime was murder. Yet, even as he thought this, a momentary ray of hope came. "Could it have been suicide?" 

Bell gave him a disgusted look. "It could have been. Just one chance in a million. Can you think of George Kimball committing suicide? And listen, Rogan, though we know what killed him-luckily the post-mortem findings of ricin are very characteristic —we have not as yet been able to discover how it was introduced into his body. We know what killed him, know the agent which entered his blood stream or tissue fluids. 

But how, or where, it entered, we do not know." 

Rogan shook his head. It was getting worse-more and more complicated. They knew what had killed the man, knew even something of the manner in which it had been accomplished; and then they confessed they had been unable to discover the exact means by which the poison had been introduced into the man's body. It all seemed

a mass of confusion to him. He stole a look at Hoff. The slight figure was sitting on the edge of his chair, his intent eyes glancing soberly over the room. Rogan had the greatest respect for the toxicologist. He took Manners' word for it that he was one of the most famous men in his line in the world. And his specialty did happen to be poison. Yet, as he sat there, listening to Bell saying that they did not know how the poison was given, Rogan stifled an oath. Did Hoff know? As if reading his mind, the toxicologist spoke. "Mr. Rogan, we are not wizards," he said. "We have to work scientifically. Now, I can assure you that Kimball was poisoned, and that ricin was the poison used. 

It's rare, difficult to secure, though any good chemist could prepare it. You cannot, of course, walk into a drug store and buy it. Also, we know it had to be directly introduced into the body. Where, we have been unable, as yet, to discover." He rose to his feet. "We are going back now to make further search. After all, we have done our part. All other questions are up to you."

There was a quizzical smile upon his lips as he started across the floor. Wearily Bell slid off the desk to follow.

Just as they reached the door, Manners stopped them both with a question. "Hoff, it could have been given with a hypodermic could it not? A doctor might—” 

Hoff's voice was that of a teacher instructing a child.

Slowly he repeated his previous statements, admitted that a doctor could have given the poison with a needle, added that it was a poison that not one doctor in a thousand would know anything about or be able to procure. That

it could be suicide was also admitted—that is, if they could imagine George Kimball killing himself or having knowledge of such a poison. Then, with his hand on the door knob, Hoff uttered his final words: "All you asked me to do was to discover what had caused Kimball's death. We did so. It's up to the police to make up their minds whether it is suicide or murder, though my advice would be that you decide at once on murder. It will save you a lot of time." 

There came the slam of the door. The sound echoed through the room. As it died away, the silence was broken by a sudden and violent oath from Rogan. Pushing back his chair he rose to his feet. The sturdy figure stood close to the desk, the gray eyes surveying gravely his two friends. He was tired; there were tired circles under his eyes. And he was anxious. The thick lips had set in a straight line. His look was itself a question which Stone answered. "He is right, Tim. It's murder we are up against. We know that Kimball would never commit suicide. And remember those telephone calls." 

Remember them! He had not been able to forget them.

He recalled the low, even voice, which for ten evenings had spoken to him over the wire. Ten calls. At the thought which swept over him he slid once more down in his chair. "If those calls have anything to do with this affair, then there is one thing certain. It's all been very skillfully planned, well thought out. But what is it Bell said that the poison would kill in either two or four days?"

It was Manners who answered, reminding him that they had been told that the length of time required for

death depended upon the strength of the poison. It was from two to four days. The answer caused the chief to shake his head; he was more perplexed than ever. Ten days before Kimball's death, his first telephone call had come. That was six days before the poison could have been administered. The voice on the wire had known that 

Kimball would die. The murderer himself had talked to him. The crime had been premeditated. He put the thoughts into words.

Both Manners and Stone nodded their prompt agreement. It was Manners' thoughtful voice which said: "It seems very reasonable, Tim. You are up against a well-planned crime; though there is one thing which still puzzles me."

Stone immediately wanted further information. The police commissioner was in a quandary. He had backed 

Rogan's suggestion for an autopsy while deep in his mind he had thought the request absurd. But the old man was retiring in five weeks, and any request he might make should be granted. This, without a doubt, would be his last case-and the lawyer had suddenly decided that it was going to be the most important case in Rogan's long career. Not only the most important, but also the most perplexing. For Harley Manners, Stone had the greatest admiration. What went on in that well-shaped head he had often thought he would like to know. And Manners had solved the last three cases which had come into the office. Any thought Manners had, Stone desired to know. "I mean this, Stone," came the slow reply. "I hate theories, for mostly they turn out to be absurd. But somehow I have an idea we must build up one now. Let us

suppose someone is trying to kill off the Kimballs, someone whose mind is deranged-for he would never call 

Rogan or warn Kimball if he were normal. Say he used poison and killed the elder Kimball, then skipped Edward, and killed his brother Henry-not with poison, but with an arrow, an absurd and rarely used weapon. Why?" 

As no one spoke, he answered his own question. "Well, why Henry was killed last night we do not know, but we may guess why an arrow was used instead of poison. The murderer knew that he would have no opportunity to administer ricin to Henry, so he picked out another weapon-an arrow." 

Rogan stirred uneasily in his chair. His lips twitched. as he spoke. "Maybe you are right, Harley. But think of it; we have no motive, no clues, no evidence. In all my experience I never saw a set-up like this." "Think of all the people who have been injured by the Kimballs," was Stone's suggestion; "those actually injured, I mean, those who hate and envy the richest family of the city. Manners will tell you, it's his specialty, that there must be hundreds of minds, minds a little disordered, who hate him, hate his family. Somewhere among them lies the individual who planned these two murders." "The same individual behind them both?" shot out the chief. 

Stone nodded. A discussion followed. It was agreed that the family itself must be carefully investigated, though all expressed the opinion that the murderer was not of the family. Stone gave a lengthy dessertation upon the money Kimball had left. Twelve millions in all, dis-

tributed and cared for by a trust fund. Not one of the family would suffer, and none stood to gain much if any other was cut off. Kimball had ordered that, in the event of the death of either of his sons, his share should be paid to the wife. In no event would any member of the family benefit by the death of another member. "Suppose they were all swept away but one?" Manners said soberly. 

Stone threw him an amazed look. "Hell! Harley, it would mean the killing of at least five people. It would also mean that the criminal Rogan must capture is one of the family. You don't believe that."

The professor did not believe that. He said so, soberly and at length. Reminding the two men that ricin was a difficult poison to procure, he added that he doubted if any of the Kimball family had even heard about it. But he argued that there seemed little doubt someone was trying to wipe out the family. By "the family" he meant the father, the two sons and perhaps the daughter. Then he made a suggestion. "Rogan, if our theory is right," he said, "it is very necessary that you take every precaution to guard Edward. Logically he should be the one the murderer next attacks. Someone should be on the estate, and Kimball should be warned to be very careful of what he doeswarned of the poison, and of its method of invasion through the blood stream." 

The chief gave a grunt, then growled a protest. "But there is someone on the grounds. I will of course send 

Kent himself up at once to break the news of what the autopsy revealed. You have an idea, then, that the poison

will be used again?"

Manners nodded. His own thoughts were a tangle of disconnected theories. The poison used had surprised him.

It was rare, the sort of thing which would be known only to an expert; this in itself presented a problem. And the psychological end of the affair was even more serious. He pictured some individual, intelligent, educated, whose mind had gone off on an emotional tangent; one who hated the Kimball family, who thought he was doing a service to the world in getting rid of them. But, as to the source of the individual's obsession, he could not name that. Had he been able to do so, he knew that the discovery of the criminal would be greatly simplified. But the poison would be used again, he felt very positive. He said as much. 

His conclusion caused Rogan to scowl. For the first time in his career he was up against a crime in which there was not a clue; what was worse, he found himself incapable of even guessing at a motive. And one needed a motive. It was from the latter one was able to build up theories, set a net for the individual who might be a suspect. But in this case there were no clues, no motive. All the police knew was that two murders had been committed.

Stone had at last risen to his feet. It was one o'clock in the morning and he had to be in court by ten. He sympathized with the troubled police chief, but there was nothing he could do. When he pronounced it time to go, Manners also rose, for there was nothing he could do. 

Like Stone, he realized that only detailed work was ahead, though morning might bring some new light on the

problems. He offered to drive Rogan home, but, with a gloomy sigh, the chief remarked that he still had a little work to do. At that both men said good-night and walked together out to the curb.

Stone's car was close to Manners' and neither man spent any time in saying good-night. As the professor climbed into his seat, he threw a look up the street. State 

Street was a long, lighted tunnel, with the office buildings vanishing into shadows above the bright lights. Few people could be seen, for the hour was late. The police station was mostly dark, though a few lighted windows remained. One of the windows held his glance, the bright square of flame which told that Rogan was still at his desk.

He had several miles to drive, and, because he was deep in thought, he traveled slowly. There was no traffic and the signal lights had been turned off. As he swung past the University, he saw a light in Hoff's laboratory, and realized the toxicologist was still at work. He would be, he thought; he neither ate nor slept when confronted with a problem. Then, as the dark campus was left behind, and the cheap houses of the outskirts of the city flashed by, he came, in his mind, to one decision.

Clues in the ordinary sense of the word would never solve this mystery. In the first place there were none; in the second, it was a psychological crime. He would classify it as a case which could be solved only by discovering the type of individual capable of plotting such a crime. 

Also, the murderer would prove to be an individual whose intelligence was equal to the deed but whose emotional balance had smashed. That the motive for the crime was

revenge, he was beginning to have little doubt.

The road leading up to his estate was reached at last, and he started up the steep, curving ascent. In the onrushing flame of his headlights, the steep ditches and high banks were thrown into high relief. Half way up the hill, there was a cleared space where a cold spring trickled through the rocks. As he passed this he noticed a car parked beside the clearing. Though there was nothing out of the way in this—almost any night a car could be found there he noted it in passing. 

His house was a dark, silent outline as he swung between the stone pillars of his drive. Not a light could be seen anywhere; nor did his dog come leaping across the grass to meet him. Once in a while the housekeeper left the big animal in his library when she returned to her home at night. As he stopped the car before the garage he concluded that she had done so tonight.

The car slid over the concrete floor to take its place beside the two other machines. Closing the garage he walked slowly toward the house. The lawn was dark, though the many stars in the sky gave some light. The trees were a blurred outline, and far below him he could see the twinkling lights of the city. It was very still, for there was no breeze and even the trees were not whispering. 

Climbing the steps of the veranda, he paused before the large door of the house as his hand fumbled for the switch. He had always hated trying to find a keyhole in the darkness and had placed a light switch on the side of the door. For a second his hand searched the woodwork, then, finding the switch, he pulled it down. As the porch

sprang into light, he heard a sudden noise. 

It was not much of a noise, a slight sound, as if someone might be behind him-someone down on the lawn. 

Something-some instinct-caused him to throw himself toward the veranda floor. As he did so, there came a twang, the odd whistling of an object passing through the air-came in another instant a sound, a heavy thud upon his front door. 

He raised an astonished head. There, in the white paneled door, in line with the spot where he had stood an instant ago, was a long, gleaming object. He needed but a glance to identify it. Even yet the deadly arrow was quivering from its impact against the wooden door.

He did not stop to think. Turning, he hurled himself down the steps, away from the brightness of the porch.

Half stumbling, half falling, he came up against the side of the house, ruining, he knew, his flower beds. Then he paused a moment to get his bearings.

The porch above him was as bright as day. One object there held his glance, the long arrow trembling in the door. The lawn was black, filled with deep shadows; the trees were splotches of gloom. Nothing could be seen there, yet he knew with certainty that, as he had stood with his back to the lawn, his figure outlined against the white door, someone had tried to kill him with an arrow. 

Incredible as it was, the fact remained.

For a moment he stood peering over the dark lawn. He had little to fear. Crouched against the side of the house he was in darkness. Somewhere out on that lawn was the individual who had tried to kill him. He would have liked to puzzle out the why of it, but it was no time now. Only

a moment had been lost, from the time the arrow struck the door, until he flung himself off the steps. The fugitive had not had time to get very far. As he thought this, a sound again crept over the lawn. It was the faint tingling of a wire-and he knew what it meant.

At the farther end of his lawn he had a small vegetable garden. It had proved to be the spot the dog loved best. 

To keep him out of it, Manners had enclosed it with wire. 

Someone creeping over the lawn had just fallen against the wires and they had given forth the warning. Then he thought of the car he had seen on the hillside, a lonely car, seemingly unoccupied, parked in a secluded portion of the hill. Could it be that his assailant was making for the car?

No sooner did the possibility occur to him than he started to move. Keeping close to the house, protected by its dark shadow, he crept slowly down its length. He had heard the sound of someone falling against the wires of his enclosed garden. It meant that the intruder was at the lower end of the lawn. There was a wall to climb over; then came a meadow to cross; from there it would be an easy thing to reach the road. As he thought this, he decided that pursuit was useless. In the darkness he would be unable either to see or to guess the way his assailant had gone. But there was one thing he could do.

He sprang out from the shadow of the house. With head bent low, he started to run to the garage. Reaching it, he fumbled for a moment with its lock, snapped it open and flung back the doors. It took but a second to spring into his roadster. The ignition was snapped on, a foot went slamming on the starter, and the powerful engine leaped

into life. Backing out of the garage, he made a wide circle as he started for the road. In a moment he was sliding between the two stone pillars which marked his driveway. 

The car's headlights were shafts of gleaming flame; they made of the road a smooth white line, a tunnel of light flanked on each side by darkness. Around the first curve he swept, then the second, and there, far below him, he saw the fast disappearing taillight of a car, a car whose speed was increasing every moment. As he saw it, his foot lifted. There was little use in trying to follow that machine. It was almost a half mile below him now, and the hill was a series of dangerous curves. Not only that, but there were countless little roads down which the machine could escape; roads, any one of which would allow the driver of the car to make his getaway. 

Manners drove almost a thousand yards before he found a place where he could turn. Slowly, then, the long roadster retraced the route back to the garage. More slowly did the professor climb out of the car, lock the door and walk to the veranda. Here he stood for several moments staring at the door. As a door, it was an artistic thing, the paint a gleaming white, the design the finest colonial; but his eyes noted none of these details. Instead they rested upon the long steel shaft which was affixed to the door-the arrow which he had escaped.

So far as he could make out upon examination, it was similar to the one found in Henry Kimball's body, a thin shaft of steel, with bright feathers on the end closest to him. His face was grave, his thoughts troubled. If he had not fallen to the floor, that arrow would have struck him in the back-and it would have killed him. Thinking of

this, he reached forth and, with a jerk, pulled the triangular point from the door. Then he inserted his key and passed within.

As he turned on the lights in the library, the dog in a leisurely fashion rose from the fireplace and ambled over to thrust his cold nose in his master's hand. As he glanced at the huge animal, Manners wished he might have been out of doors instead of locked up in the library when he had returned home. Had Satan been on the lawn, his unknown assailant would never have reached his car. With this thought in mind he placed the arrow on the long desk and went out to the ice-box. When he returned, a bottle was in one hand and in his pocket a glass. 

The drink he poured was large, and it was followed by another. He had slipped down in the easy chair, but his eyes kept going over to the desk. Upon its rosewood surface lay a glistening steel shaft. He studied the pointed end, then, following its length, observed the bright feathers. As he looked a little shiver crept over him. It was a formidable weapon-this weapon someone had chosen for his death. But why? 

Slowly his hand reached forth for the squatty bottle.

Carefully the yellow liquid was poured into the tall glass. 

As he raised the drink to his lips, there was but one thought in his mind. The attack upon him had come because of the Kimball affair. Someone had thought it necessary to remove him from the world. And he knew that there could be only one reason.

Someone was afraid, afraid of something he thought Manners knew-something that was so necessary to keep secret that the murderer of Henry Kimball had been

forced to seek a new victim-himself. This he knew with certainty, but there was now a second query in his mind. 

What was it he was supposed to know? What fact had come to his knowledge so dangerous that the criminal thought it necessary to kill him? He drained his Scotch.

If he could only answer that- But as he placed the glass on the desk and rose to go to his bedroom, he acknowledged that he could not. He only knew that someone was very much afraid of him. He wished he knew why-and what it was he was supposed to know.

CHAPTER X 

SILENCE lay over Harley Manners' lawn. Under the largest elm tree was a small table, its surface littered with sheets of yellow paper. Close beside the table was a garden chair, and beside it lay a huge brown dog. From time to time the animal would lift his large head to throw an inquiring look at his master-a master sitting with outstretched legs and eyes fixed upon a white cloud, the only cloud in a blue sky. It was quiet; no birds were singing, and the road which ran past the house was so secluded that cars were a novelty. 

Shortly after lunch Manners retired to his favorite spot, a chair and a table under his most venerable tree. He had done so with a little misgiving. Perhaps he ought to have gone to the city to discover what the police had picked up during the last few hours. He judged, however, that they could not have found anything of importance. Had they done so, his telephone would have rung long ago. He had taken the opportunity to spend the time in seclusion, seated beside the little table upon whose surface, as the afternoon lengthened, there grew an increasing pile of notes.

There were many points in the case that he wished to settle for himself. The elder Kimball had been poisoned. 

Poison was a favorite weapon in murder cases, though used far more by women than by men. Poison presupposed a long, skillfully premeditated plot. It was a signboard 147

pointing not to a passional crime but to one which had been long thought out. On the other hand, though poison was a woman's weapon, as a rule a woman's choice fell upon the more easily procured poisons; arsenic, strychnine and the like. The poison used to kill Kimball was very rare, one of which few individuals had ever heard. 

Ricin, injected either into the blood stream or through the tissues of the body. Yet Hoff had been unable to discover in what precise manner the poison had been administered. This seemed odd. Few toxicologists were as skillful as Hoff, yet, in spite of that, this point eluded him. It was this fact, coupled with his knowledge that ricin was known only to chemical experts and not to so many of these, that brought Manners to two conclusions: The crime had been long planned—and the murderer was a man. There was not a chance in a million a woman would know anything about ricin or that she would be able to secure it.

Many notes had been jotted down during the afternoon-all the random, flashing thoughts which came to him. He had even tried to arrange them in some order. 

Always, in a murder case, there were certain things to be answered. First of all one must be able to tell where the crime was committed. The answer to this they possessed, in the case of Henry Kimball. Secondly, one must be able to answer how it was committed. This could be answered in both cases-by poison and by the shooting of an arrow. 

But the third question was the crucial one upon which the solution of all murders depended. Why were these crimes committed? And this question Manners was unable to answer.

He had written down many answers under the one word "Why?" But none of them was satisfactory. The police were positive that no member of the family had any motive for the murders. Just why Rogan was so confident the family could be dismissed, he did not know; yet he himself was willing to accept the belief of the police. With the exception of the daughter, the Kimballs seemed an unimaginative, drab sort of family, who would have neither the will nor the ability to carry through a well-planned crime. Besides, they had no motive. Life in the Kimball houses might not be a thrilling affair, but at least there was no inherent reason for murder.

The daughter- He had tried to be honest with himself as he thought of her. She might possess the intelligence and perhaps the passion out of which crimes were born, but did she have a reason for wishing her father and brother out of the way? True, her life had been strictly controlled. Both her father and her brothers had ordered most of her activities. But to commit two murders-one with poison, the other with a bow and arrow-it seemed hardly credible.

In the years the Kimballs had been building up their fortune, no individual or institution that lay in their path had been spared. They had made many enemies. The unfortunate, the defeated in life might both hate and envy them. In the group would be neurotics, the strong and the weak, the stupid and the intelligent. It was Manners' idea that among this group he would have to hunt for his criminal. He had a well-defined picture of him. 

The criminal would prove to be someone whose single object in life was to injure the Kimball family, destroy it.

There was a disquieting thought which came with this last into his mind. Obviously the unknown person thought that some great wrong had been done to him, and the thought was an obsession which had changed and warped his entire life. Without a doubt this individual lived and breathed with but one object in life-to injure any, and perhaps all, of the Kimballs. And he possessed intelligence.

This last bothered Manners, for he realized that highly intelligent individuals did not commit crime, with one exception-they might do so if suddenly thrown off their emotional balance. He pictured an individual whose emotions and reactions to life were well balanced in every respect but one-the Kimball family-an individual with some knowledge of science, of toxicology. The criminal had to possess that knowledge. Ricin had only a few lines devoted to it in the leading text books. It had been administered so skillfully that Hoff, one of the leading toxicologists of the country, had been unable to discover the precise method. What the police must look for was someone with a scientific education, whose emotions had gone to pieces. And to find such an individual without clues was a task which might prove impossible.

There was another problem to consider: the many telephone calls. That was difficult; there were too many telephone calls. First the ten to Rogan, warning him that something sinister was about to happen. Next, the two calls to George Kimball, bearing the threat of death. The call to Henry promising to explain what the calls to

George Kimball had meant. The call to Edward announcing that he was to die. And, incidentally, there was the

message he himself had received last evening, the threatening call warning him to drop all interest in the Kimballs.

For a moment his eyes wandered over the green lawn, as he watched the sunlight playing upon the colorful gardens. Life was like that, was his irrelevant thought; a tangled mixture of hope and despair, a moment's happiness, years of defeat. Fourteen telephone calls (fifteen, including the one which brought Henry out to his death), and behind them was the entire tragedy of life-hatred and, no doubt, love, revenge and evil deeds. He was convinced that the calls were linked up inextricably with the two murders.

There were some odd circumstances surrounding the telephone messages. Rogan's had been a sinister warning that, after ten days, something would happen. There seemed now little doubt that the suggested event was the death of George Kimball. The poison was one which acted in from two to four days, depending upon the quantity given. This meant that whoever called Rogan night after night not only knew that Kimball was to die, but knew, as well, the exact moment when the poison would be administered. The unknown had hinted at George Kimball's death six days before the poison could possibly have been given.

This was Manners' puzzle. It showed not only design but a cool certainty that nothing would interfere with that design. If there was one thing clear in his mind it was that the murderer's voice had been the one which had come over the wire six days before marking his victim for death; he had boasted of what would happen. This unknown must be someone very close to the family, someone certain of his plan of destruction.

Then there was the call to Edward Kimball—made on the night of his father's death. It stated that in four days he would be dead. Certainty again, a calm knowledge of what was going to happen. Plan, design, clearly hinted at in all the warnings of the unknown voice. No other conclusion could possibly be reached.

More intriguing still was the speculation as to the type of individual who could plot such a series of crimes. The study of the abnormalities of human beings was Manners' life work. He realized completely, that, behind the telephone calls, was an abnormal human being. Of more importance, he realized that it was someone with a fine intelligence, whose mind had become obsessed with a single thought-the death of all the Kimballs. All the Kimballs- 

There was little doubt in his mind that they were facing a circle of deaths. Therein lay the danger. For once in a while a finely trained mind went suddenly to pieces, and it was then that the police found themselves hopelessly at sea.

Ordinary crime, committed by moronic people, they could solve by the usual police methods. But when faced with a cool, trained mind made abnormal by some fancied wrong, they were helpless. And some such individual they were facing now. 

One thing perplexed him above all. If his theories were correct, the second victim should by all odds have been 

Edward. Why was it the murderer had killed the younger son; and why had he varied his weapon? There seemed but one conclusion; Henry had been killed with an arrow because the murderer had found it impossible to give him poison. It had been Manners' idea that the criminal's mind would work in certain well-defined channels. First the

father would be killed, then the elder son, then the second. 

But Henry had been the second victim, and the weapon an arrow. Because there had been no possible way in which the poison could have been given him. Was this the answer?

His thoughts returned again and again to the poison. 

Ricin. It was not well known even to toxicologists, and the average man, the layman, would know nothing about it. 

Its use proved that they were dealing with an individual who possessed a technical knowledge of chemistry. This idea must be followed up. Furthermore, if it was true, it would absolve everyone in the Kimball family. There was scarcely a chance that any one of the family had ever heard of ricin. 

Ricin. Since Hoff had named the poison, Manners had read everything the scientists could bring together upon it. 

One two-thousandth of a grain would kill, if injected into the blood stream. It was rare-so rare that in all medical history there had been less than half a dozen deaths resulting from it, and the majority of these had been among children who had eaten the seed of the castor plant. All 

Manners' chemical journals, his text books, showed only one death from the injection of ricin into the blood stream, or by absorption through the tissues of the body. 

This fact seemed to place it beyond the range of possibility that any member of the family could have killed the elder Kimball.

As for the son, Edward was beyond suspicion, for, though he was one of the members of the family who had practiced archery, even Rogan was positive that he had been in the house that evening from twelve on. As for the

daughter—here Manners shook his head. Quick-tempered, unhappy, at war with her entire family and herself, as she was, he yet felt that murder would be beyond her. In his mind there was no doubt they would have to go outside the family to discover the murderer. 

He shuddered, remembering the attack upon himself.

At this moment the arrow was lying upon his desk, a long, sinister tube of shining steel. Why anyone should wish to take his life was inexplicable. But the attempt had been made. It could mean only one thing. His assailant had tried to kill him under the conviction that he knew something of importance, some fact which would fatally reveal the individual who was trying to wipe out the Kimball family. But Manners realized that he knew nothing. 

What was more, he possessed not even a theory. Yet whoever had tried to kill him, had sought to get him out of the world because he feared his knowledge.

Well, one could not go very far toward solving a murder unless one had some hint of the reason behind the crime. Without a doubt the attempt upon his life had been made because the killer thought that he, Manners, had found a clue to the motive. On the contrary he possessed none, not even the slightest suspicion of what it could be. 

He knew the police were in the same dilemma—that is, unless they suspected the shy assistant professor of English, Walter Oxford.

Manners was barely acquainted with Oxford. He had seen the man from time to time about the campus, a slight, delicate looking individual, with a small pointed mustache and a shyness that made his name a byword. That the 

Kimball girl, with her love for life, her vivid personality,

should have fallen in love with the shrinking assistant was something Manners did not try to explain. Her family, naturally enough, would oppose the match. Oxford had no money and it seemed very likely that he never would have any. It must have been a stormy scene when Henry 

Kimball threatened to throw the young professor from the grounds.

Was the opposition to their marriage sufficient provocation to drive either Oxford or the girl to commit murder?

The first death, that of the elder Kimball, seemed to be beyond the powers of the man or of the woman. They would know nothing about ricin. But Henry had been killed with an arrow, killed after he had threatened to have the professor thrown off the grounds. In the case of some individuals, whose temper had been whipped to the boiling point, an insult like this might provoke a tragedy. . He shook his head. The theory would not do. There was little doubt in his mind that Henry's death, too, had long been premeditated.

The sound of an approaching car caused him to raise his head. A taxi was speeding between the stone pillars of his drive; after making a wide circle, it stopped. A slight figure opened the door, paid the driver, then turned to look hesitatingly toward the house. Manners rose hastily to his feet. Only a few moments ago he had been wondering about Walter Oxford. At this moment the man was coming across his lawn. It was no social call; Oxford had never been in his house; and, from the hurried way the slight figure was moving toward him, he could guess that he was troubled.

They met almost in the center of the lawn. Oxford was

immaculately dressed, the blue suit fitting his slender body as if he had been poured into it. The straw hat was new; the blue ribbon around the band had a narrow streak of red. There was even an appropriate handkerchief peering from his pocket. The tiny black mustache had every glistening hair in its proper place, while the tie he wore was a work of art. But the man was nervous; his slight features twitched, and there was a look of fear in the blue eyes.

After expressing his pleasure at the visit, Manners suggested that he bring another chair from the veranda and that they sit by the table under the tree. This done, he motioned Oxford to sit down. His visitor obeyed, first carefully arranging his trousers so that the sharp crease would not be harmed. The offered cigarette was refused, the remark about the fine afternoon passed by as if the man had not heard it. Manners decided there was but one procedure to follow, allow the nervous professor to do all the talking. Sooner or later a reason for his visit would appear.

But he began to wonder if the man would ever speak. 

Once Oxford's lips half opened, but they were suddenly snapped shut again, as if to prevent a revelation. When at length he did speak, his soft, low voice was that of a man startled and bewildered by an experience he had just gone through. "Professor Manners," he began, "I have come to you, because because there seems no one else who can aid me." He gave a shudder of disgust. "I have just been through the worst three hours of my life. The police have been questioning me." His face grew very red. "They-

they almost accused me of murder."
