Oxford's trembling hand passed upward to fondle the tiny mustache. In his blue eyes was a wondering look as if he could not believe what had happened to him. There was fear in his face, the fear of a man confronted by a situation quite outside his usual experience. Manners realized that if he wished to secure any information he would have to do it by questioning. "Oh, I hardly think Rogan and his men were accusing you of murder, Oxford. You just happened to be the unlucky person who borrowed a bow and some arrowsand had a dispute with Henry Kimball.”

The immaculate figure shuddered as if the recollection was not pleasant. He leaned forward in his chair and his voice became urgent as he explained about the bow. He had borrowed one-it was a five foot wooden bow belonging to Marjorie Kimball-had taken a dozen arrows also.

During vacation he was staying in the city and had plenty of time at his disposal. He had borrowed the bow the afternoon of George Kimball's death, taking it to his rooms-where it was at the present moment-because he intended to practice archery. He flushed as he admitted the reason for this. On the only occasion when he had shot an arrow, his skill had been so poor that Miss Kimball had laughed. 

There came a pause. "Did you have a steel arrow?" Manners asked at last.

Oxford's reply was very emphatic. Not only did he insist that he had never seen a steel arrow in his life but he went into great detail to make clear that there were none on the Kimball grounds. The two sons and the

daughter, he stated, were expert shots but used only wooden arrows. Oxford was positive he had never seen a steel arrow in the hands of any of the Kimballs, nor, for that matter, had he ever heard of one being mentioned.

He admitted that, after hearing of George Kimball's death, he had gone at once to see his fiancée, and that he had discovered her in a wild, excitable mood, angry and talkative. "What did she say?" "Why, she insisted we go at once to Henry and tell him we were to be married." He hesitated. "I tried to talk her out of that idea. Her father had just died; it seemed to me not to be just the moment in which to talk over such a matter. But she insisted, said if she did not get away from the family now she never would be able to do so. We went to see Henry." He flushed, and Manners could see that the recollection of the visit was a painful one to him. "He was very angry, lost his temper and told me to get off the place and stay off."

Manners studied the excited figure in the chair opposite him. Oxford had forgotten that his hair had been carefully parted; a nervous hand had disarranged it, and in some manner the tie had become a little crooked. The handkerchief had been taken from its pocket to wipe the burning face, and Oxford had forgotten to return it. It was lying on the ground, close beside the chair, and the dog's big paw was resting on it. Apparently the man was being frank, yet there was one thing more that Manners wished to know. He put it in a question: "Why did your fiancée go to her younger brother? I should have thought Edward would have been the logical

one to consult; that is, if it was necessary to speak to either of them." "It was her own suggestion-not mine," was the apologetic reply. "Edward was not at home at the time, but in any case Henry had always been the spokesman for his father. He was the closest to him in spirit and thought. 

I think, though I do not know, that whatever the estate left Marjorie was to be handled by her brother Henry until she was thirty. That is my impression-I am not certain."

Manners' face became thoughtful. He glanced over the lawn, his eyes resting for a moment upon the long line of stone wall which marked the limits of his grounds. Far away lay the lake, a dark blue mass of water. Turning once more to his visitor he changed the subject. "You went to New York on the midnight train?" 

The answer surprised him. "Why, no. I intended to go. In fact, I got on the train and started for my berth; then I discovered I had left my money and tickets at home, so I walked through the cars and dropped off." 

Here was something new. Kent had been very positive that Oxford had left the city at midnight, before Henry Kimball had been murdered. This fact had made it impossible for any suspicion to be directed against Oxford. Now the immaculate professor had calmly stated that he had not gone on the midnight train. True, he had entered it-only to discover that his money had been forgotten! As Manners studied the thin flushed face he knew that Rogan would demand more details than this as to why the money had been forgotten.

"Did you tell them at the police station that you did not take the midnight train?"

Oxford shook his head. There was a grieved tone in his voice as he said: "They asked me about everything else on earth, but said nothing about that."

No doubt this was true. Kent had been so positive about the midnight train; they could very easily have overlooked questioning on this point. It would have to be looked into now, but he would leave that to the police. 

More to change the subject than anything else, he ventured another query: "Did Henry and his brother have much in common?"

Oxford reached a hand into his pocket for the handkerchief. A startled expression crossed his face when he failed to find it. Rather aimless was the search, then, when he saw it lying under the big paw of the dog, he half bent to pick it up, gave the animal another glance, and suddenly changed his mind. His eyes met Manners' blue eyes -a little weak, thought the professor. "Not in a sense," finally came the answer. "Henry was being trained to manage his father's trust. The brothers had very few hobbies, spent most of their time upon the place. Oh, they used to shoot some, afternoons—with bows. And the last year or so Henry had spent much time in a laboratory he had upon the grounds." "A laboratory!" was Manners' exclamation. "What sort of a laboratory?" "A chemical one. You see he had had a year in a technical college, and"-Oxford's hand waved vaguely in the air-"he became interested in it again. Spent some time

fooling round in a laboratory he had installed over one of the garages. "" 

Does this man have the slightest comprehension of what he is saying? was Manners' astonished thought. The police had made no search of the Kimball grounds along any such lines, feeling that none was needed. They would have to make one now. A sudden suspicion flashed into his mind. The father had died of poison. Edward had suggested that the warning telephone call might have come from some house on the estate itself, or even from within his own house. Oxford had just stated that for a year 

Henry Kimball had been fooling with chemicals, in his own laboratory- He rose suddenly to his feet. "Did you make mention to Rogan of Henry's laboratory?"

Seeing his host standing, Oxford also got to his feet. 

The question seemed to surprise him, as if he was unable to make out why it was asked. He made an emphatic and prompt denial, adding that the police had questioned him very little regarding any member of the Kimball family -except the daughter. He flushed as he made the last admission.

Slowly Manners picked up the sheets of paper from his table, folded them and placed them in his pocket. Why a woman of Marjorie Kimball's spirit should be in love with the weak, spineless creature before him he was unable to comprehend. The fact remained that she was. After all, it was her own affair. Perhaps it was the law of contrast-contrast with the driving force of her father, the rough, harsh mannerisms of her brothers—which had operated to make the shrinking, introverted professor a

paragon of virtue in her eyes. And now, in less than an hour, without understanding in the least what he had said, that same shrinking young man, with a few spoken words, had thrown the case wide open. There could be many theories tenable now.

With a hasty glance at his wrist watch, Manners noted that it was almost six and suggested that Oxford ride down to the city with him. Nodding his consent with pleasure, the assistant professor accepted the invitation. 

His word of thanks was stopped by a wave of Manners' hand. Saying that he would be out of the house again in a moment, he crossed the lawn and mounted the veranda. 

Behind him followed the dog, though the latter did not enter the house. Just as Manners reached the stand in the hall upon which the telephone stood, the instrument rang.

It was Rogan, a Rogan excited as usual, troubled and much upset. "Harley," came his hurried voice, "I have some news for you." "I have some for you, also," was the retort.

Silence for a moment-as though the chief was pondering the remark. Then it was thrust aside. "Yours can wait," he asserted. "Mine's more important. 

Listen, Harley; I've just heard Edward Kimball is dying. 

Bell tells me he cannot live an hour."

CHAPTER XI 

THE statement which had just fallen from Manners' lips caused Rogan to throw him one astonished look as he leaped to his feet. With both hands clutching the edge of the desk for support, he stared across its cluttered surface as though the professor had suddenly turned into an object which at once fascinated and horrified him. It was a searching and questioning look, that of a man who refused to accept the evidence of his own ears. "Repeat that again!" he snapped out. "What I just said, Chief, was that Oxford told me that for a year Henry Kimball has had a chemical laboratory upon his grounds, a laboratory in which he spent a great 

Ideal of his time." 

As if he had not even heard the remark, Rogan slowly picked up a long stogie and lighted it. Carefully he allowed himself to sink back again in his chair. For the moment he pushed from his mind what he had just heard. 

The chief had expected that the instant Manners entered the room his first question would be regarding Edward Kimball. Ever since Bell had informed him that the man was dying, there had been room for only one thought in Rogan's mind. All his forebodings were coming true. He had expected that Manners would share them. Instead, he had rushed into the office and excitedly demanded if he knew anything about a chemical laboratory Henry Kimball had had in a garage. 163

So full of Kimball's imminent death was Rogan's mind that the import of the laboratory did not at first strike him. Edward Kimball was dying, and at the moment of his death the chief was going to order another autopsy. 

No one would have to suggest it to him this time. And not only would he order it at once, but deep down in his heart he knew what the outcome would be. To talk about a chemical laboratory at such a time-then he suddenly caught his breath. One of the Kimballs had died from poison; another was now at the point of death, and almost certainly from the same cause. Now, out of a clear sky, dropped the information that the second son-who himself had been murdered by an arrow-was interested in chemistry!

As he stared across the table, he decided that the case was turning into one of those puzzles he occasionally noticed in the evening papers. If one equals two, what will three equal? He had made Manners repeat his statement, wondering, while he spoke, where he had secured the information. Dropping back again in his chair, he roughly demanded more details.

Quickly the story of Oxford's visit was told. Rogan's face did not change its expression when he heard that the man had not taken the midnight train. He uttered no comment until Manners' voice died away. He was thinking, and his thoughts were far from self-congratulatory. 

For over three hours he and Kent had examined that shrinking professor, and in all that time they had forgotten to ask once regarding the time of his departure from the city. Well, that was one item he hoped the papers would not get hold of. Then a sudden inspiration came

to him. "Harley," came his eager voice, "there's a flaw in his yarn about the train. You say he told you that, after going to his berth, he discovered his money and ticket had been left at home?" 

As Manners nodded his assent, Rogan's hand went slapping down on the desk. "It won't hold. You can't get on those limited trains without showing both your ticket and your Pullman berth. 

He lied." 

Manners grinned a sheepish grin. Rogan was right.

Again and again he had himself taken that midnight train to New York. A line of sleepers stood on a spur track, and one could enter them at eleven. But always one's ticket had to be shown, and the Pullman receipt as well, to the porter standing by the steps. He should have thought of that. He began an apologetic reply, only to be prevented by Rogan's cold dry voice. "Harley, Kent and I had that bird in this room for three hours. Like a pair of fools we never said a thing about the train he was presumed to have taken." He rose to his feet, feeling a little cheered by what he was going to say. "But you slipped up too. You should have spotted that hole in his story. Now I'll have to get hold of him again." He paused to reflect. "I wonder why he lied?" 

Manners could see Rogan's hand stealing toward the box of push buttons. He knew what was ahead, but, before he left the room, there was some information he wanted. His voice held the bending fingers suspended above the call box as he asked: "Tim, what's this about Edward Kimball?”

He received a long, grave look. Then the chief answered: "The man we left up at the Kimballs called the station a little over an hour ago and said they were taking Edward to the hospital. He had suddenly collapsed. I rushed Bell over there; in fact, he beat the ambulance; and he phoned back a few moments ago that he was unconscious and dying." "Did you do as I said-notify Kimball yesterday as to the cause of his father's death-beg him to have a doctor look him over, and, if he felt the least bit ill, to get an expert?"

Rogan nodded. It had not been a pleasant task, and, because he had known it would not be, he had taken it upon his own shoulders, had gone to Kimball's house, insisted upon seeing him, and bluntly told him that his father had died from poison. The scene flashed again before his eyes. Edward Kimball had not acted as if he was greatly surprised. True, he had looked ill, and, a little later, had admitted that he had been—as he put it—“a little indisposed." It was this admission that had given 

Rogan his opening. He had begged Kimball to see a doctor, to have the physician make certain at once that there was nothing seriously the matter. Kimball had agreed, and the chief had left. After all, there had been nothing more he could do. And now, the man was dying, perhaps even at this very moment was dead. All this he related to Manners. "You know what it means, Tim," came his friend's reply. "Another autopsy. And you will discover that—” 

Manners did not finish the sentence.

He had no need to do so. Well did the chief know what were the unspoken words. There would be another autopsy, the discovery that Edward had died as had his father, and the identification of the poison as ricin. And, after they had discovered those facts, why, it would be, just as before, all that they did know. Three murders would have been committed, all the men of the town's richest family wiped out; and the police would still be helpless. His teeth clenched tight on the stogie as, with a vicious thrust, his finger hit a button in the black box.

He might not know much, but there was one thing he could accomplish.

Picking up a receiver he dialed a number. Sharp and hurried was his command to his unseen listener: "Get Bell. Shoot him at once to the Kimball place. Tell him to hurry." 

Slamming back the receiver, he quickly called another number. "Tell Kent to go to the Kimball place with Bell. Have the car all ready for them."

Even as the receiver went back into place, a door into the hall opened, and a young officer came into the room. 

He also was greeted by Rogan's barking voice: "Bring me those blank search warrants."

As the man hastened away, the chief explained to Manners: "We keep a number of search warrants down here, signed by the proper judge-have them so we won't waste any time if we need one in a hurry-just fill in names and dates." He was searching a drawer for something, but paused long enough to add with a growl: "All illegal, of course, but it saves time. And we are now going to have

one nice look over that laboratory you told me about." 

The officer had returned. He was, in fact, beside the desk before Rogan raised his head. One look, then the legal-appearing documents were snatched from his hand. 

Hurriedly finding a pen on his desk, the chief's big hand began laboriously to write upon the documents. This took but a moment. Then, jumping to his feet, he located his hat on a corner of the desk and started across the floor. 

At the door, he turned to throw back a belated command: "Hurry, Harley; you are driving me to the Kimballs'.'

It was several miles to their destination from the police station. Twilight was approaching, and the lake road was lined with traffic-cars going to the peninsula. Rogan had suggested speed, and his wish was being gratified. The long blue car went weaving in and out of the traffic, its speed never going much below sixty. And every foot of the way, Rogan, slumped far down on the leather seat, with one hand gripping his hat, kept up a steady monologue.

He was only thinking out loud, but there was portions of his thoughts which would have caused most individuals, if they had heard them, to blush. These portions dealt, mostly, with what he called his own stupidity-the failure of Kent and himself to discover that Oxford had not gone on the midnight train. Several expressions were plain threats of what would happen to that young gentleman when they had him again in the office. Long and vigorous also were his comments regarding the information that Henry Kimball had a private laboratory. This was gone into from every angle. Just one thought the chief did not express. He did not mention the suspicion that was

rapidly forming in his mind. 

Meanwhile, with his eyes glued on the traffic ahead,

Manners had no time to observe the expression on the chief's face. He listened, it is true, to the steady flow of words, chuckled several times when a more vigorous expression than usual escaped from the open lips; but shortly before they reached the private road leading down to the lake, he forgot the jerky phrases entirely and began to play with his own thoughts.

Oxford had lied. The chief's blunt word had expressed the truth. It would have been impossible for him to have entered the Pullman without showing his ticket. Would it, though? Might there not be a slight chance that the porter was not standing by the steps of the car? But he recalled a second fact—and this proved conclusively that Oxford had not told the truth. In order that passengers from the city should not be disturbed during travel, their tickets were shown to the conductor who was seated at a desk in the station, but berth tickets were always shown at the car steps. It would have been impossible for Oxford to enter the train without showing his tickets. 

A disgusted scowl swept Manners' face at the thought.

He should have recognized the flaw in the story at once. 

How many times he had taken that train. But there was a second and more important thing to discover. Oxford had obviously expected to travel by that train. He must have expected to do so until the time he went into the Pullman car in which he had purchased a berth. He had entered the car, showing his ticket at the steps-though he had denied the last-then something had caused him to change his mind. What had it been? A message of some

sort, without a doubt; a message waiting for him on the train.

They had, by this time, turned off the main highway and were running down a private road which led to the lake. On each side were extensive lawns, filled with colorful flowers, and large houses, hidden from the road by high privet hedges, houses mostly of colonial design, whose white paint reflected the setting sun. The road ended at an iron gate through which could be seen a curving gravel drive, lined on each side with elm trees. In the distance could be glimpsed a long, rambling stone house. They paused, for a second, at the gate, while a man flung it open for them to enter. 

At a command from Rogan, the car was brought to a stop about five hundred feet from the house. What was in the chief's mind Manners could only guess, but he followed him from the machine. Walking past the row of elms lining the driveway, Rogan paused for a moment upon the close-cropped grass. As far as he could see, stretched lawn and grass, broken by another house perhaps five hundred yards away. This house caused his lips to tighten as he glimpsed it. At the same moment the sound of a car coming through the gate caused him to whirl around.

It was a police car, driven by Bell, who pulled to a stop beside the blue roadster. Observing his superior, he came strolling very slowly, with Kent behind him, to his side. 

He had evidently managed to secure some sleep, for the tired look had vanished from his eyes. He wore no hat, but there was nothing unusual in that-most of the time 

Bell disdained a hat. Throwing a quizzical glance at

Rogan, he turned to address Manners. "Well, Harley," he admitted, "we still have no idea how that poison got into George Kimball's body." Pausing, he searched his pockets, found a battered cigarette, lit it and added: "It's my idea we are going to find ourselves with another murder on our hands. Edward was dying when I left the hospital, and there is nothing they can do for him.” 

An odd grimace swept Rogan's face. To hide what he was thinking, he turned to Bell. Roughly but rapidly he explained why they had come again to the estate. The mention of a laboratory caused the young physician to open his eyes wide. It was characteristic of him, however, that he merely shrugged his shoulders and then simply said: "Well, let's get busy."

The four of them started toward the house, which was some distance off. Rogan snapped back a command to Kent: "You will have to get to a telephone, George, and have someone pick up Oxford. I will tell you why later. I want him at the station when we get back."

As they crossed the immense lawn, Rogan decided, for the time being, not to bother with any of the Kimball family. No doubt they were all at the hospital anyway. 

Henry Kimball's laboratory was in a garage behind the man's house. He would look there first. One thing was certain; it would be found before they left the grounds. 

Skirting a low white house, they saw a brick garage in the rear. It was a large building with many windows in the second story. The wide door was open and, as they

approached, they noticed a car standing on the concrete floor and saw that a man was making a pretence of washing it. Not until they entered the garage did the man sense their presence. When he did, he rose hastily to his feet. For a second his eyes swept over them; then, recognizing the chief, he dropped his sponge and hastened to meet them. 

It was the Kimball chauffeur. Manners remembered seeing the tall young Irishman driving about the city streets. Evidently he knew Rogan, for he smiled at the chief-though the smile vanished at the tone which snapped back a question: "Mike, I want you to answer one question, and answer it damned quick. Did Henry have a laboratory on the place?"

The young man's face lengthened in astonishment. For a second it appeared that he did not understand the question; then, all at once, his features lightened. “Oh, I know what you mean. Sure. It's up there." He waved a hand toward the second floor, as he spoke. 

Behind the car they saw a stairway-not wide or steep -leading up to a little landing on which a door gave exit. 

Rogan darted around the car and started up the steps; the others were close behind. Reaching the landing, he gave the door knob one turn. Then followed a heavy pull, but the door did not open. "Have you got a key?" he shouted down to the chauffeur.

The man knew nothing about the key. He informed them that the door was always kept locked and that he had never been in the room. Then followed a statement

which caused Bell to give vent to a long low whistle. "I have seen it though—when I carried up the pigs and rabbits he used." 

Rogan's hand fell away from the knob. He turned to cast an amazed glance downwards. "Pigs and rabbits!" he shouted. "Sure. He used them in the stuff he was doing.” 

The answer caused Kent to dart up the steps. Bell had whispered in his ear, and the head of the murder squad rushed up to join his superior. He also tried the door and, when it would not budge, questioned his chief: "Shall I bust it down?"

For a second there was hesitancy; then Rogan made up his mind. At the sight of his face, even before the voice gave him the command, Kent acted.

It was a massive door, and the lock was stout. Not until Bell had joined him was Kent able to hear the first creaking sound announcing that the door was beginning to give way. Even then it resisted, resisted until the two men drew back and together threw all their weight against it. Then there came the sound of splintering wood, and with a crash the door fell inward.

Bell was the first one to enter the room. It covered the entire second floor of the garage, a long room, with many windows on each side for light. In the roof was a large skylight, the glass spotlessly clean. But it was neither the windows nor the skylight which held the gaze of the four men. It was something else. Along each wall ran a tier of long benches, benches containing many racks, filled with test tubes. And in the center of the room, running almost its entire length, was a work bench con-

taining a series of sinks flanked with glistening apparatus. 

It was a well equipped chemical laboratory into which they were looking.

Bell continued to lead the way into the room. Silently, he walked beside the rows of long benches, his face keen and interested. His eyes fell upon retorts and flasks, glanced into test tubes by the score, noticed two German air pumps, scowled at three stills. A small electric furnace caused him to turn to Manners, and the look on his face told far more than any words. A cabinet was found unlocked, and, when the door was opened, rows and rows of bottles were discovered upon the shelves. Finally, with a peculiar smile upon his face, Bell began to read the titles of a score of books which were ranged upon a little stand. 

When he had finished he turned again to Manners. "I don't know what it may all mean; but take a look at that top row of books. All works on toxicology. The standard ones are all there-Webster, Haines and Peterson, Underhill, Sollman-" He paused, then gave vent to a violent oath. "What in hell should Henry Kimball want with those four books all dealing with poisons?" 

Rogan had trailed his doctor. His eyes also had rested upon the glistening apparatus, and now he was wondering why the books should hold Bell's interest. He could see plainly that their titles had excited the doctor. At once he demanded to know what it was all about, but before replying Bell had to light a cigarette and the first smoke ring was blown into the air before he spoke. "It's this, Chief," he said at last. "On that shelf are the leading works upon toxicology. They all deal with poison. Now what in the devil was Henry Kimball doing

with them?"

Rogan's hand darted out to pull a thick, heavy volume from the shelf. As it was opened, Manners noticed that it was the "Webster," the latest edition of the well-known work. Slowly, Rogan's heavy hands turned the pages. His eyes suddenly opened wide as they rested upon certain familiar words in its pages. With a hurried gesture he turned to the index and his search through the pages of fine print was that of a man not used to consulting reference works. It was with a little cry of triumph that his eyes at last found what they were seeking. For a moment he stared at the page then thrust the book back on the shelf. "It's in there," he said. "Ricin. It's mentioned in that book." His eyes searched the room. "Now every bottle, every box, everything here must be examined." "Chief," began Bell, "that's true, but you need an expert to go through this stuff. After all, I am a doctor, not a chemist. You'd better have Hoff come here. This room will be just the sort of thing he will understand. 

And if there is anything here, he'll find it. I doubt if the rest of us can."

It was a good suggestion, thought Manners. There was a good deal of apparatus in the place. Countless bottles and vials stood upon the shelves, and, though some he saw were labeled, others were not. On the floor, also, were large bottles filled with liquids-liquids of many colors. 

It would take an expert to report on what the room contained. And it would, incidentally, be a job the toxicologist would enjoy. He added his comment to Bell's suggestion, reminding Rogan that, though the discovery of

the laboratory was an important thing, more important still would be the exact knowledge of what it contained. "That's right," was the gloomy admission. "Doc," he added, "you get hold of Hoff at once. Better shoot in the call from up here. Also get hold of Kent's office and be sure someone has gone out to pick up Oxford." He turned to the inspector. "George, you stay right here, and don't allow anyone to enter this room."

Turning, the chief started for the door with Bell at his heels. Before following them Manners cast one last look over the room. It was a surprisingly well equipped laboratory, for a private one. The apparatus was fairly new, yet showed signs of use. There was a long array of chemicals in the various bottles and jars. As he turned to follow the two men, he realized that, if they had held any theory regarding the crimes, they would everyone have to be discarded in the light of that room. Henry Kimball not only had been familiar with chemistry, but the latest works upon poison stood upon his shelves. It was a disquieting thought-and he tried not to wonder what it might portend. 

They found the chauffeur still engaged in his pretence of washing the car. The look upon the young man's face showed that he must have heard at least part of what had been said above his head. A swift glance-then Rogan darted to the front of the machine. His hand went slamming down on the shoulder of the kneeling man. With a jerk he pulled him upright. "Mike," he barked, "you've worked for the Kimballs at least two years. What did Henry do upstairs?"

Though the chief's hand was pressing into his shoulder,

the man registered no disturbance. His face was brick red from the sun, but, though it flushed a little as he looked up, there was only a slight smile on his lips, as he answered: "Heavens above, Chief, how would I know? Mr. Kimball fooled round with smells and things up there. He used to take little guinea pigs and rabbits-the poor things -and put something in them. Then they would die, and 

I had to bury them. But what he was doing I don't know." 

The hand slipped off the shoulder. Rogan's lips grew tight. He threw an appealing glance at Manners and, as their eyes met, the chief slowly shook his head. To hide his thoughts, he put another question to the chauffeur. "When did you see Henry last?" "It was the night the old man died, I think. It was shortly after he died Henry came out here." "The night of the " Rogan checked the words. "The night George died Henry came out here?" "Yes. I heard the old man had kicked off, and thought they might want to use the car. I started for the garage and, when I got close by, I saw Henry just opening the doors. He didn't put on the light down here but in a moment one came on up above. And then, just as I was wondering if I better wait outside, I saw another man come over the lawn and go in the garage.” He paused to apologize. "It was pretty dark where I was standing, so 

I know they didn't see me. Besides, the man was in too much of a hurry." "Do you know who it was?" 

The chauffeur laughed. "I ought to," he said, "having worked here for two

years. It was that fat judge-what do you call him?”. "Judge Parrish?" inquired Rogan. "Yes, that's who it was." A flush swept over the man's face, and he became confidential. "I ought not to have done it, sir, but I was a bit curious. I came in here in the dark garage and, and-" 

As he hesitated, there came a sharp command. "Spill it!" "Well, I know I ought not to have done it, but I climbed into the back seat of the car, leaving the door open a little. Parrish and Henry were having a confab upstairs, I don't know what it was about, they spoke low, but in a few moments the judge came hurrying down the stairs and went away, and then, in about five minutes, what do you think happened?" Rogan shook his head. 

He was in no mood to think. "Why, Edward came rushing in and hurried upstairs." "You saw Edward Kimball go up into the laboratory?" was the astonished question. "Yes. And then they did have a row. I could hear some of it. Edward was ordered not to go down and see you.

I heard Henry say it would make trouble and besides was a silly thing to do. They did row it back and forth." "Do you know what the row was about?" "It seemed to be mostly over Mr. Edward coming down to see you. He got a little bit angry himself, though, knowing he had been bothered by a bad tooth for some days, I was not surprised. But when he came to the head of the steps to come down here, Henry followed him to the door. He didn't say anything to him until he had almost reached the garage door. Then he yelled at him.”

“Yelled what?”

The chauffeur's face broke into a broad grin. For a second he studied Rogan's stern, set face. Reassured, evidently, by what he saw, he cautiously moved several feet away before he said: "He yelled: 'If you go down to that damned fool police chief, you're going to be sorry, very sorry!'"

CHAPTER XII 

THE table had at last been cleared and the waiter had left the room closing the door behind him. Their table stood between two windows, and during their dinner Manners and Stone had been able to glance down at a brilliantly lighted street. Directly across from them the bright sign of a moving picture theater flickered its changing colors. Into the little room floated the confused sounds of the street, horns of cars, the shrill cries of newsboys.

It was a warm evening and the windows were thrown wide open. 

It had been Manners' suggestion that Stone should dine with him. As they walked out of Henry Kimball's garage, Rogan had been filled with a sudden determination. He was not going to return to the station until he had talked with all the servants in the three houses. Warning Manners that this would take him some time, he suggested that the latter return to the city and meet him at ten o'clock at the police station. It was a sensible idea. As 

Manners, acquiescing, started over the grass to his car, Rogan had added the suggestion that he get in touch with the police commissioner and tell him what they had discovered. It occurred to Manners that the easiest way to do this was to ask Stone to eat with him, and the lawyer readily accepted. 

Because he knew they would wish privacy, Manners had decided upon his club. It was housed in what had 180

once been a fashionable home in the city, but that was in a day long since past. It was a quiet club and so select that new members were taken in only at long intervals. 

Like himself, Stone was a member, and Manners knew that one of the private dining rooms would give them the secrecy they desired. Now the dinner was over, the door closed, and they at last alone. 

Manners lighted a long cigar before he began his tale. 

Stone had shaken his head at his proffered case, saying he preferred his own. Shrewd, intelligent, the police commissioner knew that the grave man who sat opposite him had something on his mind. He had often dined with 

Manners, and as a rule they had discussed books, or entered into a long debate upon some obscure historical fact dealing with the French Revolution. But tonight his host was silent, speaking only at long intervals. Stone was glad when the waiter departed, knowing that now he would discover what was on Manners' mind.

Quickly, but vividly, Manners went into the story of his afternoon. He spent some time describing the laboratory which had been found in Henry Kimball's garage, mentioned that by now Hoff was going over its contents. 

Then he passed on to the story of the chauffeur, mentioning Judge Parrish's hurried visit to the garage, followed by Edward Kimball's. Briefly he spoke of what they had been told regarding the quarrel between the two brothers. As an afterthought he added that Rogan would expect them both to be down at the station by ten o'clock. 

It was characteristic of Stone that he should consult his watch when Manners ended. It was also like him to make no comment on what he had been told, but to volunteer

instead some information. "Edward Kimball died tonight at eight o'clock," he announced. "I saw the item in the extra that was out. The city is already a mass of rumors. All sorts of stories are floating around. They are saying things-hinting lots of things.' 

He sighed as he looked out of the window for a moment. 

It was a fine face, thought Manners, kindly, intelligent, and very much in repose. But he was surprised when, with a sudden gesture, Stone turned to smile across the table, saying in a quizzical tone: "You know, Harley, I suppose the city calls you and me happy men. I have held a leading position at the bar for many years, and have, of course, what we call a reputation. But oftentimes I wonder what it's all about, why 

I spend my life doing the absurd things my profession demands. The law is a silly thing, an absurdity in a civilized land. But"-he smiled a kindly smile which lit up his grave face-"so is your profession a silly one, though not so absurd as my own. You spend your days dealing with what you call a science-psychology-the attempt to discover why people act as they do. And yet you do not even know whether it is a science." He lifted a hand to prevent a retort. "And I doubt if you and I ever dare ask ourselves if we are happy. If we did, our answer would be 'No.' Yet we have books and pictures and thoughts to escape into, while poor Rogan— "Poor Rogan," he repeated after a pause. "The city is already upset. Tomorrow they will begin to become alarmed. The next day they will commence to attack the police. And Rogan, who has but five weeks ahead of him,

may leave his office in a perfect storm of anger and disgrace. It will break the old man's heart if he does, for he is happy, Harley, happy over the record he has established. And now comes this Kimball affair. "I suppose there is little doubt that we will discover that Edward was also murdered-by ricin?" he asked, at the end of another long pause. 

Manners sketched the symptoms as they had been repeated to him by Rogan. They were similar to those of the elder Kimball. "I am afraid we will find that his death came from the same cause. There will have to be an autopsy," he admitted. "And there is not even a good theory to work upon." "Unless-" began Manners. "Unless-" said Stone, looking at the tip of his cigar -"unless we assume that Henry Kimball was in some unknown manner mixed up in the affair.”

He paused to look again out of the window. It was some time before he continued. "I knew all the Kimballs," he went on. "In many ways, the old man, hard and ruthless as he was, was the best of the three. Old George had but one love-money --and that's bad enough. Edward, well he sort of trailed along, not much driving force, but a pretty good business man at that. But Henry-" 

The cigar had to be relighted. Then Stone asked: "Which is the worse vice, Harley, to love money or to love power?" 

Before Manners could reply, Stone answered his own question:

"I never cared at all for Henry Kimball. He had all the worst traits of his father, together with a few he had picked up for himself. He loved power, just as much as he loved money. To gain it and to keep it, he would be absolutely unscrupulous; and he was without conscience. 

We must remember that. And-" He paused to throw a little smile across the table-"we both know what theory Rogan is going to spring on us. It seems, superficially, the logical one at the present moment." "That Henry poisoned his father?" Manners retorted. "That Edward had his suspicions something was wrong, and, finding out, killed his brother? That before this happened Henry had already given the poison to Edward? 

Yes, it sounds like a cross-word puzzle, but Rogan's theory will be something like that." "Just what was it that brought Edward down to see 

Rogan? I seem a little confused regarding his visit."

Manners explained the reasons given by Edward Kimball for having come to see the police chief. The man had mentioned a telephone call, said that his wife was alarmed, asked that two detectives be sent to guard the grounds. Yet he had denied that the family had the slightest suspicion regarding the circumstances of his father's death. The police commissioner was reminded that, at the time Edward had visited the office, they had no idea his father had been murdered. "No," was the retort, "but I heard you were uneasy.

Anyhow there was something odd in that visit. The family, through their paper, had fought the chief for years. 

Why should the eldest son, the night his father died, rush down to the station?"

"If you want to put questions like that, you might ask another," was Manners' dry comment. "What was going on at the Kimball place shortly after the old man died?

We find Henry going over to his laboratory. We discover 

Judge Parrish meeting him there. Then follows the quarrel between the two brothers. Something was happening. What?" "As for Parrish," drawled the other, "God alone knows what that vulture was up to. Maybe warning Henry that his brother was going to see the police. It would be like him. And you can rest assured that, after George Kimball's death, Parrish would rush to get in good favor with the brother who would have the greatest influence. That would be Henry."

There was one thing over which Manners had puzzled much. He informed the commissioner that he understood the Kimball fortune was tied up in a trust. That fact would make it seem absurd to suspect anyone of committing murder in order to gain a larger share of the estate. Stone's reply startled him: "The insurance shares make up the bulk of the trust. 

You know he controlled the Alpha Insurance company.

The Kimball building and the other four office buildings that are also in the trust-that money and income cannot be touched. But there is a good deal of property scattered over the city-houses, and building lots—acquired since the Kimball trust was formed. These properties would have to go through the usual legal formalities which come after death." "So someone would benefit if George Kimball and 

Edward died?"

Stone nodded. "I would think so," was his admission. "Of course I know nothing of the Kimball will. But, naturally enough, if the father and elder son were not living, somebody would be bound to benefit. It might be Henry; then again, it might not be. It all depends upon how the property was left in the will. We have, of course, the state inheritance laws."

Manners nodded. There seemed little cause for discussion. When millions were involved, it appeared hardly necessary to hunt for a motive for murder. Yet he did not care to theorize; after all, that should come after 

Rogan had presented the facts he had been able to gather.

He was interested to know what Stone thought of their discovery of a chemical laboratory on the Kimball grounds.

The lawyer had a good deal to say on that point. He admitted that he was utterly unable to account for the laboratory they had discovered. Why Henry Kimball should be interested in chemistry, why he should have spent a considerable amount of money in equipping a laboratory, he could not comprehend. "And in that laboratory," was Manners' dry comment, "were four of the leading text books dealing with poison." 

Stone made an eloquent gesture with his hands. "It seems incredible," he acknowledged. "But it's far too early to theorize about it.”

That was Manners' opinion as well. There were plenty of theories now; what they lacked were definite facts. 

Until they had something concrete, it would only be a waste of time to allow their imaginations to run wild. He

said as much and received a nod of approval from the lawyer. Then Stone suddenly leaned across the table. "It seems that all we can do just now is to be very careful Rogan makes no misstep," he said. "After all, I am his superior and can hold him down. As I said a while ago, it would be a tragedy if he were forced to go out of office under the censure of the entire city." He paused, then added: "And it may happen. The three deaths in the Kimball family-murders, all of them-will have the city in an uproar."

Silence fell. It was broken by the sound of cars in the street, the crying of the newsboys. Manners looked at his wrist watch. It was almost ten o'clock, the hour he had promised Rogan they would be at the police station. 

Rising, he mentioned the hour, to receive a nod from the commissioner.

The two men walked to the station. It was only a few blocks away and they decided that to take their cars would be absurd. The streets were deserted, likewise the shops, with only here and there some passer-by looking into their lighted windows. The public square was finally reached. On one side loomed the dark outline of the public library, across from it the squatty police station. 

But there were lights in the latter building, a few windows showing squares of flame against the darkness.

They mounted the five steps and came into the long hall. Even as they pushed open the door, they saw a man entering the chief's office. At the very moment of their glimpsing him, he vanished through the door so that they were unable to tell who it was. A moment later they themselves pushed open the door to enter the lighted

room. Once inside, they saw Rogan behind his desk and, standing facing him, the short figure of the toxicologist, Hoff. Both men turned as the new-comers entered, and 

Rogan smiled a weary, disheartened greeting. Hoff simply nodded, turning once more to the police chief. "Well," he began, "I went through that laboratory. 

It's an odd mix-up. The apparatus is good; some of it, of course, simply the things an amateur would bring together. I examined the vials and the chemicals, a queer collection—some poisons among them. What he was doing seems hard to understand." "Did you find any ricin?" Manners was moved to ask.

The toxicologist turned to glare at his friend. Behind the heavy glasses his bright eyes gleamed with excitement. For a moment he simply returned Manners' gazethen slowly shook his head, admitting: "No, none. But I did discover other poisons-arsenic, aconitine, and such stuff. What in hell he was doing with them I don't know. But ricin-" He shrugged his heavy shoulders. "There was none. It's my idea from what I saw in the laboratory that he was fooling round with coal-tar dyes. Why? You can answer that as well as I."

When he paused Rogan gave him one look, then bent his head to the papers on his desk. He had spent three hours on the Kimball estate. Save for the discovery of the laboratory and the admissions of the chauffeur, he had discovered nothing. Depressed, anxious, he had hoped

Hoff would have some real information to give him. It appeared he had nothing. But he put one hopeful question: "Do you think, Hoff, Henry Kimball could have known

anything about ricin?”

Hoff made a sweeping gesture with his hands as he replied: "Any man who was interested enough in chemistry to spend ten thousand dollars on a laboratory-and it cost about that should have brains enough to discover ricin. 

He had four of the best known works in existence upon poisons. One of them mentions ricin. Of course, I cannot put myself inside another man's mind, but it seems to me, chief, you can be very positive that he would have known about it." 

Rogan's lips tightened. Then he changed the subject. "Hoff, while you were in that laboratory Edward Kimball died," he said. He paused, as he threw a disarming glance at Stone, and went on: "I ordered an autopsy.

Bell is already at work. He expects, Hoff, that you will aid him. Perhaps you'd better run along and see what he is doing."

Hoff simply nodded. Earthquakes might come, the world could be threatened with destruction, but nothing would change his calm, stoical attitude. With a wave of his hand he left the room. 

When the door had closed behind him, Rogan thrust back his chair and gloomily regarded his two friends. It was a despairing look, filled with anxiety, and yet having behind it a fixed determination. Slowly he told of the three hours he had spent on the Kimball estate. Servants had been interrogated, but from them he had learned nothing. Only one fact had been established. Judge Parrish had been at Edward Kimball's house shortly after the elder Kimball died. One maid had seen him hurry out

of the living room, and stated that it was almost thirty minutes before he returned. To Rogan that seemed to prove that the chauffeur was telling the truth. "Did you suspect that he was not?" was Manners' question.

The chief admitted that he had reached the stage when he hardly knew whom or what to believe. The women of the Kimball family, he added, he had been unable to see; they had all been at the hospital. Then his tone grew very serious as he added: "Edward died, you know."

The admission needed no comment. Suddenly rising to his feet, Rogan thrust back his chair. His face was very red. For a second he glared across the desk. "Do you know what I think?" he demanded. "It's my belief Henry killed both his father and his brother." "And who killed Henry?" was Stone's dry query. "Edward. Look at it this way. Suppose Edward discovered his brother was up to something, knew about the poison. He was an expert with a bow. He could have very easily killed his brother. Who else is there we can suspect?" "And why was all this done? What was the motive?" 

Rogan threw a disgusted look over the desk. His voice was rough, excited as he retorted: "How in hell should I know? But we can find out. 

That's one thing we can do. And it's the only theory that fits the facts."

It was time Rogan was brought back sharply to reality. 

Rather coldly, Manners reminded him of what the facts were. They knew that George Kimball had died from

poison; that Henry had been killed by an arrow; and that Edward had died, suddenly and under circumstances similar to his father's. As yet they could not say it was poison-though they had every reason to think it might be. A chemical laboratory had been discovered on the 

Kimball grounds, belonging to the murdered son. It contained well-known text books upon poison. On the night of the father's death the two brothers had quarreled. 

These were the facts. What their meaning might be, they did not know. This last the chief should remember.

Rogan took his lecture in good grace. He had reseated himself in his chair, and, a stogie lighted, was listening without a change of expression. Somehow the sight of the two men across from him brought a little comfort to his heart. They knew things he did not know: again and again had aided in the solution of the problems which had come into his office. But-there had never been a problem like this one. Well did he realize all it implied. 

By now the city would be in an uproar. They would demand impossible things from the police. And Manners was right. They knew a few facts-but what was behind this circle of death they had not the slightest conception. 

He might have made a retort to Manners' ironical voice, but the connecting door which led into the office of the homicide squad suddenly opened. A detective thrust his head through the opening, gave one swift glance over the room, then grinned at Rogan. "We are bringing in a man to you, chief," said the officer. "That college professor, Oxford. He seems pretty mad."

Even as he spoke, the door leading to the hall opened.

Three men entered the room. One was Kent, another a detective, and behind them walked a third, the shrinking, protesting figure of Walter Oxford, his face as white as the walls of Rogan's office. 

Across the floor they came, with Kent in the lead. The plain-clothes man had now dropped behind, his hand upon the shoulder of the professor. The detective's face wore a broad grin, the smile of a man well satisfied with his accomplishment. But Oxford's face was that of one whose world had collapsed, who had come to the end of his rope. 

As they halted beside the desk, Kent laid his hand upon the professor's shoulder. For a moment he looked into the nervous face. Then, turning to his superior, he said: "Here's your man, Chief."

Very wearily did Rogan lift himself from his chair. 

Around his desk he walked, until he stood close to the shrinking figure. Deliberate was the look he gave him, a look which started at Oxford's feet and came to rest upon his face. Then his voice came, harsh as a rusty file: "Well, you are back again. I suppose you are going to claim you told us the truth when you were in here a while ago?" 

Oxford's face was white, but he threw back his head and tried to look the chief in the face. It was an odd look, a mixture of wonder and fear. His voice trembled as he replied: "I-I don't know why you brought me back again, Chief." 

Like a thunder bolt Rogan's hand slammed down on the cringing shoulder. With a sudden pull he whirled the man completely about.

"What time did you take the train to New York?" he barked. 

Oxford's face flushed. He threw an appealing glance at

Manners, a look begging for aid. Then he turned to the chief. “I—I told you before," he stammered. "The midnight train." "Like hell you did!" was the rough retort.

The stern voice, with its note of incredulity, caused the thin face of the professor to grow suddenly white. For a second he cast an imploring glance around the room. 

Then came his voice, low, frightened, hesitating: "I told you the truth." 

Rogan's hand was like an avenging angel. It whirled the slight figure once more, and the action was far from gentle. In the chief's eyes lay an angry gleam, reflected by the tone in his voice. "Oxford," he snapped out, "the time has passed for concealment. We have plenty of methods by which we get at the truth. Dark cells-oh, lots of things." He paused, then finished: "I know you did not take that train. Now come across.' 

With a violent shove, the professor was sent spinning over the floor. Catching his balance, his face white, he turned to Rogan. "Yes," he almost shrieked, "it's true. I did not take the train. I boarded it, intending to take it, and in my berth was a message, a message from-" The voice hesitated. "Tell the truth, Walter," came a calm voice from the door.

All turned to look across the floor. Just inside the door stood Marjorie Kimball. Her head was thrown back, the dark eyes were inscrutable. Wearing a yellow dress, closely fitted to her figure, she stood looking calmly across the room. She had entered the office so softly that not one of its occupants had heard the door open. 

Oxford threw her an anxious glance. His face flushed a little as her grave glance met his. "I would like to know what she is thinking," was Manners' thought. "One thing is certain; yesterday she was an irresponsible girl, but the woman who is now looking at us has suddenly grown up." "Tell him the truth, Walter," came the husky voice again. "I-well, I found a message from Marjorie," came in a hesitating tone. 

Rogan stopped the words with a wave of his hand.

Gravely his eyes turned to study the woman leaning against the wall of his office. "She is a beautiful woman," was his first thought. And the second was just as flattering: "I think I would trust her. Her every appearance indicates that she wants to help me." His question was to her. "What was your message, Miss Kimball?" 

A half smile crossed her lips. Slowly she came across the floor, accepting a chair Manners offered. Seated, she turned to the chief. "I heard you had Walter down here. As to the message 

I sent him, it's a simple thing to explain. I discovered after my father's death that my two brothers had quarreled, heard also that Edward had come down to see you. So

I called the district messenger service and asked them to

put a note on Walter's berth. I knew of course the number of the Pullman he was to take." "Did you see him later?" was Rogan's question.

She hesitated for a moment, then slowly shook her head. "No. You see Henry was killed shortly after, and that changed everything." "This woman," thought Rogan, "is telling the truth."

Why he thought so he could not have told, but he knew he believed her. There was still one question he must ask: "Have you any idea," he queried, “what it was your brothers quarreled over?"

The dark head slowly shook its denial. The low, husky voice informed the chief that she had not the slightest idea as to why her brothers had disagreed. Admitting that it was the chauffeur who had told her they had quarreled, she added that they had often disagreed.

Silence came. In it, Manners, in his turn, studied the woman sitting but a few feet away from him. Yesterday she had been a girl; today she was a woman, and a rather beautiful woman, with grave face, sensitive lips, and searching eyes. A thought swept over him. She was now heiress of a large fortune. He wondered what she would do with it. And, even as he wondered, she spoke, turning this time to the commissioner: "Mr. Stone, I heard my father once say you were not only the leading lawyer in the city but a man who could be trusted. I understand that my father's fortune will come to me; that is, I will have to administer the estate. 

Would you be willing to act for me? I am going to need someone." 

Before the lawyer could answer, there came Oxford's

shrill, protesting voice. The man's face was covetous. "Why, Marjorie, I will be able to help you take care of things. You will need no outside aid."

Gravely she studied him. In her eyes was a strange questioning look, as if she had never seen the little figure before. Seeing it, Manners hoped no woman would ever look at him with that feeling behind her glance. Low and dry was her voice when she replied: "I am afraid, Walter, it will take a great deal more knowledge than you possess to deal with my estate." 

She turned to Stone. "Understand me," she said. "I do not know much about wills and the like. But my father and brothers are dead. 

I am the last of the family. What should I do?"

There were mingled emotions in the police commissioner's heart. The woman before him, appealing in her simplicity, was the heiress of the largest fortune in the state. He remembered his official position, and that, until the case was solved, she might be under suspicion. But his heart went out to her. Forgetting that he was the police commissioner, he replied: "It's hard to say what you should do, Miss Kimball.

If you will come to my office tomorrow I will try to advise you." 

She bowed, then turned to Rogan. "It's absurd, Mr. Rogan, to hold Walter." She hesitated a moment. "After all, he knows nothing about the deaths in my family. It was my message that pulled him off the train. Won't you let him go home?" 

Rogan nodded his head. He might be foolish, but after all there was little doubt that the woman was telling the

truth. He cast a glance at Oxford and his face twisted into a scowl. Why under heaven should any woman like that shrinking figure? With a growl, he said the man might be released.

As Oxford got up from his chair and started for the door, Manners rose also. Not until he was out in the hall, however, did he speak to Marjorie Kimball. At his voice she stopped, her eyes gravely searching his. "Miss Kimball," he began, "in a sense I do not know whether I am not intruding. I know the grief which has come to you. But I wondered— Would you be willing to have lunch with me tomorrow? At my home? I have several things I wish to ask you, questions which may aid in the solving of the horrible tragedy that has come to you."

There came a long hesitation. The woman's eyes, dark and somber, burned into his own, a searching glance, questioning every motive in his heart. At last, suddenly, she smiled, and the smile was like the sun breaking from behind a dark cloud. "I shall be glad to come. Shall we say at one?" And, with that, she went down the hall, Oxford trailing behind her.

Reëntering the room, Manners found that a deep silence had come over Rogan's office. Behind the desk sat the chief, his face still grave and set. Stone was smoking a cigar and did not look up as Manners took his seat.

When Manners himself was about to speak, unable to bear the silence any longer, the door suddenly opened, and Bell came rushing into the room.

There was excitement in the doctor's face, also a look

of triumph. His eyes were shining, his hair in disorder.

He threw a hasty look at Stone and Manners, and, as he approached the desk, he waved his hand at the chief in a wild gesture. "Rogan," came his breathless voice, "Rogan, the autopsy's over. Edward Kimball was poisoned. Ricin. 

Just like his father."

CHAPTER XIII 

It was to be a very special lunch. Manners had insisted that his housekeeper use the finest china and glass, and the rarest silver. The table would be placed on the glassenclosed portion of the veranda, where they could have a view of the lawn and glimpse, far off, the lake. He impressed upon Mrs. McLeod, that it must be a very special affair, for a young lady was to be the guest. The confession caused the housekeeper to raise her eyebrowsyoung ladies were not frequent callers at the house. 

Knowing he would not have to worry about the meal, Manners picked up the morning paper, sliding down in his chair in the library. There was but one item in the paper this morning. The black headline and all but two of the columns on the front page dealt with the same subject. His eyes narrowed as he read them. The city had a real sensation at last. The three men of the Kimball family had been murdered. In hysterical, and fanciful language the paper hinted at obscure motives, unknown assailants. And on the back page was the inevitable editorial, a scathing editorial, scoring the police, demanding if it was not time the department should be placed in charge of someone with intelligence. 

With an ironic smile upon his lips, Manners read the bitter editorial. He knew how it would make Rogan feel and slowly shook his head. Allowing the paper to fall to the floor, he wished fervently its author might be given 199

the task of solving the crimes. Were the job his, never again would he write the vindictive prose just published. 

But Manners was fair enough to admit that in one particular the paper was correct. The police were without knowledge as to why the crimes had been committed. He doubted if they would ever have the knowledge needed to bring about a solution.

It was almost twelve. He had not risen until ten, and the morning was almost over. Walking out through the hall he crossed the long veranda. The table was set, glistening with silver and china; the flowers were from his own garden. Content with what he saw, he hastened back into the house and went into the kitchen to mix the cocktails. The shaker had just been placed upon the ice when he heard the sound of a car.

He met his guest at the foot of the veranda steps. She was dressed in white, wearing what he thought was a rather intriguing, though at the same time foolish, little hat. As he greeted her he realized again, in that first swift glance, that over night Marjorie Kimball had grown up.

There was a deep seriousness behind her grave look, and within the dark eyes a look of pain. Perhaps it was the dress, but she seemed smaller than he had remembered her as being.

He led her into the library, excusing himself for a moment to get the cocktail shaker. Soberly she studied the long room. There were more books here than she had ever seen before in any one place. It was easy to see it was a man's room. Even the prints and etchings upon the wall showed a man's taste. Though she knew little about prints, yet it needed no expert knowledge to see that these

were very valuable. One thing she noticed at once; nowhere in the room was there a woman's picture. 

Manners came hastening through the door. In one hand he balanced two glasses, under his arm was the shaker. 

The glasses were placed upon the desk, the stopper taken from its place. Gravely she watched the yellow liquid poured, accepting the glass he handed her. But when her host tried to persuade her to a second cocktail she shook her head. "One is enough," said the low, husky voice. "You see,

I have not always kept to that resolution, and in the past—" there came a shrug of the slim shoulders-"well, the results have not been enviable."

Dropping into a chair, she opened her bag and found a cigarette. Manners struck the match which was applied to the white tube. For a moment from under her long lashes she looked steadily at him. Then she made an impulsive gesture. "Professor Manners," came her eager voice, “why is it we do such silly things, drink too much, drive our cars too fast and-" Her hand waved vaguely in the air. 

He looked directly into the dark eyes, and the woman met his gaze unflinchingly. As he replied his voice was low. "Oh, all those things are simply means of escape," he replied. "Escape from unhappiness, from despair, from what we call reality. Some people use liquor, others drugs; all of us use something." He waved his hand toward the serried rows of bookcases. "Some of us use books, pictures, music-it's all the same thing." 

She nodded, understandingly.

"Yes," came her musing tone, "I suppose that would explain it. I have been pretty unhappy. And—I am going to make a confession; it's not a pleasant one. I am sorry of course, that my father and brothers are dead." 

She hesitated, then bravely the dark head uplifted. “But there is no sharp, burning pain in my heart. They never tried to make me happy, and I can't, even now, say I am heartbroken."

The appearance of the housekeeper prevented Manners from replying. Luncheon was ready and they went out on the veranda. He placed her chair so that she could look directly over the lawn and observe the beauty of the flowers. As he seated himself he realized that his guest was very weary. There were deep shadows under her fine eyes and she leaned back in her chair as if glad for its support. 

Though he had a reason for inviting her to lunch, he decided it could wait until the meal was over. 

Skillfully he did his best to entertain her, to make her forget, for a little, the shadow which hung over her. Many were the stories he told of famous personages he had met, of obscure corners of Europe, of his four weeks' sail along the coast of Labrador. He had entertained far more distinguished guests, but never had he tried so skillfully to play the part of the perfect host. And, before the meal was over, the woman was at ease, though one remark she dropped gave him an insight into her heart. "You know, professor," she confessed-and a slight blush reddened her cheeks-"I used to look in the society columns of the paper for the names of the guests you had in your home. They were the sort of people I had always wanted to meet-writers, artists, people who were

doing things. I suppose that is one reason I took up with 

Walter." She threw back her head; her cool dark eyes met his frankly. "That was another of my mistakes," she said gravely, "but it's over."

Not until the table had been cleared, the cigarettes lighted, did Manners give her a hint as to what he sought to discover. "Miss Kimball," he at last said slowly, "I think you and I are going to be friends-great friends. And the next time I have a real party-that is, anyone at the house worth knowing-you will be at my table.”

Her eyes lit up as she leaned across the table. "And sometimes you will let me drive up here and look at your books?"

He promptly agreed, making a mental reservation that before she came he would see to it that the contents of certain shelves were removed. Then: "There is no one but yourself, Miss Kimball, who can give me the information I need. I know you will be truthful, but I want you to be very frank. Will you be?"

Her eyes met his. It was a brave look, and she simply nodded. "This question may startle you," he began, "but do you think your brother Henry would have committed murder?" "I have wondered, myself," was the surprising confession. "Henry knew something about poisons. He had a terrific temper-which he displayed mostly upon myself. 

He hated me, for some reason, and yet was afraid of 

Father." She paused, her voice reflective. "We all were.

Ours was not a happy family. Quarrels, bickerings, loud

voices-" she shrugged her shoulders. "All that sort of thing characterized us. But, professor, somehow I cannot bring myself to think Henry would commit murder. First of all, he was a coward. Secondly, he had no capacity for detail. Out in his laboratory he was always fooling around with things-always saying he was going to find a new series of dyes-but he never carried anything through. 

He might feel like killing someone, but I can't think of his accomplishing it." "And Edward?" "Edward?" The question seemed to startle her. Then her face lightened. "Oh, I see what you mean. The arrow -the killing of Henry." She shook her head. "No, I'm sure that's out. Edward, too, had a vile disposition, but at heart he was a harmless soul. And I happen to know he never left the house the evening Henry was killed." "He was at the police station." "Oh, yes. And after that at the dentist's-but I mean after he came home. His wife said he came in the house about fifteen minutes to twelve. Henry, remember, did not go out until after twelve. Edward certainly if his wife told the truth, and I think she did-never left the house after twelve." 

"Your brothers quarreled the night of your father's death?" "Yes. But that was nothing new; they were always quarreling. You see, Henry was jealous because Edward was the elder son. Father's money had mostly been placed in a trust, but there was a lot of property-houses, land, stocks-outside the trust. In his will Father had named Edward as executor, though he was always

threatening to change the will and have Henry appointed. 

In fact, he played that idea back and forth, depending upon which of my brothers he was angry with at the moment. But one thing is certain: I have not the slightest idea why Edward and Henry quarreled that particular night. Unless it was the old family hatred functioningthe dread of letting the public know anything about our private affairs." "I think maybe she has hit it," was Manners' thought. "Rogan won't believe such a simple explanation, but I have an idea it's correct. What's more, this woman is correct. The Kimballs might make life a hell for each other, but they would stop at murder. My first theory was right. To discover the murderer we will have to go outside the family."

Silence had now fallen at the table. Leaning back in her chair, a pensive look on her beautiful face, the guest was glancing down the lawn. She could see the Airedale asleep under the tree, noticed, far in the distance, the lake. "She is beautiful," thought Manners, "and there is character and determination in her face." Suddenly he remembered that she was the heiress of the largest fortune in the state-and the thought moved him to speech: "You will have to take good care of yourself, you know.

There will be great responsibilities, now, upon those shoulders."

Gravely, she studied him. A little smile crossed her sensitive lips. She nodded: "Yes. And it's going to take me a long time to know what to do. One thing is certain. Judge Parrish will never have anything more to do with the Kimballs. I am going

to put myself into Mr. Stone's hands and let him advise me."

She was silent a moment; then a musing note crept into her voice as she said: "There is too much money, far more than I need-or want. I have to discover what can be done with money, not for myself, but for many others. I must first find out who the others are." "Miss Kimball," began Manners, "I am going to make an odd request. Will you give me the privilege during the next few days of sticking my nose into such of your family affairs as I desire?" 

A startled expression crossed his guest's face, then a little smile followed it across her lips. Impulsively, like a child, her hand went across the table to fall upon his own. "We are going to be friends, you said. Why, of course 

I will give you that privilege." "Indeed we are going to be friends." He smiled. “But we must remember that there is one duty that cannot be forgotten: the discovery of the criminal."

She nodded, then, with a glance at her watch, rose hastily to her feet. It was almost two, she reminded him, and she must be back at the house. He walked with her to the low green car she was driving, smiled good-by, and watched the machine sweep out of the drive. He was still shaking his head as he entered the house. Tragedy had placed the heaviest of responsibilities upon that young woman's shoulders. Somehow he had an idea she would meet them.

It was three o'clock when Manners stopped his car before the Star building and ran hurriedly up the stairs

to the editorial rooms. He was lucky enough to catch Gale in his office-though all the editor was doing was matching quarters with the business manager. At Manners' look

Gale dismissed his associate, rose slowly to his feet, closed the door, lit a cigarette and then accorded his visitor a long look. "You want something, of course, Harley. You never come into this joint unless you do want something. Not going to give me the low-down on those three murders?" was the hopeful question. "Nothing like that," was the retort. Pushing aside a pile of newspapers, he seated himself on the corner of the desk. "But there is one thing I do want to ask you. 

You remember telling me the other afternoon that George

Kimball received a large number of abusive letters? That he came in here and read them to you? Now, a man who would do that might also keep them. How about it?"

Gale twisted a leg over the arm of his chair and laughed as he said: "He did just that. In fact, you may not believe me, but he kept back files of them for a year. Every year he tabulated the anonymous letters he had received, and always felt a bit peeved if they were not up to the previous year. How's that for a complex?" "Where did he keep them?" "In that penthouse or whatever he called it-on top of his Alpha Life building. You know he had a threeroom place up there. I never was in it but once. But he used to retire there occasionally and spend an evening going through the papers and things he had saved. You'll find all the letters he had received this year up there

now." He paused-remembering he was an editor. "But why the devil do you want to know about those letters?" 

Shaking his head, Manners dropped off his desk, said good-by, and went off down the street. Climbing into his car, he drove it to a near-by parking station. He walked two blocks and came to a halt before the entrance of the 

Alpha Life Insurance building. It was the tallest office structure in the city; twenty-two stories high. It had been the delight of George Kimball's heart when built, and from a financial point of view had proved the most successful bit of property he owned. In the lobby, Manners studied the long list of rooms and their tenants. Several of the floors were occupied by the insurance company, the others were general offices.

Taking the elevator to the tenth floor, he stopped, upon alighting, before an office door. The glass bore but one word, "PRIVATE," and without knocking he pushed the door open and entered. It was a plainly furnished room and seemed empty. As he closed the door, however, a man came hurriedly from a connecting room, a tall man, well dressed, and perhaps fifty years old. For a moment he seemed astonished to find anyone in the room; then a smile crossed his face. George Kimball's private secretary had met Harley Manners on many occasions. 

Brushing aside the words of greeting, Manners went at once to the object of his visit. "What I want, Cross," he said, "is a little information. 

You, of course, will not need to see my police card. First, did George Kimball keep any sort of a day-book, a book in which he put down his engagements?" "Oh, yes. He was very particular in such matters."

"I want to see the book."

The secretary hesitated, then, with a glance at the professor's grave face, nodded and led him into the other room. It was small, with every inch filled. Steel cabinets lined the walls, a stand occupied one corner, and a typewriter was on the edge of the small desk which stood near the window. Pulling open a drawer, the secretary fumbled for a moment: then his hand came forth with a large red book. Placing it on a desk he courteously drew up a chair. 

As Manners seated himself, he gave a hasty glance out of the window. It was a corner office and there were two windows, one gave on a view of the lake, the other, on the hills behind the city. Only for a moment did he gaze downward, then he bent above the engagement book. 

What he wished to see were the engagements for the ten days which had preceded Kimball's death.

Each day's notations in the book filled a complete page. 

They were in two handwritings, persumably that of Kimball himself and the secretary. The pages were filled up to the day before the man's death. Every hour, from early morning until five o'clock in the afternoon seemed to have been taken. Engagements of all sorts-interviews and appointments; and the names written beside the hours were those of well-known business and financial leaders of the city. What he had hoped to find he did not know; but when the book was thrust aside, he decided he had failed of any discovery.

Leaning back in the chair, he stared gloomily down at the book. Then a thought struck him. He picked it up again and went back to the tenth day before the man's

death. That was the day the first telephone call had come to Rogan. Entry by entry his eyes ran down the page.

Nothing of importance, he thought, as he rapidly turned the other pages. Only one appointment seemed conspicuous because of its unvarying hour for five successive days. 

Kimball had gone to see his dentist daily for five days and always at twelve o'clock. The oddness of the hour was what appealed to Manners. Well, there was nothing of further interest in the book. It contained simply the hurried engagements and conferences of a busy man. 

Pushing it aside he turned to Cross. "Cross, I understand Mr. Kimball had a penthouse on the roof of the building.' 

"Yes, he did," was the answer. "It was a sort of a storehouse for odds and ends he did not care to take home with him. Occasionally he used to spend his evenings there."
