"Have you the key?" was the question. "Yes," Cross admitted, though his tone expressed reluctance. "Let me have it," was the command. 

The secretary threw him a doubtful look. Not until

Manners reminded him of his police card, adding Miss Kimball's promise that he should be granted any favors he desired, did the secretary go to a file and produce a flat key. Thanking him, Manners went out to the corridor. 

He hesitated a moment before the elevator. Kimball, in life, had been a boastful, talkative man, proud of his business deals, and ready to talk to anyone who would listen to him. He remembered the notation in the engagement book. Five times he had been to his dentist in the days preceding his death. If there was one thing certain,

it was that during those hours he had sat in the chair Kimball must have talked-if it had been physically possible for him to do so. He made a sudden resolution. After all, the dentist was in this very building; he would drop in and see him. 

Up three floors he went, getting out on the thirteenth to walk down a long hall. Pausing a second before the glass door which bore the name Doctor William Langfelt, he smiled. The man was the leader in his profession in the city, an odd stick personally, but a remarkably skillful practitioner. He pushed open the door and walked into a white reception room. The nurse, who appeared a second later from another room, to whom he gave his name, said the doctor could see him at once.

He was ushered into the consulting room. Only a moment did he have to wait. Through the door came the tall dentist. No one had ever accused Langfelt of being goodlooking. His face was stern and austere that of a man who worked constantly beyond his physical capacity. Behind large glasses his dark eyes studied his visitor. In a moment came the voice harsh and hurried. "No, doctor," laughed Manners in reply, "I am glad to say I do not need your professional services. I came on another matter. I understand that for five days, shortly before his death, George Kimball was your patient. Now, to be very frank, knowing how well the man loved to talk, I am wondering if, during the time he was your patient, you observed him to be worried, upset over anything."

The doctor's eyes opened in astonishment. It was clear the question was unexpected, and for a moment Manners thought him a trifle angry. Then, with an abrupt laugh,

the dentist replied: "The Kimballs have been patients of mine for years. 

I think the only thing that bothered Mr. Kimball was the ulcerated tooth for which he was being treated. I was removing the nerve and it's a rather painful operation. But he seemed not different to me than at other times I had seen him. I am afraid I am unable to aid you." He started hurriedly toward his operating room. "I am afraid you will have to excuse me," he added, "for I have a patient."

It was failure, thought Manners, as he again waited for the the elevator, but he smiled as he thought of Langfelt. 

An odd stick who within the last year had suddenly become a religious leader of sorts. But a fine dentist, he admitted, as he stepped into the elevator and commanded: "The roof."

He had to climb a short flight of stairs before emerging on the roof. The Alpha Insurance building was the highest one in the city. Its roof was pebbled, and a small, low coping ran around the edge. Before him, directly in the center of the roof, rose a low, one-story, wooden building-a penthouse of sorts. It had a few windows and, directly opposite him, a door.

He stood for a moment taking in the view over the roof edge. The lake was a shimmering mass of silver, and far over the water he could distinguish an uneven, dark line, the shore line of Canada. Turning about he looked back toward the hills, a circle of green enclosing the city. 

Everywhere were houses, and far overhead an airplane, like a silver dragonfly, droned on its way to the lake. A slight breeze was blowing, whipping at his coat, causing him to push his hat tighter down on his head. Then, key

in hand, he approached the penthouse door.

There was no difficulty in unlocking it. In fact it was 

Manners' thought that almost any key would have worked. Pushing it open he stepped into the room, and let the door close behind him. It was small-about sixteen feet square-cluttered with boxes and old bookcases. A worn table stood close to a window. Beyond, he could glimpse another room, and, stepping over the floor, he looked within. A couch furnished it, along with two chairs and more bookcases. Beyond this he guessed there was a third room, but he did not look into it. 

Lighting his pipe, he turned, instead, to the contents of the bookcases. They contained mostly old records; the volumes when opened revealing the fact they had been used as scrapbooks. The contents were yellow with age: the faded newspaper stories of the past. They all dealt with the same subject-some activity of George Kimball -items telling of his business career, mentioning a new piece of property he had acquired. Book after book was pulled from the cases and all were devoted to the same subject, clipped items which treated of George Kimball. 

Despite himself, Manners found the faded clippings interesting, and on the surface they told the story of a hard, ruthless man, seeking his own interests always, driving from one success to another. But to Manners they told another and hidden story. The scores of scrapbooks, the careful manner in which the clippings had been arranged and dated, told of long hours spent in arranging; they spoke also of the man's pride in what he had done. 

He thrust the books aside at last. After all, they were not for what he was looking. Boxes were next examined,

the contents rapidly searched and the box dismissed. At last he found what he was after. Under the table in a large pasteboard box lay a mass of letters: letters written on the poorest sort of paper, atrocious in spelling and handwriting: letters in which unknown writers had frankly told their opinions of the city's richest man. 

He dumped the contents of the box on the table and, pulling up a chair, began to examine them minutely. In a sense they were all alike. Vicious, sarcastic-all of them expressed clearly one thing: that the writer had hated

George Kimball. Two hundred or more in all, and without a doubt written by moronic, illiterate persons. In the end he replaced them all but six. The six he left on the table interested him.

To begin with they were the only letters in the box which had been written on a typewriter; the paper was of good quality, each letter being one-half of a sheet of correspondence paper. The lettering was black and sharp, evidently made by a new ribbon, and the message of four of the letters was identical: "You have been responsible for the deaths of hundreds of persons. Some day you will pay."

This was repeated in four letters. As he glanced at them he saw they bore no date. It was impossible to say when they had been written. Gale had told him it was Kimball's habit to keep the letters received during the current year. 

This would mean that these had been written during the last seven months. The fifth and the sixth, however, though written by the same typewriter, bore a different message. "Because of you, hundreds have taken their lives. Some

time you will pay for this." 

Manners' brow knit. "Hundreds have taken their lives," because of Kimball. It was an absurd statement. But as he gazed at the white paper, glimpsed the sharp black letters, he was certain of one thing: the writer of those letters believed what he had written. Why he believed it was something Manners would have to try to discover. 

A sound caused his head to suddenly lift. It came from the door, a slight noise, as if an unseen individual were walking over the pebbled roofing. Rising from the chair he darted to the door. His hand, falling upon the knob, gave it a quick pull. The door would not open. He had left the key in the lock when he entered, and the door ajar. Now it was closed. Someone had locked him in the penthouse.

CHAPTER XIV 

WITH his hand resting upon the unyielding knob of the door, Manners' glance went over to the windows. There were four, two on each side of the room. None of them faced the door which led down from the roof. The windows, offering easy exit from the penthouse, made one thing very clear. There had only been one object in locking him in. He had left the key outside when he entered the room; someone had crept across the roof, locked the door and departed with the key. Who it might be, and the object, he could not for the moment see.

Cross, who for years had been Kimball's private secretary, had known he was coming to the penthouse-in fact, the man had given him the key. No one else had known his destination, unless it was the elevator boy. He tried to remember. Had the elevator started its descent before he walked up the short flight of stairs? He decided that it had; in fact, remembered seeing the shadow in the shaft as it moved downward.

Going back to the table he looked down again upon the six letters he had been examining. With a sudden resolution his hand reached out for them, thrust them into an inside pocket. Then, walking over to the nearest window he threw back the catch and raised the sash. The sill was only about three feet from the roof, and it was an easy matter to step outside. As his feet struck the uneven surface, he threw a hurried glance across the roof. He saw 216

no one-he had not expected to. 

Going round to the front of the penthouse he looked at the door. He had left the key in the lock when he entered; there was no key in it now. Whoever had locked the door had vanished with the key. Why the mysterious visitor wanted it was a question Manners would have given a great deal to be able to answer.

When he reached the floor just under the roof his finger pressed the button for the elevator. To his right were the stairs, and they would go winding down floor after floor to the street level. The colored elevator boy was positive that no one but Manners himself had come up to the twenty-second floor. Though it was a double shaft, only one elevator ran to the top of the building. No other passenger had been to the twenty-second floor during the time the boy had been on duty. As he stepped out at the street level Manners smiled. He had not expected the taker of the key to use the elevator.

It was getting dusk as he stepped out of the building and the street lights had just come on. He had an arrangement with his housekeeper that if he did not communicate with her before four o'clock she was not to expect him home to dinner. For a moment he played with the idea of going to the police station, and, with a sudden resolution, decided to take Rogan to dinner. The chances were the chief could not recall at what hour he had eaten his last meal.

When he pushed open the door of Rogan's office and looked within, he glimpsed the chief behind a cloud of blue smoke. The white head was bent over the desk, evidently studying a report; the startled way in which he

threw a glance across the floor told that he had been lost in a cloud of thought. His lips broke into a smile when he saw who his visitor was, and, pushing back the papers before him, he turned round in his chair. "I am taking you to dinner, Tim," was Manners' greeting.

There was a feeble protest. Under his friend's questioning, the chief mildly admitted that breakfast had been his last meal. But not until he had been threatened with forcible eviction from the room did he consent to leave his desk; and, even after his hat had been jammed down on his head, he paused by his desk to throw a last regretful look about him.

Manners took him to his club, and for the second night in succession insisted upon one of the small private dining rooms. Purposely he led the conversation away from the 

Kimballs. The man who sat across from him was tired, and for a few moments he wished to direct his thoughts away from his work. Not until the dinner was ended and 

Rogan had lighted a cigar of Manners' choice—the finest in the club was a word of shop spoken. "Well, Tim?" then came the question.

Reflectively the chief studied the tip of his long Havana. "It's a fine cigar," was his thought, "the best Harley can buy, and I ought to be grateful. Yet I must admit I'd much rather be smoking one of my stogies. Still-I suppose it would never do to pull out one of those cheap things in here." He felt a bit rested. He had not wished to admit, even to himself, how weary he had been when 

Manners pulled him out to dinner. With a start he realized that his friend had just asked him a question.

"Today?" he replied. "Well, you know of course, the autopsy showed that Edward had been poisoned—the same poison which killed his father. But Hoff, for the life of him, can't see how it was given. There's not a mark on the man's body, and he has been over it again and again. "Harley," went on the weary voice, "one thing keeps bothering me. Are we mixed up with one crime or two?

Of course the death of two can be laid to the same door. 

But how about Henry? That damned arrow. Are we dealing with two murderers or one-one crime or two different crimes, whose motives are far apart? For the life of me I cannot make up my mind.”

Manners had made up his mind. Earnestly he insisted that the three deaths sprang from one and the same motive. What it might be, he admitted he had not the slightest theory. But he was positive they would discover that in reality it was one crime, three victims, and the same murderer. "So we will discover that!" replied Rogan with a touch of sarcasm in his voice. "But tell me, why was not Henry poisoned? Why use such a silly weapon as an arrow?" "Because, though it was possible to kill two of them by poison, for some reason the murderer was unable to administer it to Henry. That's the reason for the arrow; that and nothing else." 

Rogan looked down through the open window. The main street of the city lay before his eyes, filled with the crowds hurrying to the theaters. He pondered over what 

Manners had just said. No doubt it was correct. Then, turning, he glanced sharply across the table, as an arm swept forth in an expressive gesture.

"Well, all day long I have been wondering. Is this case a set-off? Henry killing his father and brother, Edward bumping off Henry? It could be that. If so, you have two murderers, each the victim of the other." He paused, to add reflectively: "I have never heard of such a thing, but it would have been possible."

Manners shook his head. Briefly he told the chief of his conversation with Majorie Kimball. There might have been bad feeling in the family, individuals with hot, uncontrollable tempers, but Rogan was assured it was hardly possible any of them would, or could, have committed the crimes. His insistence upon this only caused Rogan to shake his head gloomily: "Then we are up against a criminal who has not made the slightest mistake."

But Manners maintained that viewpoint was absurd. 

The murderer had made a mistake-a very serious one. 

Heatedly the chief asked what it was. "The telephone calls," came the answer. "There is where our criminal made the great mistake.

If there had been no calls, I doubt if we would have ordered an autopsy; without it, no one would ever have known anything about the poison." "That may be true so far as the poison is concerned," was the retort, "but what about Henry's death? It was plain enough that was murder.” "That's all true. Yet I insist that, if the murderer had not called you on the phone, had not indulged in warning his victims, we would not have known anything about the poison deaths—and, furthermore, Henry would not have been murdered at all. When he called Henry, the mur-

derer was becoming alarmed over the other calls he had made, for he realized two things." "What two?" "First: that he had made a bad mistake in using the telephone. Secondly: that he was afraid Edward would remember his voice. You recall Edward told us the voice sounded familiar." 

Rogan nodded. "I conceive that after calling Edward the murderer began to realize what he had done. Edward might conceivably identify his voice. Recall how he later phoned Henry telling him he would inform him who had been calling his father. Is it conceivable it was Edward who did that?

Even if for a moment you had any theory that he killed his brother, Henry would have known his brother's voice.

Also, he would have gone directly to Edward's house if his rendezvous had been with him—not to the shore. No, Rogan, the murderer made his great mistake when he undertook to use the telephone."

Refraining from any comment, Rogan began to recount the day's work. Evidently everyone on the Kimball estate had been interviewed, with no results. No one had ever seen a steel arrow upon the place, or even, for that matter, heard of one. Edward's wife had stated plainly that her husband had come into the house shortly before twelve on the night of his father's death, had told her he had seen the police chief and the dentist and had not again gone out. 

Hoff had repeated his examination of the laboratory and, though he had discovered a weird and curious mixture of chemicals, with some poisons, had not found the slightest sign of ricin. Rogan admitted he was at a standstill. Only

one thing he reported was new. Marjorie Kimball had that morning ordered Judge Parrish off the grounds. This information the chief gave with a smile. "Well, there is still Oxford," grinned Manners.

Rogan made a grimace. He admitted there was. Yet no doubt remained to him that the woman had told the truth. 

A message had been sent to Oxford at the train, and the man had gotten off. A Pullman porter remembered seeing the ticket: recalled that, when the train started, the man was not on board. And since, after the sending of the note, tragedy had stalked into the Kimball grounds,

Oxford naturally had not seen its writer, but had taken the six-thirty morning train to New York. "He is out of it," was Rogan's positive statement. 

With a start, the chief stopped to glance hastily at his watch and gave a startled expression as he saw the time. 

Rising to his feet he informed Manners he must hurry back to the office. It was his expectation that the professor would come with him; but, upon reaching the street, he was told his friend would see him in the morning. The two men then said good-night and went off in opposite directions. 

Manners drove slowly home. He was in no hurry, and, besides, something was bothering him. Try as he would, he could not make the suggestion come clearly out of his subconscious-but there was something he ought to remember, some odd statement he had heard, either today or in the past two days, that was of the greatest importance. He was psychologist enough to know it was useless to try to force his mind. Sooner or later the hidden fact would leap out into the open. Until then there was noth-

ing he could do.

He was glad to sit down at last in his library. The past three days had been filled with excitement. Putting on only the shaded lamp upon the desk, he sank down in his big chair and slowly lighted his pipe. A little smile crossed his lips as his eyes traveled peacefully over the room. Behind the long bookcases, gay-colored volumes were waiting to be opened. On the desk were the late magazinesthe popular ones and beside them, the high pile of technical journals which he read. But he was not going to look at them. Inside his pocket were six letters which he was eager to examine minutely.

Before taking them from his pocket, he opened the drawer of his desk and brought forth a magnifying glass. It was of exceptional strength-and he knew he would need it. The six letters were next taken from his pocket, and for a long time his eyes searched the white paper. Again and again the glass was used, hovering for whole minutes over certain individual letters. When at last, both glass and letters were carefully placed in his desk he had reached several conclusions. 

First: there was little doubt the letters had been written by someone of education. The spacing was correct, the spelling and punctuation perfect. Secondly: the typewriter had been new. It would not be very difficult to recognize other letters written upon the same machine. 

He knew that the type of every machine is individual, that, even though it were a new and almost unused machine, it would show certain well-defined peculiarities. 

And such a peculiarity had been found in the six letters. 

All had been written on the same machine-a machine

which had just the slightest imperfection in the letter "e." 

With his head thrown back, he tried to puzzle out the warnings. How could hundreds of persons have lost their lives because of anything George Kimball had done? 

Then he remembered the two letters in which the warning had varied-without a doubt, though undated, the last two written. In these the charge had been changed. Hundreds had "taken their lives" because of Kimball. For a long while he puzzled over this. Then, suddenly, with a leap, he was out of his chair and rushing for the telephone in the hall. As he dialed the number, he suspicioned his ring would be useless. His friend, the general counsel for the Alpha Life Insurance Company, was hardly ever in the city, and when he was, never could be located at home. 

To his surprise, in a moment there came in reply the deep, slow voice. "Joe," he began, "this is Manners. I want some information. I suppose during this time of depression the suicide rate among your policy holders is going up. Is that true?" "Yes, Harley, it is," came the surprised voice. "But

God knows you already knew that. It's up almost fifty percent-perhaps even more. Last year they say, though the figures are not as yet tabulated, the insurance companies paid out forty million dollars more in suicide claims than ever before in their history. But why do you want to know all this?" "Never mind. Now answer another question. "Can you think of any of your policy holders in the city—I mean, someone with a large amount of insurance, who committed suicide within the last ten months or so?"

There was a moment's hesitancy before the delightful voice replied: "I can't. We have had a few in the city, mostly small claims. Not any with much insurance. And again, what are you after?" "Another question, Joe, or rather, a request. Will you, tomorrow morning, as soon as you reach your office, get me the names and addresses of the policies your company has paid out in the last ten months, in which the cause of death was suicide-all of them?".

His friend began to protest. "Do you know what you are asking me to do?" he began. "Certainly," was the quick reply. "And listen, Joe, you don't want me marching in there with Rogan and Kent and all the police, do you? Besides, Miss Kimball says that any information I need about anything connected with her family I am to have." 

He heard a chuckle from his friend. Then came the understanding voice: "All right. You know what you are after. Good luck to you."

Back in the library he paused by the table to look at the pile of magazines. Then he darted out to the kitchen. 

He knew now it was going to be a long night. Returning, he placed a bottle on the desk, a glass beside it-and hurriedly he began to run through the magazines. Which was the one containing that article on suicide? After much searching he found it.

Before he read the four pages of fine print, the glass was partly filled, a sip taken, then he settled back in the

chair to give his attention to the article. It was a lengthy technical discussion upon suicide, read as a paper before the "Convention of Life Insurance Executives." It bristled with graphs and figures, and at the bottom of each page was a mass of footnotes, printed in a type so fine that Manners wished for a moment he could express his opinion to the editor who had allowed such print. But so absorbing was the article that he read it through a second time, then went over it again. Perhaps he was the only person who so honored the article. 

It presented what had become a new and grave problem in the insurance world. It dealt with the suicides of men who found that only by taking their lives would they be able to provide for their families. The story revealed by the mass of statistics was an appalling and pathetic thing. 

Since 1929 the suicide rate had been going upward, slowly at first, then with a sudden leap during the past twenty months. Behind the sharp lines of the graphs, the tabulated rows of figures, lay stories of human suffering, tragedy and despair. Manners' lips became a tense straight line as he read. Perhaps no other reader of that article ever saw what lay behind it as did the psychologist. 

Twelve thousand suicides in 1930. And then the graph for 1931 and 1932 moved sharply upward. He could picture men who had seen their business swept away by the depression, bewildered, heartsick, broken men. He thought of the sleepless nights they had spent, the hours of anguish. There would be days of despair, vain endeavors to find some way in which they could support their families. 

And then would come the desperate thought of their insurance policies, policies which contained no clause

against suicide. At first the sinister idea would be swept aside, but every hour it would return, until there came the time when it remained ever present. Then-some dayin order to wrest security for those they left behind, these individuals would slip out of life. The thought made him heartsick.

There came the moment when the magazine was placed on the desk. He reached out for the glass and drained its contents. The pipe, which he had allowed to go out, was relighted. Again the letters were taken from the drawer, but this time it was only the last two which were examined. Replacing them, he rose and went over to one of the bookcases. The volumes which were brought to the desk were large and technical. Only for a few moments did he glance at them, and then it was but to look at the table of contents. At last he leaned back and began to think.

It might be so. A fantastic theory was beginning to form in his mind. It seemed the only logical explanation for the last two letters. They had been anonymous, written as a warning to Kimball, and Manners had no doubt the man had, never known what they were really about. "Hundreds have taken their lives because of you."

Could the unknown writer have been referring to the suicides? Kimball was the head of a great insurance company; many policy holders had committed suicide. Was that what the writer had meant by the words: "Because of you hundreds have taken their lives"? He suddenly decided that the words could mean nothing else.

But that raised a new question. Was the writer of those letters the same individual who had made the telephone

calls? If so, he was inevitably the murderer. But there was still another fact to be explained. There had been six letters. The first four charged Kimball with the death of hundreds of individuals. The presumably-last two said these persons had "taken" their own lives—suicide, of course. Why had the man-for he had no doubt it was a man-suddenly stopped writing letters and begun to use the telephone? And when had he stopped writing? At the thought he rose to his feet and once more hurried to the phone.

It was the Star he called this time, insisting that he must speak to Gale. In a moment there came the drawling voice of the editor, and when the latter discovered who was calling he simply said: "Still at it?" "Listen, Gale," came the request. "You told me Kimball had the habit of reading to you the anonymous letters he received. Do you remember his reading to you two which said: 'Hundreds have killed themselves because of you'?" "Sure I do. The old man and I wondered at the time what it was all about. Kimball said he had lots of sins on his soul but so far as he knew he had never caused anyone's death. Do you know what they meant?" 

Manners thrust the inquiry aside. "Gale and it's very important-when did he read them to you?" "Oh, I'd say about two weeks before he died," drawled the voice. Suddenly the import of the questions seemed to hit the editor. His voice rose. "Harley," he began. But 

Manners had rung off.

Standing again by his desk Manners' eyes narrowed as he looked down at the magazine whose article he had just been reading. Slowly he poured a little liquid; it would be his second drink. When he drained it he did not even taste the smoky Scotch. His theory was falling into place, fact after fact coming in proper order. He lit his pipe and reseated himself.

The letters had ended about two weeks before Kimball was killed. That would be only a few days before the first telephone call had been made to Rogan. He reviewed each telephone call for a moment. They had been, every one, a mistake the murderer's great mistake. Clearly at the time the first call had been made the criminal was certain of one thing: some time during the next few days he would be able to poison his victim. The period for writing letters was over; the time for action had arrived. No other explanation would fit the facts. Though he looked at them from every angle, there was nothing else he could see.

One problem, however, had still to be seriously considered. If his present theory was wrong, he knew that until his dying day he would regret it. Was Marjorie 

Kimball in any danger? The men in her family had been killed, and she was the only direct descendant left. Would the murderer in his frenzied rage seek to make her the next victim? But upon his theory he based a new theory that there was now no danger for her. So far as the Kimball family was concerned it was over. The men-yesbut they had all held important positions in the insurance company. If his theory as to the motive of the crimes, his conception of the type of individual who had com-

mitted them, was correct, the woman was in no danger. 

The murderer would not be interested in her at all. 

The type of individual- He realized now that he had been correct at the very beginning. It was an educated person, whose mind had gone off on a wild tangent. How to find this individual might still be difficult, but he was certain now that it would be accomplished. And then, all at once, into his mind there leaped the thought he had been trying to call up all evening. So astounding was its implication, so incredible its inference, that the pipe fell from between his teeth, to go rolling across the floor. 

His hands clenched, his face whitened. Very slowly he rose and for the third time went to the telephone.

It was Hoff who answered his ring this time, a sleepy 

Hoff, with a voice rough and angry at being disturbed.

Discovering who was speaking, however, he growled out a short greeting. "Hoff, tell me," came Manners' words, "you have been unable so far to discover how the ricin was given the two Kimballs?" "Hell! Harley," came the disgusted retort. "You know we have not discovered how it was given. We have searched the bodies, looked everywhere. And still we don't know." "It had to be either by hypodermic or through the tissue fluids?" was the second question. "Of course," came the barked-out reply. "But you know that." Then something in the tone of Manners' voice caused Hoff to become excited. "Are you going to tell me at midnight that you know how it was administered?"

The pause seemed endless. So long was it indeed that 

Hoff wondered if Manners would ever speak. Then his voice came floating over the wire: "Yes, I know. There was only one way it could have been given. But I am not going to tell you how-yet."

CHAPTER XV 

It was not Manners' habit to attend lectures, but as he was dressing, after his bath, he thought of a visiting English poet he had once heard who had gone into a rhapsody over the beauties of early rising. He had not agreed with him then, nor did he now, at six o'clock in the morning. It had been long after two when he retired, having filled sheet after sheet of paper with the notes he knew he must make. Now, with a very active day ahead, he was anxious to reread what he had written, make some toast and coffee and set off for the city.

The dog followed him down to the front door and was allowed to go out into the sunshine. Manners stopped on his way to the kitchen long enough to pick up the sheets of paper on the desk; then, the gas lighted, the coffee put over the flame, he found the bread and put the toaster to work. Keeping an eye upon the clock he tried to recall just how long it took to prepare coffee. In the end his results were fairly satisfactory; and, with two slices of toast before him, he sat down before a small table with the notes.

While eating, he slowly read what he had written the previous evening. Sheet after sheet of paper had been filled, gaps occurring on some pages where there would be but a single word jotted down, or merely a quaint little figure he had drawn. But every sentence, all the words and the weird figures had a meaning. To no one 232

but Manners would they have made sense. But, if his theory was correct, these words and figures contained not only the motive for the murders but, what was more, the method by which they had been committed and the name of the murderer. 

Could he prove his theory? True, there were connecting links which must be filled in, new facts to be discovered; but that it would be very difficult to find these facts he rather doubted. What promised to be the difficult thing was the welding of his suspicions into one great overwhelming fact, a fact so clear that everyone would see it as correct. 

Pushing aside the plate, he lighted his first cigarette of the morning. There was an active day ahead, one which would be mentally filled with uncertainty and perplexity. 

And when night came- That was what bothered him the most. Reflectively he glanced down at the table, thoughtfully gazing once more at the sheets of paper. If he was correct, another case was rapidly drawing to a conclusion. 

During the day he would have to fit the puzzle into a perfect whole. But as he rose, he knew it could be done. 

He paused in the hall to make a telephone call. Then, hastening to the garage, he threw open the doors and climbed into his blue roadster. A moment later he was descending the hill. 

It was a beautiful morning, though behind the bright sunshine lay a hint of a storm. As he went sweeping around the curves, his thoughts were far from happy. 

There were only two things about crime which interested him; his mind was invariably intrigued by the two questions which must always be answered: "Why" and

"How." He was satisfied, now, that he had discovered the answer to both these questions, and in doing so had lost his interest. The end of a case, the arrest of the criminal had always been, to him, distasteful. This one, he knew, would be even more displeasing to him than the average.

Reaching the city line he turned to go down the avenue leading past the University. He reached the campus and had just skirted past Hoff's laboratory when he suddenly brought his car to a stop. Across from him, its sides covered with ivy, was a little church, and in the yard, bending over a flower garden was a white-haired man. The sight of the clergyman had brought a question into Manners' mind-one he could not answer for himself.

He crossed the street and pushed open the gate and stepped upon the lawn. It was a small bit of lawn, vividly green and containing three flower beds. Not until he was almost behind the stooping figure did the man sense his visitor's presence. Rising, he turned then, a happy smile breaking over his kindly face, as, with hands outstretched he greeted him: "Well, Harley, who would ever expect to see you at this hour in the morning?" 

Manners smiled. He had known the clergyman since his boyhood days. His church was small-though it was the most liberal one in the city-and it was only on very rare occasions that Manners had honored it with his presence. But the white-haired man who smiled at him was without doubt the intellectual leader of the city. Attend church Manners did not, but many had been the evenings when, before his fireplace, the two men had

settled the problems of the universe. "Doctor Wright," he began, "I want to ask you a question. Do we have many Mennonites in the city?" 

The clergyman gave a little chuckle. "And you're even getting interested in theology," was his sly thrust. "We do have a few. It would be very odd if we did not. For, after all, there are more followers of that sect in this state than in any other; and of course a few drift away from the farms and come into the city. 

They even have a small city church. It has been established during the last two years." "Do any of our better-known citizens belong?" "One or two. You see, it's rather the rule that if one is brought up in one of those rigid sects, and breaks away —that is, goes into a profession-one is apt to drop all one's early religious background. But there are several persons whom you know in the professional world interested in the little church. You know Stevens, the lawyer?

And there is Doctor Langfelt, and one or two others. It's a very small church, however, Harley, very small indeed. 

But why all this interest in that obscure sect?" 

Shaking his head, Manners lingered for a few moments.

There were roses which he was told he must see, also the Doctor had just read a new work upon witchcraft, in which, he urged, his guest would be interested. In truth, Manners would have enjoyed spending the entire morning in the garden; but there was work for him to do. 

Pressing three of his finest cigars into the hand which bade him good-by, he walked back to his car. He was very thoughtful when he sat down on the leather seat, and for a moment he postponed starting the motor.

It was just eight o'clock when he finally entered the police station. Rogan's door was closed and he doubted very much if the chief was in his room. At the end of the hall was a door which bore the words Homicide Department. Pushing this open Manners came into a square room which contained but one window. There was little furniture, only a filing cabinet, two chairs and a small desk. Behind the desk George Kent raised his eyes to see who his visitor might be.

He watched the professor pull a chair close to his own. 

The inspector had always stood a little bit in awe of this friend of Rogan's. As he glimpsed the stern face now, he was confident of one thing. Something was up. He recalled other occasions when this tall man had pulled a chair close to his own, and then in a low voice mentioned a conclusion to which he had come. How Manners managed to reach these conclusions of his was a mystery Kent had never been able to fathom. But as he took in the well-pressed gray suit, noticed the expensive tie, glanced at the calm face, he was convinced something was up. "Kent," came the low voice, "there are a good many things you will have to do for me today. I want them kept away from Rogan. I do not want the old man bothered until I am certain." "Certain?" was the amazed inquiry. "Yes, I have a theory, and it seems to fit. Later in the day I will be able to put it all together and let you and 

Rogan both know what it is. I will wish to see you at noontime. Now, this is what I want. You are to have one of your men, by hook or by crook, have two lines written upon a certain typewriter. Make certain that in those

two lines he hits the letter "e" again and again." 

Leaning over the desk he suddenly whispered a name to Kent. It caused the inspector to push his chair back suddenly from the desk and start to rise. "For God's sake, Manners, you don't think-" began his horrified voice. "Never mind mentioning the name, Kent. Just do what 

I say. Send one of your men-in plain clothes, of course -and have him get a message on that individual's typewriter. Then there is another thing-and you'd better do this yourself. I want, by noontime, every single item you can dig up about this man-his sports; his clubs, if any; what he does; how he spends his time. Get it all.” "But Manners," came the protest. 

The inspector received a long, warning glance. Then

Manners continued, his voice low and thoughtful. The officer was told there was a chance the professor's theory might be correct. He was reminded that, on the other hand, it would take the strongest kind of proof to convict the individual. In fact, Manners was doubtful if any jury would convict him, even if all the facts fell into place. Leaning across the desk Kent was informed that, for the present, the murderer was harmless; his obsession against the Kimballs had been gratified. But, and herein was the danger, no one knew when the twisted mind might center upon others as victims who deserved death. That was the danger. And the sooner the police were able to act, the better for the safety of the city.

He left a very thoughtful police official behind him when he walked out of the station. There were many places he must go, and he drove first to a bookshop. Here

he was lucky enough to find the proprietor in, and in a moment had made clear to him what he desired. There was only one work upon toxicology dealing at any length with the poisons employed to kill the Kimballs. Had anyone bought this title in the last few months? He was told that two had been sold-both had gone to doctors whom the bookseller named.

Out of the bookstore he hastened across the street to a sporting-goods store. Here his questions were similarly brief. Had they ever carried in stock any steel arrows?

The reply was that they had not, and the clerk volunteered the information that, so far as he knew, no one in the city had ever used them. Then, hesitating a moment, he added that to his knowledge only one individual had ever inquired about steel arrows.

Climbing back into his car, Manners sat thinking over what he had just heard. Under his questioning the clerk had managed to remember the name of the individual who had inquired about steel arrows. It had happened some months ago; and it was the clerk's impression that the man had walked off with a sporting-goods' catalogue, which contained the address of the firm making the new arrows. He thought he had done so; he was not positive. 

Requesting a similar catalogue, Manners thanked the clerk and left the store. 

His next stop was in front of the Alpha Life Insurance building, but, before taking the elevator, he stepped into the Western Union Office and sent a telegram. It might be a vain effort, yet every clue was worth running down. 

He stepped out of the elevator at the fifteenth floor.

There was a long corridor to be traversed, many open

doors to be passed. At the end of the hall was a door bearing two words upon its frosted glass, LEGAL DEPART- MENT. The first room to which this gave access was an office where two stenographers seemed to be doing nothing more than reading the morning paper. One glanced up as he entered and, after a moment, deigned to ask him what he wanted. With a little shake of his head he walked past her, to push open the door which bore the name of his friend.

Behind a large mahogany desk reading a voluminous brief, sat the attorney for the insurance company. His eyes glanced over the top of the brief; then, seeing who had interrupted him, he flung down the document upon the desk. Whirling about in his chair, the lawyer pulled open a drawer and out of it produced a number of sheets of paper. "Your request made a devil of a lot of work, Harley," he growled. "But here is the list of all of our policy holders who have committed suicide during the last ten months.

It so happened we had a partial list on file; otherwise it would have taken a week to get it made. Now, why under heaven do you want it?"

Sinking down in a chair, Manners slowly shook his head, as he lighted a cigarette. "That's my business, Joe," was his retort as he took the typewritten manuscript from the desk. One look, and he saw it was arranged in alphabetical order. That was a help, and he rapidly turned the pages over until he came to the letter he was seeking. 

There were not many names under it, but one was familiar. His lips tightened into a straight line as he saw it, observed also the address and the figures typed after

the name. "Suicide with a gun, $40,000," was what he read.

Though the attorney was curious to discover why the list had been requested, he was wise enough to ask no questions. He had known the professor for some time, knew well that one could derive no information from him unless he was willing to give it. The set lips, the grave expression upon his face, showed it was no time to ask questions. They would not have been answered if asked.

He did, however, mildly suggest that the list had better not be taken from the office. The suggestion fell upon deaf ears. Manners retorted that it would be perfectly safe in his possession, and that in a day or two he would let him know why it had been required.

He left after that remark, refusing an invitation to lunch, on the score that he could not linger. On the main floor he stepped into a telephone booth and again called a number. The conversation was brief, and in a moment he was outside. Rather idly he noticed that there were seven telephone booths in the lobby-then walked out to his car. Before stepping in, he looked at his watch and saw there was time for what he wished to do.

He drove out into the country. When the city had been left behind, he began to increase his speed. It was an unfrequented road and there was little traffic. Crouched far down in his seat, his eyes glued upon the twisting road, Manners ran over in his mind the results of the morning. Nothing definite had been discovered, yet everything fitted in with the theory he had formed. He pictured Rogan's astonishment when he heard what was in his mind. It was going to be very difficult to convince the

chief-and he realized that he could not blame him for being doubtful.

No matter what facts might be in his possession when the day ended, no jury would ever convict on the evidence. It was not the usual type of evidence: physical facts, dates, witnesses who would testify that they saw the individual commit the crime. In a sense it was far more positive proof than was presented in the ordinary murder trial. His were psychological inferences, based upon the curious bypaths that a twisted mentality will take. And what he knew would not be enough for a jury.

They would have to trap the man-make him convict himself in such a manner that the proof would be overwhelming. And he knew how it might be done.

He stopped thinking, to watch the road. The time had come to turn off the smooth highway and run down a little rutted country lane. It curved and twisted toward the lake, a narrow road filled with deep ruts and covered with shifting, tricky sand. The country was desolate, partly marshy, the fields covered with small, stunted trees and rough high grass. The wind had commenced to blow, a cold wind, bearing the hint of rain. For over a mile the car slid along the sandy road, then came a little hill, and, upon cresting it, he discovered a country cemetery.

It was a small burial ground and in a terrible state of neglect. The iron fence which enclosed it was rusted with the years and in many places had fallen to the ground. 

Stopping the car, he climbed out and walked through the tall grass into the cemetery. The grass was almost waist high, and creeping vines had overgrown many of the gravestones. In the middle of the desolate acreage he

paused.

The cemetery lay on the very top of the hill. Below him, and not very far in the distance, was the lake, its surface white in the rising wind. Around him stood mosscovered stones, whose dim lettering had been eaten away by the ravages of the years. They were partly hidden by the rough grass and in some cases the stones had fallen to the ground. Tall wild grass and vines were everywhere, a tangled mass which would take days to clear away. 

There was but one exception. Down in the extreme corner, the one closest to the lake, was the only cleared spot. It was to this he directed his steps.

There were three gravestones here, and he noticed that one was new. Its whiteness stood out in great contrast to the other stones in the cemetery. And the lot presented another striking contrast. This was the only burial lot in the cemetery which had received any care. The grass had been cut, the tangle of vines cleared away. There were even flowers standing in a vase before the newest monument. Someone had taken the trouble to see that this particular lot should receive care.

Walking over to the white stone, he stood looking at the lettering on it. It bore the name of a man, and the date showed that the man had been dead only ten months. 

A familiar name, one he had often heard. For a moment he stood looking soberly down, then turned and glanced back. A shudder crept over him at what he saw-desolation, neglect and decay, a country cemetery on a windswept hill, bleak, lonely and, apparently, save for the graves he had been looking at, forgotten by the world. 

He was rather gloomy as he drove back to town. The

. 

cemetery had started a train of thought. Some of the oldest settlers in the state were buried there, men and women who had felt the urge of adventure, who had pushed into the new and unknown country, blazed the way for those who were to come after them. They had loved and hated, enjoyed their moments of triumph, endured seasons of defeat. Now they lay neglected, on a hill swept by the breezes of the lake, forgotten by all. Such, he thought, was the ultimate fate of everyone.

It was just noon when he entered the police station. 

Hurrying to Kent's office he found that worthy standing before his desk. Evidently he had just come in, for his hat was on his head. Without a word the inspector reached into an inside pocket and handed Manners a sheaf of papers. Instead of looking at them the professor spoke. "Kent, have the man on the switchboard put in a call for Stone. You'd better talk with him yourself. Tell him it's very important that he be here in a few moments." 

Turning, the inspector hastened through the door. 

Manners' eyes dropped to the notes. Written in Kent's sprawling hand, they covered five pages. The information was precisely what he required-though one item caused his eyes to open wide. So the man did know about archery! Years ago, it seemed, he had won a state archery contest. Well, so much the better. It removed one doubt from his mind. But it was the last sheet of paper which was the most interesting.

Three lines had been written upon it by a typewriter. 

The words made no sense-being simply random wordsand the last line was simply a long row of "e's" running across the entire page. His face grew grave as he looked

at the letter. Yes, there was no doubt of it. The same little imperfections he had noted in the two typewritten letters taken from Kimball's penthouse were here. The same machine, the same imperfections; and it told but one story. They had now identified the typewriter upon which the anonymous letters were written. 

Thrusting the notes into his pockets, he turned to hasten out into the hallway. Going down its length he threw open the door to Rogan's room and hurried in.

The chief was standing at the window engaged in no more arduous duty than looking out at the noonday crowd. His face lit up as he turned and saw who had come in. 

That he was glad to see his visitor was putting it mildly. 

Kent had rushed into the office early in the morning, hinting at incredible things which Manners had said. 

Just what these things might be was not, to the chief, very clear. But they seemed to bring a ray of cheer and of hope and hope was something of which Rogan was now very much in need. The morning paper had contained another editorial, this time more vicious than its predecessors. It was time the police chief should speak, should tell the city what he was doing-so went the attack upon him. 

But how could he speak when there was nothing he could say? 

All these thoughts Rogan might have voiced if the hallway door had not just at that moment opened. It was 

Stone who entered the room. Behind him came the inspector, his face flushed as if he had been hurrying. He called out to Manners: "I found the Commissioner coming up the steps of the building."

Stone's sharp glance swept over the two men by the desk. Rogan, he saw clearly, had no idea as to why Manners had brought them together. As for the professor, he was standing by the corner of the desk lighting a cigarette, and there was a calmness about his friend's face which caused Stone to draw a sudden deep breath. He had seen that look several times in the past-and only when Harley Manners was very sure that he had reached a solution. Pulling a chair close to the desk, the lawyer seated himself and waited for what might come. "I brought you together," said Manners, who seemed inclined to stand, "to say that I think our only solution to the Kimball murders lies in trapping the murderer into a confession."

The voice was so calm, the certainty behind it so dominant that Rogan cast one swift look at the grave face, then burst into an excited protest: "Anyone would think you knew who it was." 

The reply stunned him. "I think I do know, think also I can furnish the motive, as well as the manner in which the poison was given." 

Stone's sharp glance fastened upon Manners' face. 

Satisfied by what he saw, the lawyer took a cigar from his pocket and very deliberately lighted it. It was Rogan's rough, impatient voice which again broke the silence. "For God's sake, Harley," he urged, "tell us what you mean. Who is it you suspect and why did they do it, and-?" 

Lifting his hand, Manners stopped the excited flow of words. Very deliberate was his reply: "It would take too long, and do no good, to tell all

that I have in mind. I do not wish to name the individual now. It is not really necessary. Tim, here, will have to trust me. My theory appears to me to be entirely correct; all the little clues point only one way. But there are still things to be done. I simply want to remind you of something."

He then went at length into what he called a description of the mind of the murderer. The individual had committed what he no doubt considered to be a perfect crime. The method was one which appeared to present not the slightest chance of discovery. The motive was so obscure that it lay hidden deep within the vagaries of an unbalanced mind. Yet, every criminal, after his crime had been committed, was a prey to one disturbing anxiety. "Have I left a single clue behind?" was always the thought which pursued him. "Is there anything left undone which 

I should have done?" No matter how secure he might feel there was always that uneasy question: "Am I safe; did 

I leave a clue?" It was precisely this obsession of the criminal of which he now wanted them to take advantage. 

He told them of the penthouse and of the letters which he had found. Vividly, there followed the recital of a footstep on the roof; his discovery that the key had been taken. They were assured the unknown had taken the key because later he wished to return to the penthouse. 

Both Stone and Rogan had listened carefully throughout, though it was the lawyer who had the better conception of what was being implied. When Manners' voice died away, after giving the details of the anonymous letters and of the distinguishing marks made by the typewriter, there fell a silence. It was broken by Rogan.

"The man must be crazy!" he said. "Not in the accepted sense of the word, Tim. It might be easier for us if he were. We are dealing with a mind crazed by just one obsession, one which makes of him a cruel, cold murderer, stopping at nothing to satisfy the impulse which cries for revenge. You have, in most murders, well defined motives-love of money, of power, sex and revenge. It's the last we are dealing with here. An individual not technically insane, but very close to it."

He paused to shrug his shoulders. "One who may soon become so. And, though I am confident as to who the individual is, I realize we need far more proof than simply my theory. So that is why I offer you a plan." "What is it, Harley?" asked Stone. "Without a doubt the murderer has forgotten the letters he wrote Kimball. I suggest we send Kent around to see him. Kent will become very talkative, though extremely vague. He will say the police are all at sea, know nothing at all. Then he will add that Harley Manners suggests that Kimball's penthouse may contain a cluethrowing in a hint of the rumors about Kendall having received anonymous letters which he kept in the penthouse. Over this last idea Kent will grow eloquent. He will state that the police might get a clue from these letters-from the handwriting or the type. Then he will add that early tomorrow morning the authorities will search the penthouse." "And you think Kent's talk will send the man to the penthouse?" was Stone's question. "It should, if his mind is in the uneasy condition I think it is. The idea of going may not be a new idea

to him-that may explain the stealing of the key. But if the individual I have in mind comes to the penthouse tonight, if he searches it for six letters which will be there -and takes them away-you can be very sure my theory is correct. After that you can search his apartment, his place of business. Intercepted, he may even be thrown into such a rage that he will confess, or at least break into a raving torrent of speech."

Stone's head was thrown back in thought. "I wish I knew who Harley has in mind," was his thought. “But no doubt he has a good reason for not telling us. He has given us no details except the story of the letters. There must be a good many things we yet know nothing about. 

Manners is intelligent; he has never gone off on a tangent before." The commissioner stole another flashing glance at the calm, set face of his friend, only to decide that he had gone off on no tangent now. He had made up his mind to do as Manners wished, but he had one question to ask: "You do not think you have sufficient evidence with which to go before the grand jury?" 

The reply was emphatic. "No. In fact, the evidence I have is mostly psychological, a delicate tissue of facts which no grand jury would ever understand. And then, Stone, you must remember we are dealing with perhaps the most dangerous criminal the city ever had, a man of education, with a fine mind broken to bits by his obsession. It is very probable that he may go forth again and plan another series of deaths. 

No, I think my idea is best. You have no case for a jury yet." 

The lawyer rose to his feet.

“All right. I have an engagement. You and Tim make your plans. I suppose we will spend a good many hours in that penthouse tonight. Simply let me know the time 

I am to be with you and I will be there." With that he turned to hurry from the room.

Rogan was far from satisfied. There was more than one question he wished to ask. Some he attempted—but with no results. In the end, he agreed to what had been suggested. Deep down in his heart he knew he himself was willing to clutch at any theory. Only, he did wish Manners would tell him the name of the individual he had in mind. Secrecy he did not like. It was with an uneasy expression in his eyes that he watched the tall professor pass out through his door, taking Kent with him.

In the inspector's room Manners carefully laid down his instructions. Kent's eyes had opened wide when he was told where he was to go. He started to make a protest-only to have Manners shake his head. The directions had so startled the police official that for the time being he was beyond speech, but he listened carefully, and was told that the entire success of bringing the man to the penthouse, lay in his being able to sow the seeds of suspicion in his mind. When Manners at last left the room, and the door was closed, Kent sat for a long time staring across the desk. Some moments passed before he gave vent to a long, low whistle; a whistle far more eloquent than any words.

Manners spent a long, weary afternoon in the public library. The books examined were directories of social clubs, records of professional and technical societies, and a long series of rather dreary religious works. Once in a

while a note would go down on a piece of paper, though mostly he sat far down in a chair with his eyes glued to the printed page before him. Once there was a telephone call made to the harbor master, asking the registration of a boat, its speed and tonnage. Dusk was creeping into the room when with a sigh he thrust back the books and went out into the fresh air.

It was raining, not the gentle shower of a summer evening, but the beginning of a hard and boisterous storm.

The wind was sweeping in from the lake, blowing in wild, uneven gusts. Already the streets were gleaming wet, and the rain swept in showers of spray over the sidewalks.

He shuddered as he felt the sweep of the wind, and bent his head to struggle against it. It was bad enough here on the street. What it would be that evening, twenty-two stories up in the air with the wind blowing a gale, he did not care to think. It was going to be a horrible evening. 

That he knew.

CHAPTER XVI 

It was a little after ten o'clock when Manners drove his car into a parking place. Coming down to the city from his house, he had battled against a sixty-mile gale which hurled the rain in blinding sheets across his windshield. 

The streets were deserted, and the few cars he had passed were creeping along at a snail's pace. It was not only the worst storm of the summer, but the combination of high wind and blinding rain was something he had not seen in years. Nor was there any promise the storm would die down.

With his hat pulled low over his eyes, he started out of the parking place. As the wind swirled around him, he was glad he had not far to go. All arrangements had been made, and he was confident there would be no slip in their plans. Across the street he struggled, the rain dashing in showers of spray across his face. Turning into an alley, he found he must go very slowly. It was a dark lane between two buildings. On either side towered the high structures, whose tops were lost to sight in the darkness. Half way down the alley was a half opened door, and there he found Rogan and Stone.

The police chief was nervous and, after a growled greeting, volunteered that he had seen the superintendent of the building and that all necessary arrangements had been made. An elevator would take them to the top floor. 

Its operator had been sworn to secrecy. Detectives had 251

already surrounded the building, though Rogan would have felt far more at ease had he had some slight idea for whom his men were supposed to be on the lookout. 

With a grunt he suggested that they'd better go above, and he led the way into the building. 

The elevator shaft was at the end of the passage. The operator's face was a mixture of keen curiosity and awe. 

It was the police chief the young Negro persistently stared at a glance which Rogan returned with a menacing scowl. As the elevator started upward, the chief was far from at ease. If he but knew just what it was Manners had in mind! But he did not know. As they rode to the top of the building he stole a quick glance at his friend. There was a look of determination upon Manners' face, a calmness which encouraged Rogan's troubled heart. Manners at least was not disturbed; and this brought a little assurance.

The elevator came to a stop and the door was opened. 

As they stepped out into the little room, Rogan turned to the operator. "George," came his warning voice, "you remember no one is to know you brought us up here. One peep out of you and I send you up for life." 

The boy's eyes rolled until only the whites could be seen. Hastily they were assured he would say nothing, and the speed with which he closed the door and started downward would have been amusing under different circumstances. None of the three men smiled, however.

Stone was listening to the sound of the wind above his head, the sharp and wailing noise it made as it swept over the roof. Rogan was looking up at the short flight of

stairs, thinking that in a moment they would be ascending it. As for Manners, he was doing no thinking at all. 

He was, however, the one who led the way up the steps. 

He had to use all his strength to open the door at the top, for the wind pressed against it like an iron hand. 

As it was flung open, to be swept violently out of his grasp, the rain came dashing in a torrent of spray over his face. For a second he peered across the roof. Only a vast expanse of blackness met his eyes. It was several moments before he was able to distinguish the dim outline of the penthouse; a sinister, threatening outline he thought, as his eyes searched the darkness. 

To cross the roof proved a difficult task. Like a dragon with angry claws, the wind clutched and swirled about them. To stand upright was an impossibility. The rain was a raging torrent slapping against their faces with stinging force. Far away in the direction of the lake could be seen the lighthouse, its light a grotesque reflection in the sheet of rain. The edge of the roof could not be distinguished, and, because of the height, no adjacent buildings broke with their outline the black void around them. 

They had to battle against the wind before reaching the side of the penthouse. With his hand upon the wet slippery side of the small building, Manners felt his way along its length, until his fingers slipped at last over the wet glass of a window. It was only the work of a moment to slide it up and to climb inside. In a second Rogan and Stone had followed. Then the dense blackness was broken by the illumination of a flashlight. The shaft of flame was directed toward the floor, splitting the darkness like a straight line. Slowly it crept across the floor.

It was Manners who had directed the light. In his pocket were the six letters he had discovered the previous afternoon. They must be replaced in the box in which he had found them. This was accomplished and the box so arranged that no one would suspect it had been disturbed. 

With a word to the two men to follow, he led them through a door into the second room. For a moment the light played over its meager furnishings; then he crossed over to raise the window. "It will be rather stuffy in here, without any air," was all he said.

Rogan found a seat in a near-by chair. Stone took his place beside Manners upon the battered couch. Promptly, then, the light was extinguished and the room became dark. It was an intense darkness, so impenetrable that no one could see an inch before his eyes. And as the room became dark, it seemed also to shrink, to press in upon them in some sinister manner. They could feel the air rushing in through the open window-though the window itself they could not see-could hear the rain pattering upon the roof over their heads. 

No one spoke; nor did anyone feel like speaking.

Within the room was silence; outside was the angry wailing of the wind. It shrieked and howled around the building as if it would like to sweep it from its moorings. They could feel the penthouse tremble and shiver, as the angry gusts swept around it. Above their heads was the steady pounding of the rain upon the flat roof, a sound which never ceased. If they had wished to communicate with each other they would have been forced to shout in order to be heard above the noise of the storm.

On the couch, leaning back against the wall, Manners was conscious of but one wish-that he might smoke. He knew, however, that this could not be indulged in. Beside him was Stone, and, though unable to see the lawyer, he was reassured of his presence each time the couch creaked. Somewhere, across from them, sat Rogan, though the only hint of his presence came when he shifted his weight in his chair. It would groan and creak as if protesting against the presence of the chief.

Though he knew his friends thought him at ease, Manners was far from a state of calm. During the afternoon his plan of drawing the murderer to the penthouse had seemed very reasonable. Early that evening Kent had telephoned, saying that the suspect had listened with marked interest as he rambled on about the letters in the penthouse. But now in the sinister blackness, as he listened to the angry storm, heard the wind shrieking around the penthouse, he began to wonder if he had, after all, been very wise. Would anyone be foolish enough to fall into the trap he had laid? Would anyone even think of coming out on such a night? 

Minutes slipped away; an hour passed. Once Manners rose and groped his uneasy way across the dark floor until he found the window. But it was useless to look out. 

There were no stars, and over the city hung a heavy blanket of thick clouds. Because of the height of the building, he was unable to see the lights of the street, though far away in the distance was visible the steady flare of the lighthouse. Even the edge the roof was hidden from sight by the impenetrable darkness.

Groping his way back to the couch, he stumbled against

Stone as he reseated himself. By instinct a hand searched in a pocket for the cigarette case, felt its smooth surface and then was hastily withdrawn. Leaning against the wall he allowed his thoughts to wander as they would. The pictures which came were oddly assorted: Doctor Wright with his snow-white head bent over his flowers: Marjorie 

Kimball seated at his table, her dark head held proudly high: the dark rolling waters he had once seen off the coast of Labrador, and the snow glimpsed at the same moment on the hills of Newfoundland: and, at last, little cafés in Paris, and a snow-white mountain top in Switzerland. With the last, he stopped thinking.

In a chair, close to the door, sat Rogan, engrossed in turn with his own thoughts. He knew his friends were only a few feet away and, though he could not see them, the knowledge brought comfort. But once in a while an unusually loud gust of wind would cause the penthouse to tremble, and the sound always made the chief jump. An unlit stogie was thrust between his teeth (he would have given a good deal to be able to light it). Deep down in his heart he was wondering if they were not upon a fool's errand. Only the fact that Manners' judgment had so often proved correct in the past prevented him from putting his doubts into speech. 

There came at last the moment, for all three men, when time had lost all meaning. They seemed to be alone in space-space so dark and mysterious that only the sound of the wind and rain could be heard; space wherein time had become an unknown thing. And, because they could not see across the dark room, it now began to sway out into enormous dimensions. Nervous, anxious, the

three men shifted uneasily, wondering if their waiting was ever to end. And, just as the suspense became well-nigh unbearable, above the wind and the rain crept a new sound.

It was more a suggestion of a sound than any noise they could really distinguish. Each knew instinctively that the others were bending forward, straining every nerve to listen. Then as they listened, it came again, and this time it could not be mistaken. Floating in from the other room came the faint metallic sound of a key fumbling and scraping at a lock. It stole in to them between the howling of the wind, as if someone were trying anxiously to open the door of the penthouse.

The door leading into the other room had been almost closed. As he heard the sound of the key, Manners rose from the couch to creep softly over the floor. Standing a little to one side, he looked into the other room, saw only a light stealing slowly over the floor, resting for a second upon an object, only to creep on to another. Then, at last, he saw the man.

His back was toward them, and Manners could not see the face. He was a tall man, dressed in black, holding in his hand a flashlight. Manners saw the long nervous fingers sweep the light over every object in the room. 

And one thing was very certain. The man had no suspicion that he was not alone in the penthouse; he was perfectly at ease.

The connecting door was almost closed, and from where he stood Manners found it impossible to take in all of the other room. The man moved, from time to time, out of his line of vision, and he could then but guess what

he was doing. There came the sound of books being flung on the floor, the rustle of turning pages, followed by a muffled oath. Book after book must have been examined. 

And then Manners noticed he could see just the edge of the table, could glimpse the box below, the box in which lay the anonymous letters.

But the box did not attract the searcher's attention for some time. As if disgusted by the scrapbooks, the man finally rose and walked around the room. Manners could see, occasionally, the man's back, observe the tall figure, glimpse the long fingers clasped around the flashlight. The face he was unable to see. Even when the light swept under the table, and the tall man paused and bent down, his face remained hidden. A long arm reached down and picked up the box. Sinking down in a chair, the man poured the contents of the box upon the table. 

Manners could see only a portion of the table. On it lay now a cluttered heap of letters. All sorts of letters, as he well knew-the sarcastic, threatening screeds which Kimball had received. He watched the long fingers reach forth, witnessed letter after letter as it was picked up, glanced at, then flung down upon the floor. The process seemed endless, and he began to wonder if the typewritten letters would ever be found. Then, just as he thought they had been overlooked, he saw the long fingers reach forth and clutch eagerly at a sheet of paper. 

With his eyes glued to the opening, Manners peered on into the other room. Behind him, their breath upon his neck, were Stone and Rogan. He did not know what his companions were thinking; for himself there was only one thought. His plan had been successful. The murderer

had crept up to the penthouse and he was recovering his letters. If the man would only turn around! But one thing was needed that Rogan and Stone should see the face of the individual in the other room. Unless they did so, his plan was a failure. 

Would the man ever turn around? He watched the letters being picked up, saw the head bend low over the table. 

A motion of an arm showed that the letters were going into a coat pocket. There came the sound of the chair being pushed back, and he saw the tall figure rise slowly to his feet. At the sight Manners knew suddenly what he must do. His hand swept back. Whose arm he touched he did not know, but he felt a responsive head brush against his shoulder, and, at the touch, he whispered: "Don't come in until it's necessary."

As he bent forward again to look into the other room, he still hoped the man would turn, but he soon saw that this wish was hopeless. The tall black-clothed figure was walking across the floor toward the door. Ahead of him the flashlight cut a bright path in the darkness. At the sight, Manners threw back his shoulders, pulled open the door, and stepped into the other room.

But two steps was he able to take before the figure ahead of him paused. Then the man stopped, suddenly whirled. One hand clutched the flashlight, the other swept with a threatening gesture toward a coat pocket. With his head held high, his long face flashing with rage, the man lunged forward, and, as he did so, instinctively Manners moved backward. Before him, his face twisted with angry passion, his dark eyes gleaming with rage, was the dentist, Doctor Langfelt. The man was speaking, his voice

threatening in its icy coldness: "So-so it's the meddling professor! The professor who could not take a warning. The professor who enjoys interfering in other people's business. And he has done it once too often!"

Manners took one step toward Langfelt. As he did so the doctor's hand swept to his coat pocket and came forth with an ugly looking revolver. The man's eyes had narrowed and were now tiny slits of flame, and behind the burning gaze was a hint of madness. For a moment he glared savagely across the room, then, with a gesture of his hand, he motioned to the chair behind the table. "Sit there," came the cold even voice. "And listen to what I am to say. I want you to hear it." The voice paused, then became icy as it added: "before you die."

Manners took three steps and dropped down into the chair. There was nothing else to do, and the menace in the cold, icy voice told him that resistance would be useless. Langfelt's long face was twitching with rage; his eyes had become fiery pools of light. Standing on the other side of the table, the revolver clasped in his hand, he appeared like an avenging angel of doom. For a moment his sharp eyes continued to blaze into the professor's; then came his low voice. "You must die-and in a sense I am sorry. But you know too much. You should not have bothered with the 

Kimballs." The voice was rising as he spoke, trembling with wild emotion. "They were evil people-cursedcursed." The voice sank to a whisper. "Cursed by God. 

And I-I-was God's agent."

The last words were delivered in a wild, triumphant

shriek. The tone rose shrilling above the howling of the wind, the bombardment of the rain. Behind the wild outcry was conviction and certainty. As the words died away, the man leaned far across the table. "There was blood on the hands of the Kimballsblood. The blood of hundreds of persons. These cried out to be revenged. God heard them and I was the humble instrument in his hands."

Manners' eyes had never left the hand holding the revolver. It was a steady hand, not wavering for a second. 

He knew the man across the table had worked himself into an insane rage, knew also that his own life was held by a thread. And then, as he watched, there came a slight sound from the room behind him. Either Rogan or 

Stone had moved. At the sound, the dentist's eyes left his own to dart across the room. As they did, Manners acted.

With a sudden shove he sent the table flying across the room, and flung himself to the floor. The overturned table struck Langfelt's legs and sent him half sprawling to the floor. As he fell, Manners leaped to his feet and darted toward him. The dentist's hand slashed upward, only to be knocked aside. The revolver was sent flying from the grasping fingers, to fall against the wall with a loud crash. As it fell, the doctor's fist struck out, hitting

Manners upon the shoulder and turning him completely around. Instantly Langfelt dashed for the door.

Rogan and Stone were now hurrying into the room, but it was Manners who was first to rush out into the wind and rain. He knew Rogan had said his men would be watching for anyone who might try to descend from the

roof, but there might be a slip. As he plunged into the darkness, felt the rain come stinging over his face, he tried to get his bearings. The door leading to the building must be directly opposite him. With this for an objective, he started on a run across the roof. 

He was unable to see more than a foot before him. Rain swept his face; wind clutched wildly at his clothing.
