He knew that somewhere on that roof was Langfelt, Langfelt crazed with rage, who was endeavoring to escape. Struggling through the darkness, Manners crept on over the roof. Suddenly his hand fell against a smooth surface. As it did so someone stumbled roughly against him, clutched fiercely at his shoulder, then all at once tried to throw him to the roof. 

With a burst of strength, Manners threw off the clutching hand, lashing forth with his fist. He felt it strike, heard a grunt of pain, then two strong arms came hurling around him. He vainly tried to throw them aside. Then he thought of a trick of his college days. Suddenly he let himself fall, and, at the same moment, slashing out with his hands, clasped his opponent's knees. A shove, a quick jerk, and the man came crashing to the roof.

But it was not finished. Rolling over and over they went across the pebbled covering, to come up with a bang against the stone coping which enclosed the roof. Manners had tried once more to throw off the encircling arms, but the effort was vain. Like cords of steel they clutched his body, and again and again a hand had tried to creep upward and grip his throat. Only with the most tremendous effort was he able to throw aside the creeping hand. And when he did so, it only started all over again

its effort to reach his throat.

A light suddenly pierced the darkness. He knew what it was, the flashlight held in Rogan's hand. Its bright flame brought reassurance. Aid was at hand. With a sudden upright thrust, he managed to pull himself to his feet and shake off the encircling hands. Then he staggered backward. Directly behind him was the slight coping which ran around the roof. He felt the pressure of the stones against his legs, stones wet and cold. Across his face slashed the rain, stinging like the slap of a cold hand.

The doctor had struggled upward. His black coat was thrown in relief as the light swept over it, but his face remained in darkness. His body was swaying back and forth, and from his lips fell little rasping cries. Manners saw the long hands reach forth, noticed for a second the clutching fingers, fingers like the threatening claws of a bird. Then, with a leap, the doctor sprang forward. As he did so Manners jumped to one side.

The flashlight was now steadily playing over the plunging figure. Under its flame they saw him go stumbling against the low coping; saw the black figure sway back and forth in an effort to keep its balance. For a moment they thought he would fall to the roof. But they were mistaken. Instead, he went plunging against the coping, and there for a moment swayed back and forth as he tried vainly to catch his balance. Then suddenly, before their eyes, he fell forward into the darkness, vanishing from their sight. Above the shriek of the wind came a loud despairing cry. Then it died away. Only the sound of the wind and rain remained.

CHAPTER XVII 

ALL the lights were on in the living room of Stone's apartment. The three men had just entered. Tired and weary, Rogan and Manners had thrown themselves down in chairs, and watched their host vanish from their sight. 

They did not speak to each other, and the only sound was the howling of the wind and the beating of the rain against the windows. It caused Rogan to lift his head slowly and glance across the room. His tired eyes rested upon the large windows, observed the rain trickling down the panes. He shuddered. Once more he heard the wind as it swept around the penthouse, saw again the dark figure which had gone shrieking to oblivion. 

He stole a glance at Manners. His friend was far from a pretty object. The once carefully pressed gray suit was stained and torn. One sleeve was hanging by a few threads, and no laundry would ever find it possible to remove the dirt and grime from the gray fabric. Across Manners' soiled cheeks ran a series of red lines, at the sight of which an irreverent thought swept over the chief's mind. 

The professor would find it rather difficult to make people believe that no one had scratched him.

Stone came hurrying through the door. In his hand was a large tray, containing three glasses and two bottles. He paused beside Rogan, watched him pour a generous portion from the squatty bottle, smiled as with an after thought the chief again tilted the bottle. Generous also 264

was the portion poured into Manners' glass, and Stone served himself likewise. In silence the drinks were sipped.

They had come to Stone's apartment both because it was only a short block from the insurance building and because for a few moments they wished to be alone. 

As he reached out to the table, to pick up the bottle and pour another inch of liquid into his glass, Rogan was thinking hard. In a short while he would have to meet the reporters. They would ask him many questions, demand to know precisely how he had been able to discover the murderer. The doctor's name would rouse the city to frenzy, for he was very well known. As he stole a glance across the room at Manners, Rogan realized that there were many questions he was yet unable to answer. He turned to Stone. "Henry," came his dry voice, "I think it's the way

Harley always ends his cases that gets on my nerves. 

God knows I will never be able to forget that wild, despairing cry which escaped from Langfelt's lips when he plunged off that building." He shuddered again at the mere thought and hastily drained his glass. "But, like myself, Stone, you must be wondering how under heaven 

Manners ever came to suspect the dentist."

The police commissioner had been slowly filling his pipe. The operation was suspended for a second as he emphatically nodded his agreement. The Kimball murderer was dead, and, though the broken, hysterical speech which had fallen from the man's lips in the penthouse had amounted to a confession, there were many things 

Stone still wished to know. He said as much. "I suppose you want me to tell you, at once, what it was

made me first suspect Langfelt," was Manners' reply. “In a sense I never suspected him at all, or suspected anyone for that matter, until one short sentence fell from the dentist's lips and made me suspicious. In fact it was that sentence that convinced me no one else could have killed George and Edward Kimball. And, in reality, it was a very innocent remark."

He went on to tell them how when he had gone to Kimball's office to see his engagement book, there had been small hope that the results would be of value. The visit had been made rather as a mere matter of routine, an endeavor to discover what had occupied George Kimball's time during the ten days or more before his death.

In the engagement book, however, there was one item which stood out because of its repetition. It was the daily visit to the dentist. More because the man was in the building than for any other reason, Manners had stopped at the doctor's office, simply to inquire whether the dentist had noted any particular nervousness or evidence of anxiety in his patient. They were reminded that Kimball was an incessant talker, and it had seemed reasonable to suppose that while in the dentist's chair he might have talked a good deal. "And," said Manners in conclusion, "one thoughtless remark of Langfelt's, in reply to my question, gave the whole thing away.' 

"What did he say?" was Rogan's impatient inquiry. "Assuring me that Kimball had done no talking, he reminded me that his patient had been undergoing the extraction of a nerve in one of his teeth. The operation 

I was told, was painful, and took some time. Last night,

when I thought over that remark of Langfelt's, it suddenly flashed over me that I knew how George and Edward Kimball had both been poisoned."

An understanding look flashed across the face of the commissioner. Rogan, however, shook his head. Manners must know what he was talking about, but the words meant nothing to him. Impatiently he demanded more information. "Why, it's just this, Rogan. Langfelt was the best and without a doubt, the most up to date scientific dentist in the city. If there is one thing that is not done today by the better dentists it is the removal of nerves from teeth and the filling of root canals. All the best authorities are against this procedure, their experience being that a tooth whose nerve has been extracted, that is a dead tooth, is a dangerous thing, a means of focal infection. And yet

Langfelt, the leading dentist in the city, told me that for five days he had worked upon George Kimball's teeth, doing that very thing. It caused me to put a question to myself." "What question?" "Why did he do it? It was against all his scientific training, against his own and his colleagues' experience. I discovered this afternoon that he once read a paper before the state dental society in which he advised that under no circumstances should a nerve be extracted and the dead tooth left in the mouth. Yet here was the best dentist in the city doing the very thing he had warned against.

And he did it again in the case of Edward. It was then that I realized I knew how the poison had been given." 

He paused to light a cigarette. The operation was

watched by Rogan, who wished his friend would keep on talking. In a second this wish was gratified. "You see," went on Manners, "at the very beginning of these murders it appeared to me very probable that we must seek some individual who not only was well educated but presumably had received technical scientific training. Ricin was no ordinary poison; the average individual either had never heard of it or would have no opportunity to secure it. We knew, furthermore, of only two ways by which it could have been introduced into the body. First it might have been given as a hypodermic. 

If we assumed that that was the method, we at the same time broadened the group among whom we had to look for a suspect. Anyone can use a syringe. But Hoff and

Bell were unable to discover any marks upon the two bodies, or even a suspicion of a mark. So—that left only one other method." "And that?" "Through the body tissues. In this case, through the deposit of ricin in the root canal of the tooth from which 

Langfelt had removed the nerve."

Rogan's tongue made a hasty exploration of his mouth. 

Most of his teeth were sound, and for that he was now thankful. If what Manners said was true, he doubted if he would ever again be able to sit in a dentist's chair. 

But could it be true? There came his protesting voiceand Manners' immediate rejoinder: "There seems no other way in which the poison could have been given, Tim. Remember, there were no suspects in this case, no marks upon the two bodies. When I heard 

Langfelt's remark about extracting a nerve, I realized

that it was a very odd bit of technic for him to be engaged in. I realized something else; that when he filled that root canal, after the nerve had been removed, all he had to do was place in the opening as little as one two thousandth of a grain of the poison, seal up the canal, and let his victim walk out of the office. The poison would seep through the root into the tissues and, in a safe interval, say from two to four days, would kill the victim.

Not only would it kill, it would almost entirely assure the absolute safety of the poisoner. That is unless he made some mistake, and we must be thankful murderers always do, even the shrewdest of them." 

Continuing, he reminded his listeners that, if there had been no telephone calls to Rogan, it was extremely doubtful that anyone would even have thought of foul play.

The phone calls had done one thing. When, at the end of ten days, something did happen—the death of the city's richest man-Rogan had become uneasy. In the chief's mind the two things were connected. The result was the autopsy. Furthermore the calls made to George Kimball and the one made the night he died to his son Edward, had brought disaster. "How?" demanded Stone. "I can see Langfelt made a mistake in calling Rogan. In fact it's difficult to understand his foolhardy gloating over the telephone. But I cannot quite see how the call to Edward Kimball brought disaster." "Let me answer the last question first," was Manners' quick reply. "The telephone call Edward received the night his father died brought him down to the police station. And it was the information that he was going to

confide in the chief that caused the quarrel between the two brothers. Remember, Edward had also been suffering from an infected tooth. Recall that he went out of Rogan's office and got directly in touch with his dentist, and had it treated. The nerve was removed that night, and of course the operation sealed his death. Without a doubt he must have confided in his dentist, told of his visit to the police station. How else could Langfelt have known? 

And he did know. Late that evening he called Henry, you recall, and it's my idea that Henry recognized his voice.

This explains why Henry Kimball was willing to go over that dark lawn in response to the call. Remember also that Langfelt for years had done all the Kimballs' dental work."

Stone protested a little, though it seemed reasonable. 

But he asked several questions. Why should the dentist become frightened by the news that Edward had visited the police station? And above all why should he have killed Henry Kimball? The retort was instantaneous. "All the men in the Kimball family were marked for death. It was only a question of time when Henry, in his turn, would sit in that fatal chair. The dentist's office is a place we all visit sooner or later. But I have some doubt as to whether Langfelt originally intended to kill them all as quickly as he did. Edward's visit to the chief forced his hand. He was shrewd enough to realize that three deaths in a row would look very suspicious. But he did make one great mistake. He gloated over his victims on the telephone." Manners turned to Rogan. "I think you will discover that each of the calls you received in your office came from the booths on the first floor of the Alpha

building. You remember that they all came into your office shortly after five o'clock. That is just the time the dentist would be leaving his office. But let me tell the story in more connected fashion." 

They were reminded that, though the dentist's remark about blocking the root canal had revealed to Manners how the poison might have been given-yet he still had no real evidence against the dentist. In fact all reason seemed to argue that even to suspect the man was absurd. 

He was well known in the city, his professional standing of the highest. Besides, there seemed to be no motive that could be attributed to him, for murder. True, he appeared to be the only individual who possessed the opportunity to give the poison, just as he seemed to fit the type of individual who would be likely to know about ricin. That only a technical or professional man could have known anything about the poison had been obvious from the beginning. And the motive had been found in the letters Manners had discovered in Kimball's penthouse. They had all been written upon Langfelt's typewriter. The imperfections in the letter "e" were clearly marked in the letters themselves, as in the copied lines procured by Kent upon the doctor's machine that afternoon. "Motive," burst forth Rogan's astonished voice. "I don't see any motive. And all those letters did was to accuse 

Kimball of being responsible for the deaths of hundreds of people. And that of course was all damned foolishness." "Not from the point of view of the man who wrote the letters," was the grave reply. "From his point of view,

what he said in the letters was simply the truth.” "How could it be?"

Again they were reminded of what the letters had said. "Hundreds have lost their lives because of you,” the first four had been worded; but the last two had said: "Because of you hundreds have taken their lives." Here was a different slant to the charge. Manners admitted that at first he had discounted the statements as the usual ravings one finds in anonymous letters. Then he began to follow out their significance. Kimball was the founder and head of a great insurance company. During the past two years hundreds of men had committed suicide so that their families might be able to live upon their policies. A warped, disturbed mind might conceivably brood over the suicides to a point where it came to feel that Kimball himselfas the head of a powerful insurance company-was directly responsible. There could be, it seemed to him, no other interpretation placed upon the letters. And then 

Manners had discovered a series of facts. "You see," he said, "all along I had the feeling that behind the crime was an educated mind which had been thrown off its emotional balance. In a sense we had no clues-nothing to go on-yet, whenever we did discover an isolated fact, it led back always to one type of a man. 

I now discovered that Langfelt was a Mennonite." 

Rogan threw a disgusted look across the room. "What's that word you just said? And what in the devil is it anyway?" "Mennonite. They are a small religious sect which we have in this state. They do not believe in taking oaths, in theological education (or for that matter much educa-

tion of any sort), and they are violently opposed to insurance. They do not allow their members to carry life insurance, claiming that it is gambling with one's own life. 

And Langfelt had been born into that sect." "Wait a minute, wait a minute," interrupted the commissioner. "There seems to be a contradiction in what you say. I see of course what you mean. But how could

Langfelt have been a Mennonite, and at the same time be a leading dentist and all that?" 

No contradiction existed, came Manners' denial. He then began to recount to them the series of facts he had discovered facts of which they knew nothing. He described what had been discovered of the dentist's early life, told how he and his brother-a brother ten years older than the dentist-had broken away from the little sect in which they had been born. It had been the brother who had paid for the dentist's education, put him through his course. During the period in which he was securing his education and the longer period when he was becoming the leader of his profession in the city, Langfelt had of course swung far away from the rigid beliefs of his childhood. But always he was close to his brother, held by a friendship and a bond of affection which seemed to preclude women from playing any part in the dentist's life. 

The man had never married and they were reminded that he had never been seen with any woman. "None of us," was Manners' summary, "no matter what we may do or what we become in later life, ever get completely away from the influences of our childhood. We may forget them, think they are out of our life, but, subconsciously, we either swing far out in the opposite

direction, or we become a mass of repressions and complexes. At the best, the doctor was an odd stick, shy, introverted, repressed, without friends or the desire to seek them. He lived for but two things, his profession, and the brother whom he worshiped. And, less than a year ago, this brother committed suicide. Without a doubt there was but one reason for the act-that his wife might be supported through the forty-thousand-dollar insurance policy he carried in Kimball's Alpha Life. There you have the motive for the murder." 

Stone nodded gravely. He saw what Manners was driving at. It was excellent reasoning, though, of course, very far from legal evidence. Even the shrill, hysterical words they had heard on the roof of the building were not, unfortunately, conclusive evidence. And, though Stone wished he could forget the picture of the dentist as he went whirling into space, yet in a sense he was glad it had all ended so. It was far better than a long trial, far better than dragging the tragic story day after day through the court and the newspapers. But Manners was speaking again. The commissioner stopped to listen. "Let me put it another way. The training of his early days must always have tugged at his heart. Two years ago he began to give some support and aid to the little Mennonite church which had been established in the city. 

It was the faith of his childhood, a faith opposed to most of the things which were a part of his daily life. There is evidence of a serious mental conflict here, one he only partially solved by going back to the religious beliefs he had been taught as a child. No doubt it was far more of a mental conflict than we are able to conceive. It must

have been; and suddenly, in its midst, comes the news that his brother, the one thing in his life which meant the most to him, had died, had committed suicide in order that his wife might realize on his insurance policy. That was the beginning of the end. "I suppose," he finished slowly, "after that there persisted but one thought in Langfelt's mind. Day after day, hour after hour, he dwelt upon his brother's death-death by his own hand. Sometimes he must have remembered the teachings of his early days. Insurance was an evil.

It was gambling with human life. The religious leaders had been right. If his brother had not been insured-why, he would still be alive-there would be no incentive for the taking of his life. And then, at last, I am certain there came a time when Langfelt began to think that God had punished him for turning away from his early faith. Day by day this obsession would grow, as the man's mind became a morass of hatred and broodings. It was inevitable that his hatred turned toward the insurance company. 

He would read in the paper of other suicides, of others who, like his brother, had killed themselves in order that their families might benefit by their policies. And recall that every day, or almost every day, he must have seen the Kimballs. In the end there came the day when he could see them only as an evil, came also the moment when he decided he would be but doing the will of God in removing them. When he reached that decision, their doom was sealed."

Sharply, as Manners concluded, there came the ringing of the telephone. Stone rose and vanished. Leaning back in the chair, Manners looked over the room. There were

books everywhere, and, because there were so many bookcases, hardly any other furniture found room. A few chairs, a table, and that was all. Stone's head appeared in the door motioning to Rogan. With a sigh, the chief rose and went into the other room. The heavy rumble of his voice could be plainly heard. When he returned, it was to sink down in his chair, after throwing a curious look at Manners. The glance was a mixture of admiration and wonder. His hand searched the inside pocket of his coat, found a stogie and lighted it. With a motion of his hand toward the room he had just left, he spoke. "That was a call from Kent," he said. "They have completed the search of the doctor's office. Bell reports they found ricin. The officers who went through his apartment found a bow and some steel arrows, and, think of it, a diary. The diary tells all about the murders. The man must have been crazy," he burst out. "No," said Manners with a shake of his head. "Not crazy as you use the word. There is a very fine distinction, a narrow margin indeed, between technical insanity and the morbid, unbalanced emotional condition typified in Langfelt. His mind was a tangle of hatred, and, in the end, I think, exaltation. When he decided that the Kimballs were because of their insurance company-an evil, he had whipped himself to the point where he believed he was the agent of the will of God. A good many persons reach that conclusion, though, luckily for society, most of them do not commit crime. But they are all emotionally unbalanced. Langfelt wished revenge; he wished to drive away the unrest within his own soul. He believed that his brother's death was a punishment for his

own turning away from the religious belief of their childhood days. And so he planned what was well-nigh a perfect crime." "It's all over," thought Stone. The call from Kent, adding the discovery in the office of the poison, the finding of the bow and arrows in his apartment and the diary- Well, no more proof was needed. The city would be convinced. As he thought this, he sighed. The average person would never be able to quite understand why it was the well-known dentist had developed into a spectacular murderer. But meanwhile there were other questions to be asked. Putting them to Manners he received a prompt reply.

Manners asked them to remember that at the beginning of the case they had been confused by two sets of conflicting facts. The Kimballs were not loved in the city and there were hundreds from its population who might have desired their deaths. The discovery of the poison had, to be sure, limited the murderer to someone with a good education and intelligence. But they were still utterly in the dark as to who it could be. The killing of Henry with an arrow complicated the matter. So far as they could discover, no one in the city had ever used steel arrows, although the fact of their use once more pointed to an intellectual individual. There were, in addition, the marks 

Manners had discovered on the sand the night of Henry's death, the triangle formed by the prow of the boat. But whose boat? There were hundreds of boats owned upon the lake. No, physical clues were totally absent, and the family angle would not hold. Had it not been for the slip of speech, some time might have elapsed, perhaps eternity,

before they ever discovered how the poison had been administered. "And, incidentally," he admitted, "I discovered only today that years ago Langfelt won an archery contest. 

Found also he had inquired at a sporting-goods store regarding steel arrows. I learned that he had a boat, and 

I have no doubt you will find, from the photograph of those marks upon the sand, that the prow fits them. 

Everything today pointed directly at Langfelt; and, though it would have been difficult ever to have convicted him, he convicted himself. It was my hope he might." 

Rogan's eyes had been examining the bottle upon the table. It was a wet, stormy morning and he soon would have to go out in the rain. He remembered that he was an old man and ought to take care of his health. With this as an excuse, he rose to walk over to the table. Slowly pouring himself a drink into the glass, he was suddenly struck by a thought and he whirled around. "And you, of course," he said to Manners, "believe he was the one who tried to shoot you. Why did he do that?" 

Manners shrugged his shoulders. "There is no doubt he was the one. The reason? His well planned crime was breaking down. He had based his actions on the certainty that the deaths would be considered to be from natural causes. Unlike most poisons, ricin only hits in during the last few hours of life. But now his criminal design had broken down. His telephone calls had been a mistake. The police were active. I have an idea he remembered that poison case we had last year, decided, if you will allow me to say so, Tim, that I was

more to be feared than the police. So he tried to get me out of the way—and came close to succeeding." 

Rogan let the last remark pass. But he commented: "He certainly thought out a new way to kill. God knows 

I am going to be afraid to go to a dentist after this." "It was not a new method," came the answer. "The poison used was a new one in crime, but the method had been used once before. Not many years ago, in South 

America, a dentist went on a rampage, killing a number of prominent people before it was discovered what was in progress. He also thought he was God's agent. It was the same method, injection of a poison in the nerve canal of a tooth, though the poison was not the same." 

Rogan gave a shudder. Hastily he drained his glass, then glanced across the room. What he saw caused him to feel depressed. The rain was beating a steady bombardment against the window. Little streams of water trickled down the glass. The wind was like a lost soul, wailing out a sinister warning. If possible the storm was even worse than earlier in the evening. And he must go out in that storm, battle his way to the station where the reporters were waiting.

Turning he gave a little sigh. It would be a long time before he would be able to forget the sight of that stumbling figure outlined against the sky-even longer before the wild, shrill, despairing cry would vanish from his memory. His years rushed over him. For the first time he thought it might be a welcome thing to be out of police work. But one fact was not to be disputed. His friend, sitting across the room, whose gray suit was stained and soiled, had made it possible for him to retire with honor.

And so he spoke and the softness of his tone reflected what lay in his heart. "I am glad it is completed, Harley."

So gentle was the tone that Manners was moved to speak two words. "What, Tim?" 

There came a wide gesture of the chief's hands: "Glad the circle of death is over."

THE END
